#2988. Federal Reserve Board requests public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 2:30 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday requested public comment on two proposals related to establishing a regulatory framework for Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers under the GENIUS Act.

The first proposal would require that Board-supervised payment stablecoin issuers fully back their stablecoins with certain permissible reserve assets, such as short-term Treasury bills and certain other high-quality, liquid assets. The proposal would also establish standardized capital requirements to address certain credit and operational risks of payment stablecoin activities, as well as risk management standards, in accordance with the law. Separately, the proposal would introduce rules for Board-supervised firms that safekeep the assets backing payment stablecoins. It would also clarify the permissibility of stablecoin and related activities for Board-supervised banks.

The second proposal would establish a tailored application process for Board-supervised banks applying to issue payment stablecoins. These applicants would be required to submit a business plan and financial information, among other documents. The proposal would also create a process governing appeals, hearings, and final determinations for applications.

The comment period will close 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call (202) 452-2955.

#2987. The Austin Powers steamroller: How to trade CoreWeave's slow moving crisis

In Austin Powers: International Man of Mystery, a Virtuocon guard famously stands in front of a commandeered steamroller, screaming "No!!" three times before being run over. The joke, of course, is that the hazard moved so slowly he had ample time to step out of the way.

For months, equity traders holding CoreWeave (CRWV) have played that guard. Despite credit markets flashing warnings, equity holders essentially just stood there. Now, after a shocking 27.5% drop from last Friday's close to this Thursday morning's 52-week lows, followed by a whiplash 17% rebound, equity investors, if not squashed like Dr. Evil's henchman, are dazed and confused. Shares are down another 5% today.

With earnings just a few weeks away, massive leverage, a heavily split sell-side, and 15.4% short interest, the stock remains primed for explosive volatility.

The hazard approaching CRWV equity wasn't hidden. The AI cloud infrastructure platform essentially didn't exist five years ago and has accumulated $51.6 billion in debt (~$46 billion net of cash) against a backdrop of zero historical profitability. That was well known, but the CEO's comments that the company aspired to be investment grade so it could borrow "unlimited sums", even as investors already anticipated nearly another $60 billion in debt in the next 18months, spooked folks. (Trick or Treat!) Especially since the debt issued only in June had already fallen 20% since.

From an options trader's point of view, it was hard to reconcile deteriorating credit and rising leverage with falling options premia; as recently as last week, they were in the 6th percentile, 1.5 standard deviations below the one-year mean. At 72%, options weren't cheap in absolute terms, but given everything going on, they should have been higher. The stock also looked somewhat complacent, with the equity chopping around historical support levels dating back to May 2025. The credit side was on tilt:

Because CRWV is levered by more than 2:1, a 10% move in enterprise value translates to a nearly 22% swing in equity value.

Despite the dire credit picture, equity sentiment remains fiercely divided, driving this week's 17% bounce from the lows. For example, HSBC maintains a Sell rating with a $39 price target, essentially calling for the stock to be cut in half. Conversely, banks like BNP and Cantor Fitzgerald (my former employer) suggest these fears are overblown and the stock should more than double from here.

It's not just bankers defending the company either. Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang downplayed systemic concerns, and ARK Investment stepped in to buy 192,000 shares. BofA argues that CRWV's ability to bring active power online at scale, combined with higher pricing from Nvidia's upcoming Vera Rubin deployments, will drive faster-than-expected revenue growth. They described execution risk as a bigger concern than financing; I'm not sure those who bought the 2032 8 1/2s at par would agree.

With CRWV's credit heavily distressed but its equity heavily defended by high-profile dip-buyers, going outright long or short into earnings carries excessive directional risk. A November/January Call Diagonal, currently trading for just under $6 per spread. Almost 9% of the current stock price. Normally, that's a lot, but given this stock has fallen 27.5% and then gained 17% in the past 5 sessions, it isn't.

#2986. Kellen Moore confirms Charlie Smyth will kick for the Saints this week

The Saints released veteran kicker Daniel Carlson on Tuesday. Coach Kellen Moore confirmed Wednesday that Charlie Smyth will kick for them this week.

"Yeah, Charlie will kick this week," Moore said, via video from the team. "Tough decisions. Daniel, he's made some really good kicks for us. I think just the consistency thing. Just felt like this was the best thing for our football team moving forward. These things are never easy, but Charlie's done this for us back in the last year, so he'll be ready to go."

In his first season with New Orleans, Carlson made just 8-of-12 field goals in seven games. All four misses came on attempts of at least 50 yards. He missed a 52-yard attempt during the fourth quarter of the Saints' Week 7 loss to the Steelers in Paris.

Smyth, a native of Northern Ireland, went 12-of-16 on field goals in six games for the Saints in 2025.

#2985. Next step? Fischer says Nets hope for big return for vets

The Nets rebuild can probably be traced back to June 25, 2024 when Brooklyn made two trades within minutes of each other that signals half steps were over. One sent Mikal Bridges to New York for five first rounders, a first round swap and a second rounder; the other an exchange of firsts with the Rockets that brought back a first rounder and a first round swap they had originally sent Houston in the first James Harden trade.

The second step was the two-year-long tank that helped the Nets get two high — although not as high as they had hoped — firsts in Egor Demin and Mikel Brown Jr. as well as five other late firsts. The hope, at least among fans, was that the losing or at least the deliberate losing would be over. Not yet, apparently, as Brooklyn is off to a 1-6 start and the two rookies have shared the court for only a few minutes. For the second straight year, the Nets are also the youngest team in the league, not a recipe for contention.

Now, into the fan caldron of disappointment, Jake Fischer seemingly dropped some knowledge that would seemingly ensure a longer rebuild. Speaking to Yahoo! Sports, the writer suggested that the next step could be trying to move veterans Michael Porter Jr and/or Julius Randle presumably at the February trade deadline, presumably to a contender, for more assets, presumably more picks or maybe other assets. Scoring, already an issue, and wins would be sacrificed.

"Brooklyn, I would still categorize as a team that is in more of a sell situation. I believe that Brooklyn will hope that a Julius Randle, a Michael Porter Jr. could still potentially draw some real returns," Fischer said.

"The Nets, they don't have their draft pick this year and they're looking to be better and compete in the Eastern Conference, take some strides forward in the East.

"I don't think they're going to be a full sell job right now, but I do think that they are still in a position to solicit trade offers for their veterans to get draft capital, potentially young pieces back in return."

Reading carefully, rather than scanning aggregators' headlines, reveals that Fischer isn't making predictions that the Nets will push hard to move both. He talks about "a Julius Randle," "a Michael Porter Jr." not both. Nor does he specifically mention the trade deadline. Nor does he gauge interest levels on the other side.

Adding to the Nets' cache of assets, of course, does make sense from a number of aspects. If, as Sean Marks has said, the ultimate goal is a player or players who will "automatically and absolutely change the trajectory of the franchise." then the accumulation of assets makes sense, particularly in an era where trades, not free agency, is the route to acquiring stars and superstars. Look at all the stars who moved this summer. Only LeBron James signed as a free agent and that of course was an oddity.

Either of the two players would seem to be the ideal final piece for teams hoping to make a final run at a title. Randle and MPJ can put up big numbers and have. Porter Jr. also has a ring. Yeah sure, both have baggage, but their ability to get buckets is undisputed. As Basket News noted,

Porter Jr. is averaging 20.7 points, 7.0 rebounds, 1.4 assists and 1.6 steals, shooting 38.1% from the three-point line.

In the meantime, Randle holds the averages of 20.9 points, 7.7 rebounds and 4.9 assists.

Their contracts also militate for moves unless the Nets see them as part of their future. MPJ will be an unrestricted free agent come July. If the Nets don't plan on extending him — and there's no indication at this point that they do, then trading him for assets in the interim would be imperative. Similarly, Randle has a player option at the end of this season and he too can leave in July if Sean Marks & co. decide not to make him an offer or an offer he believes fair. (MPJ earns $40.8 million, Randle $33.3 million with an option of $35.8 million.)

Of the two, Porter Jr. would seem to be the more likely to return assets. He's nearly four years younger than Randle, although Randle is closer to iron man status, having averaged 68 games a year over the last eight. He also has proven that he can be a leader during his time at Flatbush and Atlantic.

It should also be noted that any trade is unlikely to produce the automatic and absolute game-changer Marks has spoken about. More likely, it'd be the big trade before the bigger trade which might happen in the summer of 2027.

One of the most intriguing but also less than concrete suggestions in Fischer's comment is that the Nets might move away from accumulating draft picks to seeking "potentially young pieces" — that is, undervalued players on rookie deals. After all, they do have 13 firsts and 19 seconds over the next seven years. That strategy has been a key element in a number of recent rebuilds, most notably OKC's acquisition of a young Shai Gilgeous-Alexander, in the trade of Paul George to the Clippers back in June 2019. That one trade got Sam Presti one building block after another from SGA to Aday Mara. We should be so lucky.

Of course the trade deadline — 3:00 p.m. ET on February 11 — is three months away and there are few trades in the first couple of months of any season. The unofficial start of trade season is December 15 for two reasons. It's roughly 20 games into the season when teams understand what their needs are going forward as well as the first day that players signed or resigned during the off-season can be dealt. That expands the pool of players who are available by dozens. That's especially relevant putting together complicated, multi-team deals. Perhaps the spate of early season injuries will move things up, but it's unlikely.

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:00 p.m. EDT Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation and the Federal Reserve Board today published feedback letters for resolution plans submitted in October 2025. The agencies did not identify any shortcomings or deficiencies in these resolution plan submissions.

Resolution plans, also known as living wills, describe a banking organization's strategy for orderly resolution in the event of material financial distress or failure. The agencies conducted a joint review of the 2025 resolution plan submissions from 15 banking organizations with over $250 billion in assets.

In addition, the agencies determined that the shortcoming previously identified in the 2021 BNP Paribas resolution plan has been satisfactorily addressed.

#2978. Federal Reserve Board finalizes changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 9:00 a.m. EDT Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Wednesday finalized changes to enhance the transparency and public accountability of its stress test and reduce volatility in its stress test-related capital requirements. The two final rules are largely similar to the proposed rules from 2025. The Board also requested comment on a proposal to better capture differences in banks' business models to generate fee income.

The Board conducts stress tests to ensure that large banks are sufficiently capitalized and able to lend to households and businesses even in a severe recession. In December 2024, the Board announced it would modify the test to improve its resilience.

"The stress test is an essential component of our regulatory capital framework," said Vice Chair for Supervision Michelle W. Bowman. "Today's changes preserve its resilience by ensuring that it is transparent, granular, and risk-sensitive. The public will now have greater assurance that the risks banks take will be reflected appropriately in their stress test losses and their capital requirements."

The first final rule requires the Board to invite public input annually on the stress test scenarios and any material model changes. It also updates the framework that guides the design of the hypothetical scenarios and adopts the models that will be used for the 2027 stress test. In addition, the final rule makes several adjustments to the stress test calendar. The final rule also updates the global market shock component, which is a set of shocks to specific market components applied to banks with large trading books. These banks will now be tested against two global market shock components each year, and the Board will use the shock that produces the largest losses for each firm to calculate the firm's stress test results.

The second final rule requires the Board, when calculating stress capital buffer requirements, to average the results from the two most recent annual supervisory stress tests for firms subject to the stress test in both years. The Board will begin averaging stress capital buffer requirements in 2028 to ensure that only models incorporating public input are used in the calculation.

The proposal would request public comment on a revision to the Board's noninterest income model that would improve the stress test's ability to capture business model diversity across firms. If adopted, these adjustments would replace the current noninterest income model that projects each bank's fee income under stress. Comments are due 60 days after publication in the Federal Register.

Together, the changes are likely to reduce year-over-year volatility in capital requirements by approximately 50 percent and are not expected to materially affect aggregate capital requirements.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Federal Register notice: Modifications to the Capital Plan Rule and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement

Federal Register notice: Enhanced Transparency and Public Accountability of the Supervisory Stress Test Models and Scenarios; Modifications to the Capital Planning and Stress Capital Buffer Requirement Rule, Enhanced Prudential Standards Rule, and Regulation LL

Federal Register notice: Request for Comment on Model Changes for the Boardâs 2027 Supervisory Stress Test

#2976. Rams cornerback Jaylen Watson to miss rest of season after shoulder surgery

Rams cornerback Jaylen Watson will have shoulder surgery and is out for the season, coach Sean McVay said Wednesday.

Watson, who signed with the Rams as a free agent after winning two Super Bowl titles in four seasons with the Kansas City Chiefs, reinjured his shoulder during last Sunday's victory over the Chargers at SoFi Stadium.

"That's a big blow," McVay said. "Bummer for him. He's been such a stud for us. He wanted to be able to play through it but just kind of understanding the totality of it all, just some of the different things, recommended to get that surgery.

"He's been everything and that much more that we had hoped."

Watson, who signed a three-year contract that included $34 million in guarantees, has one interception and nine passes defended.

"A guy that will be missed for sure," quarterback Matthew Stafford said. "He's a big-time talent and a guy that was kind of an enforcer for us on the edge."

Read more: Myles Garrett returning to Rams practice, but it's unclear when he'll play

Josh Wallace and Emmanuel Forbes Jr. are expected to help fill Watson's spot, and McVay indicated that the Rams could explore adding a cornerback before Tuesday's NFL trade deadline.

McDuffie, who has intercepted two passes, suffered a back injury against the Chargers and was not scheduled to practice on Wednesday.

The Rams opened the window for edge rusher Myles Garrett, tight end Terrance Ferguson and running back Ronnie Rivers to return from injured reserve, and all were scheduled to practice Wednesday.

Stafford downplayed having left the locker room on Sunday with an ice pack on his right elbow. He said he has iced throughout the season. "I'm 38 years old, trying to keep up with these young bucks," he said. "Little bit of soreness and stuff that is not uncommon this time of year. Again, haven't a rep in practice. Haven't missed a rep in a game. Feel good. So I'm not too worried about it." … The Rams cut receiver/punt returner Alex Bachman and re-signed him to the practice squad. McVay said running back Kyren Williams or receiver Tutu Atwell would return punts. The Rams also signed cornerback Cam Lampkin to the roster from the practice squad, added cornerback Nyzier Fourqurean to the practice squad and cut outside linebacker Garrett Nelson. Running back Dean Connors was placed on injured reserve after undergoing surgery for a hand injury suffered against the Chargers.

In Alvarado's absence, the Knicks have used Tyler Kolek more off the bench.

During Sunday's loss against the Magic, Kolek had zero points on 0-for-4 shooting, but came down with three rebounds and nine assists in his 15 minutes on the court. In Tuesday's win over the Pistons at MSG, the guard had two points, one rebound, three assists and two steals in his 11 minutes, helping the Knicks to their first win of the season.

Jose Alvarado will miss his second straight game with an ankle sprain as he will not feature for New York on Tuesday night against the Detroit Pistons.

Alvarado last played in the team's 107-103 loss against the Boston Celtics on Friday and struggled on the court during his 11 minutes of action.

He scored just two points to go along with two rebounds, two assists, and a steal, but he turned the ball over three times and finished as a minus-10.

Alvarado fared better in the season-opener, scoring eight points with three rebounds, three assists, two steals, and was a plus-6 in 16 minutes.

Jose Alvarado popped up on New York's injury report on Saturday and has now been ruled out for Sunday's game against the Orlando Magic with a left ankle sprain, according to head coach Mike Brown in his pregame press conference.

The backup point guard is the lone Knicks player to appear on the injury report.

#2970. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

#2964. For today and tomorrow, Labaron Philion needs more minutes

The Sixers are 7-1 after Monday night's 126-107 victory against Chicago. Three of their next four games are against fellow Eastern Conference contenders in Detroit, Cleveland and Boston. Perhaps for that reason, Nick Nurse might not make drastic changes to a rotation that keeps winning. After all, the Sixers haven't opened a playoff round at home since 2023 so locking up wins against other teams in the Eastern Conference now can only help for seeding later. However, there's one glaring mistake with Nurse's rotation that he needs to address.

When it comes to the bench, Nurse is playing Anfernee Simons, Dean Wade and Kentavious Caldwell-Pope regularly. These are veterans who figure to factor into the playoff rotation as well come the spring. However, whether Labaron Philon sees the court in April and beyond or not, the rookie out of Alabama should be seeing more than the eight minutes per game he's currently averaging. Even in a 19-point win against the Bulls, Philon played just two minutes.

While Philon was drafted towards the end of the first round this past summer, now's the time to find out what kind of player he could become. Of course, he can always grow into a bigger role next season and beyond, but should the Sixers set him on a path that's essentially burning a year of his rookie contract? Maybe that's a harsh way of phrasing things after only eight games, but it's the realest way of saying Philon isn't playing nearly enough.

For context, Tyrese Maxey was drafted 21st overall in 2020 which is just one spot earlier than Philon's 22nd overall in 2026. In the 2020-21 season, Maxey's rookie year, Maxey played 15 minutes per game and started eight games. That's a considerable uptick from the amount of minutes Philon is currently playing. Additionally, under Nick Nurse in 2024-25, Jared McCain played 25.7 minutes per game as a rookie in an injury-shortened season that saw him still start eight games. Neither of these comparisons is perfect as McCain was nearly a lottery pick having been selected 15th overall in 2024 and Maxey would have probably been a lottery pick under non-COVID circumstances in his draft eligible season in college.

Part of the reason we're saying the Sixers have a backup center problem yet again is because they whiffed on the Johni Broome pick recently and Adem Bona doesn't seem to have any offensive skillset. Granted, those two players were recent second-rounders by Philadelphia, but the point stands. You want to get your draft picks on the court in their rookie seasons in the regular season when your rotation should be deeper. It's cheap production if they're good right away, gives you more clarity on what they could develop into down the line, and would also make it easier to move off of them in trades while they still have some trade value if the early returns aren't to your liking.

In the short term, don't we all agree Maxey could use more of a breather than what he's currently getting? Maxey is averaging a whopping 37.4 minutes per game which is currently second in the NBA to only Denver's Jamal Murray. Maxey's injury history is nowhere near as lengthy as Joel Embiid's, but that doesn't mean Maxey wouldn't benefit from seeing his minutes scaled back a bit. Maxey's also doesn't seem like the kind of player that would be concerned about various individual accolades that might not come his way if his playing time decreased. More importantly, everyone can agree Maxey needs to be healthy and full go come playoff time for Philadelphia to make a deep run.

In the long term, wouldn't it be great if Philon developed into a sixth man at the very least for this team? Remember how awful Philly's bench was in the playoffs last year? Philon represents a long-term solution to what's been a long-term problem for the Sixers even if it's hard to see him ever getting on the court with long-term building blocks in Maxey, VJ Edgecombe and Jaylen Brown all at the same time.

Make no mistake, winning is fun and the Sixers are doing a lot of it right now. But the best teams in every sport can fuse the present with the future and win in the current season and in seasons of tomorrow. I get it Sixers fans, the current season is probably all that's on your mind and I can't blame you as the Sixers have as good a chance as any other team does to win the NBA championship in 2026-27. But there's going to be basketball played in 2027 and beyond and Labaron Philon was drafted to be a part of that basketball for Philly so this season should also be about finding out what role Philon can have within this organization's long-term plans.

→ Baca artikel asli

#2963. NFL Injury Report, Week 9: Latest news, updates on Bhayshul Tuten, Breece Hall, more players for fantasy football

Week 9 is the official midway point of the 2026 NFL season. So by now you know what you have on your fantasy football roster. You also know who has been healthy the majority of the year and which players have missed time. The injuries keep coming as we get into the middle of the campaign. As always, we're tracking all the latest news and updates for you, to help with those fantasy football lineups.

Lamar Jackson, BAL (thumb) — Will be placed on injured reserve

Tyler Huntley, BAL (toe) — Reportedly done for the season

Deshaun Watson, CLE (concussion protocol)

Ravens QBs: Jackson left in the first half of Sunday's loss to the Bills. He needs surgery on his thumb and is headed for IR. He'll miss at least four games, if not the rest of the season. After Jackson exited, Huntley entered the game and also had to leave due to injury (turf toe), which will end his season. That leaves Baltimore down to third-string rookie Joe Fagnano, who will start against Jacksonville on TNF. Hayden Winks takes a closer look at what Fagnano could bring. The Ravens traded for Jacoby Brissett, but he will not be ready to play this week.

The QB instability is obviously a huge downgrade to the passing game. Fagnano went 10 of 21 for 91 yards and an INT in his first NFL action in Week 8. Zay Flowers is more of a WR3/flex option while Derrick Henry should get a healthy amount of work, though in a tough matchup. Outside of those two, the rest of the Ravens offense should be avoided in fantasy until we see more from Fagnano or the Ravens make a deal for a QB.

Darnold: The Seahawks will be without Darnold in Week 9 against the Cardinals due to quad and rib injuries. With Darnold out, the Seahawks plan to start Drew Lock … who is also dealing with an elbow injury. Lock was excellent when he had to start in place of Darnold against Arizona in Week 2, going 19 of 26 for 235 yards and three touchdowns in a 31-7 win. Jaxon Smith-Njigba caught nine passes for 155 yards and three touchdowns in that game, proving JSN is QB-proof and an auto-start again this week.

Bhayshul Tuten, JAX (ankle/foot) — Game-time decision

Jeremiyah Love, ARI (ankle) — Will be placed on IR, miss 4-5 weeks

Breece Hall, NYJ (quad) — Expected to be back in Week 9

Travis Etienne Jr., NO (hamstring) — Could be activated off IR

Tuten: Jaguars coach Liam Coen said the running back will be a game-time decision for Thursday's game against the Ravens. Tuten was a DNP all three days to start the week, with ankle and foot issues. He is coming off his best game of the season. He took 18 touches for 159 yards and two scores in Week 8. Chris Rodriguez Jr. is also dealing with a hand injury but was limited on Monday and Tuesday.

If Tuten is out, Rodriguez gets elevated into the RB2/3 conversation given the Jags are a 10-point favorite. In the rout of the Colts last week, Rodriguez had 58 yards on seven touches and a score. Ameer Abdullah also saw six carries against Indy and could see some work if Tuten is sidelined.

Hall: We thought the Jets were going to get back Hall in Week 8 against the Raiders. That wasn't the case and we got another solid game from Braelon Allen. At this point, even if Hall comes back, expect Allen to be involved given how well he's performed. Allen is the RB5 overall in half-PPR scoring from Weeks 5-8 with Hall sidelined. We'll need to get a look at how the split is in Week 9 with Hall, who got in a limited practice Wednesday, expected back. Plus, it's a tough draw against Kansas City's run defense. But better matchups loom, so don't be surprised if both Hall and Allen are viable RB2/3 options.

Cardinals RBs: Love suffered a new high-ankle sprain in the comeback win over Dallas in OT on Sunday. Knight exited after a big hit and should land in concussion protocol. It'll be tough for Knight to clear in time for Week 9 against the Seahawks, but we'll see. Love has had so many issues with the ankle in his rookie season, and is expected to miss 4-5 weeks while on injured reserve, per NFL Network's Ian Rapoport. With Love already out, if Knight sits, Tyler Allgeier will be playable, but don't expect 33.6 fantasy points — or anywhere close to that — against Seattle's top defense.

But with Love on IR and Knight starting the season with a lesser role, expect Allgeier to be the lead back moving forward. There could be weeks when Knight is healthy in which the staff rides the hot hand. But Allgeier was brought in to be a high-value backup to Love. Allgeier will have RB2/3 value week-to-week depending on the matchup. Also keep an eye out for James Conner, who is on IR but could be a useful stash.

McCaffrey: CMC comes off the bye navigating a mild hamstring strain. It doesn't feel like this will cause him to miss Week 9 against the Raiders. But we saw before San Francisco's bye that Kyle Shanahan feels comfortable giving the ball to rookie Kaelon Black. We could see more of Black on Sunday to help keep McCaffrey fresh.

Garrett Wilson, NYJ (concussion protocol)

Emeka Egbuka, TB (foot) — Could play in Week 9

Jordyn Tyson, NO (hamstring) — Could come off IR

Watson: The Packers' lead receiver had a miraculous exit and return to TNF's win over the Panthers in Week 8. Watson finished with an 8-113-0 line on 10 targets, catching a few passes after coming back from the hospital. But Watson revealed he's dealing with a broken rib, which is less than ideal. He'll have ample time to rest before Week 9's matchup with the Patriots. Watson could play through the injury, but if he's active, he would be at risk of exiting the game early. If Watson ends up having to sit, Tucker Kraft and Savion Williams would likely see more targets. Matthew Golden would be elevated into WR1 territory as well.

Wilson: The Cardinals' receiver told reporters after Sunday's win over the Cowboys that he's dealing with broken ribs. It appears Wilson is going to play through the injury. He's a risky option in the flex in PPR formats going up against the Seahawks' stout defense in Week 9.

Egbuka: The Bucs wideout has missed the past two games, and Tampa's passing game sure could use him. The Bucs didn't complete a pass in Week 8's shutout win over the Falcons, instead opting to run the ball 56 times for 324 yards. That likely won't be the case in Chicago in Week 9. If Egbuka can get back — he didn't practice Wednesday — it's a decent matchup, though Tampa Bay ranks dead last in passing offense this season. Egbuka would be a risky flex at best.

Ferguson: The Cowboys lost Ferguson to a low-ankle sprain early in the game against Arizona. He didn't return and his status for Week 9 against the Colts could be up in the air. If Ferguson is out, this is still a passing game dominated by CeeDee Lamb and George Pickens. Ryan Flournoy has stepped up as a solid WR3 as well. Brevyn Spann-Ford played 88% of the snaps in Week 8 and had four catches on five targets for 31 yards. In deeper formats, Spann-Ford isn't a bad streamer. He could see garbage-time looks if the Cowboys run away against a struggling Indy team.

Ricky Pearsall, WR, SF (knee) — Out for season

Jayden Higgins, WR, HOU (knee) — Out for season

The data provide a representative picture of what Americans own, how and how much they borrow, and how they bank. The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2951. Dumont family takes full control of Mavericks, buying Miriam Adelson's shares

Patrick and Sivan Dumont have formally become the controlling owners of the Dallas Mavericks after purchasing Miriam Adelson's shares of the franchise last month, a story broken by Tim MacMahon of ESPN. The Dumonts now own 69% of the Mavericks.

While this is a change at the top, it should have little day-to-day impact, as Patrick Dumont has served as team governor and been the acting owner on the ground since the day Mark Cuban sold the team to the Dumonts and Adelsons. Here is what Dumont told ESPN:

"It's really about long-term investment in the city of Dallas," Dumont told ESPN on Tuesday. "The Dumont family is really committed to building what we hope is the best arena in the NBA, a really significant investment in the city that will help create the new 40-year home for the Dallas Mavericks, a great experience for our fans and inspire our team to win championships. That's really what we're focused on. We had the opportunity, and we're really excited about it."

As Cuban said when he sold his majority share of the team, the NBA has become a real estate business, and what Dumont is looking to build is not just a new arena but the anchor of a much larger development. That is the trend with professional sports franchises in general, including the Dallas Cowboys.

Dumont, the Las Vegas Sands Co. CEO, has experience with building and development. He didn't have experience as an NBA owner when he took over the team, and that position comes with a learning curve (a longer one for some than others). Dumont became a villain early in his ownership of the Mavericks when he approved the controversial trade of Luka Doncic to the Lakers, which led to severe backlash from fans in Dallas. Dumont told ESPN's MacMahon he learned from those experiences.

"The most important thing every day is just trying to work harder to get better," Dumont said. "It's something that really applies in life, but it's something that really applies to here. It's been an experience as a new owner in the league. Dallas is a great sports city. I think we have the best fans in the NBA, very passionate, and it's my job to make sure that we win and that we support our fans. I'm really focused on that. I've learned that there are a lot of things you can do -- sometimes they work, sometimes they don't -- but you've got to keep pushing, got to keep going."

Dumont made changes in the front office, bringing in Masai Ujiri to run the show, then Ujiri fired coach Jason Kidd to bring in Dusty May. Dumont spent millions to upgrade the team's practice facilities. All of this with the idea of trying to maximize the team around Cooper Flagg, who in his second NBA season is looking elite and like an All-NBA player already. The new arena will ultimately be remembered as the one Cooper built.

#2950. This Janus Henderson fund manager owns CoreWeave debt — here's why he's sticking with it

CoreWeave can overcome the market's skepticism about its debt.

That isn't the consensus view — some Coreweave debt remains deeply distressed with yields above 15%. But Coreweave should power through this tough stretch, according to John Lloyd, global head of multi-sector and corporate credit at Janus Henderson.

"It's a little bit of overreacting," Lloyd tells CNBC, referring to the sell-off in Coreweave bonds, which have tanked along with its equity.

Lloyd owns Coreweave 9.75% bonds due in 2031 in the firm's Multi-Sector Income Fund. The yield to maturity on the debt is now 15.97%, a more than 70% jump since its issuance in April. Bond prices and yields move in opposite directions.

"Bond prices are lower, spreads are wider, yields are wider, and I don't see a huge fundamental change in the ecosystem right now that would warrant that, other than their higher cost of financing, which I think they can get creative with," Lloyd added.

CoreWeave has taken on a massive amount of debt to fund its data center buildout. The company has roughly $51.6 billion in total debt, including about $35 billion in notes, bonds and loans.

The market is getting nervous about how CoreWeave will be able to keep financing its data centers, and at what cost. Its stock has tumbled 20% over the past week, while the spread on its five-year credit default swaps is nearly 1,081 basis points — lower than earlier in the session but up 13% week over week. Spreads of 1,000 basis points and above are generally a sign of credit distress.

CoreWeave shares moved roughly 2% higher on Tuesday, after tumbling over the past several days and hitting a 52-week low earlier in the session. Its bond yields and spreads remain highly elevated.

Lloyd believes the selloff is a bump in the road for the neocloud company.

The market has been spooked by the jump in Treasury rates, which pushes up funding costs for companies, he explained. The focus largely turned to companies like CoreWeave that have refinance risk when rates move higher, he said.

CoreWeave leverage is high with a debt-to-equity ratio of 702%, up from 290% a year ago. Moreover, CoreWeave will spend an estimated $36.4 billion on capital expenditures this year against Ebitda of $7.5 billion, according to consensus estimates.

Revenue and Ebitda will ramp up next year, hitting $26.5 billion and $16.7 billion respectively, analysts estimate. But capex will hit $47.9 billion, indicating the company may need to finance its data center buildout with more equity issuance and/or debt.

Lloyd isn't necessarily worried about CoreWeave's ability to finance its capital expenditures, despite the fact that its capex outweighs its free-cash flow.

"Next year … they're going to be generating operating free-cash flow that'll support some of that capex need," he said.

The company can also turn to other methods of funding, like more GPU financing, which will allow it to borrow against its graphics processing units or rental contracts, he noted. In addition, CoreWeave could ask its biggest customers — including Microsoft, Meta Platforms and OpenAI — to increase their prepayments, he added.

"They can probably get creative enough where they don't even need to come to the high-yield market," Lloyd said.

The company also doesn't have any near-term maturities, which means it isn't facing a refinancing risk anytime soon, he added.

Meanwhile the supply/demand dynamic should remain tight across the AI compute landscape. Overall adoption rates within the ecosystem are running above consensus demand curves, he said. Add to that a bottleneck in supply, with chips and memory power sold out, and it's a nice set up for CoreWeave.

"All data point to the business model functioning very, very well," Lloyd said. "CoreWeave is one of the best-in-class at getting data centers up and actually running them," he added, noting that CoreWeave's financials have been improving throughout the year.

Another turning point could come if Anthropic goes forth with an IPO, expected in November, followed by OpenAI. Successful IPOs for the frontier labs could soothe market nerves and help support the neocloud operators.

"You just need more of those data points where people become more comfortable with the AI demand side," Lloyd said.

CoreWeave still needs to convince markets it can handle higher market rates and get through the financing bottlenecks.

Lloyd sees the company making it, albeit with some bumps along the way. The bonds should recover, he says, "just not in that straight line."

#2943. Jeremy Sochan’s fall from NBA lottery pick and champion to the G League

When the Spurs first drafted Jeremy Sochan out of Baylor with the 9th overall pick in 2022, I remember probably being more thrilled with the pick than warranted. I hardly knew anything about him, but I did know he had size and was not a guard, and that's all I wanted. If you ignore 2019 (when the Spurs drafted a bust power forward in Luka Samanic and a truly "small" forward in Keldon Johnson) and 2015 (when they drafted and stashed center Nikola Milutinov), you otherwise had to go all the way back to 2014 and Kyle Anderson to find the last time they drafted a player who was not a guard or small wing in the first round. (And even then, Anderson was more of a guard in a forward's body.)

As good as some of those picks were — steals with Dejounte Murray and Derrick White late in the first round, Devin Vassell as their first lottery pick since Tim Duncan — others were merely okay or even complete busts that failed to address what was constantly an area of weakness during the post-Kawhi Leonard era: size at the forward positions. They finally changed up their drafting pattern when they took Sochan, and he seemed like a great fit with the club, both by filling a position of need and bringing a sense of humor during a couple of rough "tanking" seasons.

He had a good rookie season with the Spurs, making the All-Rookie 2nd Team and proving to be one of the better perimeter defenders in the league. He also had a knack for defending the rim and grabbing rebounds despite being slightly undersized when playing center. Across his first three seasons, he averaged a consistent 11+ points and 6+ rebounds on solid shooting numbers — inside the arc. A good comparison for him would be Malik Rose (the Spurs' undersized backup big man from their first two championship teams) but therein lies the problem. Rose was ideal for that role in the 2000's, but a power forward/undersized center without a three-point shot just doesn't fit today's game, and that's where Sochan found himself.

After winning the lottery and drafting Victor Wembanyama in 2023, experimentation began to figure out how to best utilize them together. There was the ghastly Point Sochan experiment in his second season (Wemby's first), and he lost his starting spot and eventually fell out of the rotation while dealing with various injuries during his third season. Finally, by last season, it was clear he wasn't part of Mitch Johnson's future plans despite having good chemistry with Wemby. He requested a trade and was eventually waived and signed with the New York Knicks, where he didn't play a role but at least won a championship against his former team.

That would be the last bright moment in his NBA career that started promising but dimmed with time. He signed a training camp deal with the Portland Trail Blazers this summer, but despite a solid preseason, he was waved before the start of the regular season. He was then drafted 1st overall by the Texas Legends in the G League Draft (the Dallas Mavericks' affiliate), marking a stark fall from just a year ago.

At his introductory press conference, he was humble and respectful. When asked about the roller coaster of the last 10+ months, had the following to say:

"I think the way I grew up, my family, my parents; it's always been like that. I could look at it negatively (the Portland situation). But when I look back at that situation, I think the two months I was there, I grew a lot. I was healthy throughout the whole training camp, preseason and open gym. So I think that was a blessing. When we got into preseason games, the minutes I got I think I used them to the best of my ability. I had good games. So what would be the point of being negative and angry for getting waived? Of course, you can look at it as that's the NBA and I have a contract. But at the same time I have good momentum. So, why would I hold weight on something that I can't control? And instead, be in this moment and do what I can do and keep that momentum going."

He also discussed having to let go of the past and focus on what he's learned so he can move forward.

"I think the biggest lesson for me is just being as present as possible. I keep going with this 'presence' because there's a lot of things that you can't control — in life and in basketball. Sometimes I feel like I've been a victim of the past where I've kinda held onto that, instead of acknowledging that and the just keep going. I think that's the biggest lesson, for sure."

It has certainly been tough to watch how far the former fan favorite has fallen. Even if it was for the better, I was personally sad to see Sochan leave San Antonio (enough so that I didn't begrudge him for getting a ring with the Knicks). He mentions being a victim of the past, and while I'm not sure what his own context is, he is certainly a prime example of a player who would have been much better off in a bygone era of basketball than he is today.

Hopefully this isn't the end of his NBA journey. While it's odd to see a former NBA lottery pick with four years of experience enter into the G League draft, there are no experience limits, and he fit the requirement of having recently been waived from a training camp roster. Choosing the G League — as opposed to going overseas — shows that he is still very much interested in making it back to the NBA and is not ready to settle for anything less.

Hopefully his signature competitive drive and determination keeps him going, and he eventually makes it back someday. He was a fan favorite for a reason, and even if the parting was mutual, I think we can all agree that we want Sochan to get another chance in the NBA, even if it's not with the Spurs.

#2936. Raiders release early-season TD machine Cody White

A few weeks ago, wide receiver Cody White was the Las Vegas Raiders' secret weapon. Now, he's out of a job.

The team released the veteran on Wednesday after as they needed room to activate reserve tight end Carter Runyon from the injured reserve. The Raiders had until Wednesday to either put Runyon on the 53-man roster or shelve him for the rest of the season. Runyon was hurt in training camp and put on the injured reserve.

The Raiders signed White to the practice squad in September, reuniting him with head coach Klint Kubiak. They won a Super Bowl ring together with the Seattle Seahawks last season. The Raiders elevated White from the practice squad for the first three games of the season before signing him to the 53-man roster when fellow receiver Jack Bech went on the injured reserve. Bech returned in Week 8.

White's first three receptions were touchdowns and he made some big plays on special teams. He ended up having eight catches for 112 yards and three touchdowns on 19 targets. His play time dwindled in Week 2 to two offensive snaps. If he clears waivers Thursday, the Raiders could sign him back to the practice squad.

Runyon, an undrafted free agent in 2025, played in 13 games last season. He logged 206 snaps on special teams and 45 on offense. He is the Raiders' fourth tight end with Brock Bowers, Michael Mayer and Ian Thomas.

The Raiders have six wide receivers on the 53-man roster. To take White's place on the game-day roster, the Raiders will likely choose between Runyon and receivers Dont'e Thornton and Malik Benson, who were both inactive in Week 8.

#2933. What Steve Kerr told Warriors' Yaxel Lendeborg after Luka Dončić trash talk

What Steve Kerr told Warriors' Yaxel Lendeborg after Luka Dončić trash talk originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

Few rookies draw the spotlight after just one game in the NBA, but Yaxel Lendeborg managed to do so following the Warriors' season-opening loss to the Lakers.

The Warriors rookie's callout of Luka Dončić in his postgame press conference made national headlines. All eyes were on Lendeborg to see how he would follow it up, particularly when the league's scoring leader and the Lakers rolled into Chase Center the following week.

Warriors coach Steve Kerr, speaking Wednesday with ESPN's Ernie Johnson on "Inside the NBA," shared how he coached Lendeborg after the rookie's comments to the media.

"We talked to him as a staff; we talked as a team," Kerr recalled. "Steph [Curry] had a great comment after that Laker game. He said, 'Hey, I love Yaxel's competitive desire, but now he's got to back it up,' and I thought that was perfectly said.

"So, I kind of reiterated that with Yaxel, like, hey — we love this spirit, we love the competitiveness, but just know that Luka's going to be coming."

Dončić, to no surprise, came out of the gates hot in the rematch, scoring 21 points with five 3-pointers made. Golden State was down 38-15 after that first quarter but mounted a furious comeback to finish with a 112-95 win.

Although Lendeborg's fiery comments might have provided some extra motivation for Dončić in his opening flurry, Kerr appreciates the rookie's spirit.

"There's an old saying in coaching — you'd rather rein a guy back in than try to get him going," Kerr said. "And I think that's true with Yaxel. He wants to be great. He's got this competitive spirit.

"So, in terms of reining him in, it's more just — hey, this is what the league is about. You're going to see these kinds of challenges all the time. And do what Steph did: back it up. And Yaxel's been doing that. So, I love coaching him. I think he's got an incredible future."

Lendeborg only scored six points in the rematch with the Lakers but has scored in double digits in each of his other seven games played this season. In his next game after the rematch, Lendeborg scored a career-high 37 points.

Entering Wednesday, Lendeborg ranks third among rookies with 21.5 points per game. He has a chance to improve that Thursday night against the Miami Heat at Chase Center.

The next time Lendeborg will have an opportunity to face Dončić and the Lakers will be Jan. 21.

#2931. 2026 Atlanta Braves Player Review: Austin Wynns

A serious bout of catcher injuries struck the Atlanta Braves during the first half of the season, and desperate times called for desperate measures. One of those measures, of the band-aid variety, ended up being Austin Wynns, who spent two weeks with the club in June and appeared in six games.

The Braves added Wynns in the middle of the season, while both Drake Baldwin and Sean Murphy were down with injuries. They sent cash considerations to the Angels in exchange for Wynns' services, and designated longtime third-string catcher Chadwick Tromp in a corresponding move. The reasoning was a bit weird, though it was all meant to be a temporary fix anyway: Sandy Leon was getting the bulk of playing time anyway, so the Braves opted to acquire a backup-to-Leon instead of using Tromp in that role for whatever reason.

One silly thing about the Wynns acquisition is that the Braves kind of prompted it with their own DFA of Jonah Heim earlier in the season. The Athletics, who acquired Wynns in a midseason trade in 2025, DFAed him three days after trading for Heim. He was released and signed with the Angels, but didn't get promoted to their big league team. Three weeks later, the Braves traded cash considerations (which is what they received for Heim) to Anaheim for Wynns.

There weren't any real expectations, to be blunt.

Coming into 2026, the 35-year-old Wynns had a career 74 wRC+ that featured a pretty sizable xwOBA overperformance (.282 wOBA, .265 xwOBA) in 783 career PAs spanning parts of six major league seasons. He had never topped 180 PAs in a single season, nor 1.0 fWAR. He came kind of close in 2025 riding a massive xwOBA overperformance (.365 wOBA, .295 xwOBA), though. He also wasn't highly regarded defensively, with just one good defensive year (2023) behind the plate and some substantially below-average performance in other seasons.

He wasn't doing much of anything in 2026, either — a -47 wRC+ (yes, a negative sign) in 43 PAs in Sacramento before getting released. Basically, the Braves acquired a guy who had 0.6 career fWAR in 826 PAs at the big league level.

While it's not entirely clear what the Braves were thinking in swapping Tromp for Wynns, it was a fairly inconsequential shuffle to try and survive until Baldwin and Murphy returned.

Wynns was only with the Braves for two weeks, acquired on June 3, recalled on June 4, and granted free agency on June 16, the day after Baldwin returned from the Injured List.

During his time in Atlanta, he appeared in six games and was 1-for-14 at the plate. He had just one single and no walks, leaving him with a sad .071/.071/.071 slash line that was somehow even worse than his -47 wRC+ with the Athletics. In what would be his final game with the team, he went 0-for-4 with three strikeouts against the Mets. It wasn't much better behind the plate, where he allowed six stolen bases and didn't throw a single runner out, and lost nearly a run of defensive value despite barely playing, even after accounting for the massive catcher positional adjustment. In total, he put up -0.3 fWAR in 14 PAs, which by far his worst stint-with-a-team-in-a-season of his career, small sample size and all.

At least the baseball gods threw him a bit of a bone: after he rejected the Braves' outright assignment and became a free agent, he signed a minor league deal with the Rangers, spent a month playing in their minor league system, and then got promoted for a month on the big league roster. In 32 PAs in Arlington, he somehow managed a .312 wOBA with a .195 xwOBA, and his defense improved-ish. All in all, Wynns finished 2026 with -0.7 fWAR in 89 PAs, but it would've been worse without that stint in the Lone Star State. The Rangers outrighted him in August, and he once again elected free agency, and spent the tail end of the season in the minors with the Cubs.

It wasn't a fun season for Wynns, as he tied his career-worst in fWAR, had a sub-100 PA season for just the third time, and had a career-worst wRC+ (2).

Per Statcast, his defense was commensurate with being in the 73rd percentile in blocks above average? That's about the only good thing you can say for his season, and it's largely negated by the fact that he was below average in caught stealing above average, framing and pop time.

In somewhat fitting fashion, Wynns' "best" game as a Brave was the beginning of their downfall — that 6-5, extra-inning loss to the White Sox. Wynns' hit was a one-out single that put runners on the corners on with one out while the Braves already had a one-run lead. They didn't end up scoring in the frame, in part due to a fielder's choice that threw the runner trying to go home out, and, well, you know how that game and the rest of June went for the Braves.

Not to be harsh, but, pretty much everything. He wasn't expected to be a plus bat but one could have at least hoped he could've improved on his -47 wRC+ from earlier in the year. That's how you get a career-worst year.

If you had to pick a specific game from his six as a Brave, well, the day after that horrible loss to the White Sox, Wynns really faceplanted. He flew out with the bases loaded and two out in the second. Then he flew out with the go-ahead run on second and two out in the fourth. Then, after the leadoff man reached in the seventh and Wynns represented the tying run, he lined out. He didn't get another chance in that game, as Eli White pinch-hit for him and drew a walk in the ninth, representing the tying run, but the Braves lost that game, 2-1.

With Wynns set to turn 36 in December, it seems quite possible this is the end of the road for his major league career. He may get another chance with a team if they pick up a rash of catcher injuries, but it's hard to imagine that would be the Braves again. There are also enough catchers out there on the waiver wire that a guy who neither hits nor fields the catcher position particularly well doesn't seem like anyone's first choice, but… the Braves did deliberately acquire him last year, so you never know.

#2929. BlackRock's Rick Rieder sees 'historic' opportunity in high-quality bonds

Investors may be wary of the volatility hitting parts of the bond market right now, but they have an "historic" opportunity to grab attractive yields in high-quality credit, Rick Rieder, BlackRock's chief investment officer for global fixed income, told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Hypothetically, it's now possible for investors to build a diversified portfolio that yields more than 7% with less than three years of duration and an average credit quality of A-, he said. Duration is a measurement of a bond's price sensitivity to fluctuations in rates, and issues with shorter maturities tend to have lower duration.

"Not only is it historic in that these yields are where they are, but the only time these yields are ever here is when there's some problem, like an economic problem or default problem," Rieder said in an interview about his fourth-quarter outlook, released Wednesday.

That isn't the case now, where the economy remains strong, he said. "So you don't really have to worry about defaults of any significance."

While Rieder didn't specifically identify the credits in the aforementioned portfolio, the iShares Aaa - A Rated Corporate Bond ETF (QLTA) has a 30-day SEC yield of 5.68% and an effective duration of 6.48 years — higher than Rieder's low duration focus.

The iShares Flexible Income Active ETF (BINC), which is managed by Rieder, has a 5.66% 30-day SEC yield and three-year effective duration, but its portfolio extends beyond investment-grade credit.

Rieder sees the best opportunity right now in investment-grade corporate bonds — which he pointed out he hasn't said "in a really long time." Investment grade bonds are rated AAA through BBB- by S&P and Fitch, while Moody's rates them Aaa through Baa3.

That includes hyperscaler bonds, which have flooded the market with issuance this year as they ramp up their AI infrastructure. Alphabet, Amazon, Meta Platforms and Oracle have issued a total of about $228.4 billion in bonds year to date, as of Oct. 20, according to LSEG.

"They're very low leverage. They have tremendous amounts of cash flow coming in," Rieder said of the hyperscalers in general. "You're now getting to buy these entities which are higher quality, paying you a very attractive yield to get it."

Rieder's iShares Flexible Income Active ETF has upped its allocation to investment-grade corporates to 14.3% of its portfolio. In July, that stood at 8.5%.

That said, investors need to be patient and sit tight since rates could still move higher from here, he advised.

"Your starting point is by far your best barometer of what your return is going to be," he noted. "So if you get seven and a quarter, maybe you get eight, maybe you get six, but ... if your holding period is a year or longer, you're going to get that yield."

Caution should also be exercised outside of investment-grade credit, which requires more selectivity and deep research, he warned. That includes AI names.

"There's parts of the data center supply that's not hyperscaler guaranteed or hyperscaler backed," he said. "That we've been more cautious around, and we'll continue to be."

The bond market, particularly high-yield credit, has been rocked as investor concern grows over the massive amount of debt some companies are taking out to fund their artificial intelligence build-out.

Junk-rated CoreWeave has become the poster child, seeing its stock drop, bond yields jump and credit-default spreads widen. Oracle, which is sitting in the lower end of investment grade and is seen as a bellwether on the broader AI market, has also taken some hits.

Rieder is also keeping an eye on the Federal Reserve, which is expected to raise its federal funds rate again in December to combat inflation. The market is currently pricing in a nearly 90% probability that the central bank will hike at that time, according to the CME Group FedWatch tool.

The money manager expects that to happen, although he does not agree with it. Higher rates are hurting parts of the economy, like housing, while not having much of an impact on other areas — such as tech companies' capital expenditures, which have become a big driver of growth, he said.

"They're not borrowing off the overnight funds rate," Rieder noted. "There's a lot of floating rate finance in housing, credit card, auto loan finance, and you're seeing those areas already under pressure."

Instead, the market is and will continue to price the AI supply coming into the market, he added.

Rieder sees a number of possible risks ahead, including rising levels of government debt across the globe. In the United States, the budget deficit has grown to $2 trillion for fiscal 2026.

"The more the central banks raise the rates, the cost of debt to these to already indebted governments is something we've got to be careful about over time," he said.

Bond supply coming from AI companies should also be watched.

"With the amount of supply that's coming — data center, hyperscaler — there's some crowding-out effect that's taking place," he said. "With government rates moving higher, that is something that we just got to keep an eye on."

In the meantime, Rieder would just continue to reap the rewards of high investment-grade yields "and smile a lot," he said.

#2927. Reviewing the Bears’ PFN grades from Week 8 vs. Seahawks

The Bears made an impact in primetime on Monday night, decisively beating the Seahawks 34-17 to improve their record to 6-2 for the 2026-27 NFL season. It was a convincing win which saw Chicago excel on both sides of the ball, limiting the Seahawks' offense and gashing apart what is generally seen as one of the best defenses in the NFL. There were a lot of positive takeaways out of the All-22, and the analytics reflect that.

Each week, I'll review how Chicago's eligible performers graded by Pro Football Network's Impact Scoring model. Now that the rest of the NFL has caught up, and the numbers have all been crunched, here's how Week 8 looked.

Caleb Williams was an above-average performer at quarterback across the board on Monday night, which is made more impressive considering the quality of the Seahawks' defense. He graded in at least the top 12 in such categories as EPA per dropback, success rate, adjusted net yards per attempt, completion percentage, completion percentage over expected, and passing yards.

It helped that he had three efficient weapons to throw to; though Luther Burden III led all Bears weapons in PFN Impact grading, it was Rome Odunze who had the highest EPA per target, ranking fourth in the metric at +1.07. Likewise, Chicago had two top-ten performances at guard for the week out of both Jonah Jackson and Joe Thuney.

Jeffery Simmons made his Bears debut on Monday, and he absolutely shined on the big stage. His six tackles tied for the most on the team and tied for third among 92 qualified defensive tackles, and he also added a sack into the mix, despite facing a heavy double-team focus from Seattle's offensive line. The two biggest benefactors from Simmons' presence in Week 8 were Montez Sweat and Devin Bush, as both ended up with sacks, and the latter tied Simmons for the Bears' lead in tackles for the night.

The Seahawks picked on Tyrique Stevenson often, as he was targeted ten times and gave up a targeted passer rating of 124.2 for the game. However, strong auxiliary performances from Cam Lewis, Austin Booker, James Lynch, and D'Marco Jackson gave Chicago an all-around solid defensive outing.

Cairo Santos hit all of his field goal and extra point attempts, nailing his two field goal tries from 44 and 38 yards, respectively. His +0.4 field goals made over expected tied for the fourth best in the NFL in Week 8. On the other end of special teams, Tory Taylor finished above average with a 45.0-yard net punt average, including the lucky bounces he got on the one punt that got tipped.

#2922. A Surprisingly Dominant Relief Campaign

Welcome to our series grading every member of the San Diego Padres' 40-man roster. The roster being evaluated is current as of Oct. 18, prior to the end of the World Series. Because of that, it will include players who will be free agents once the postseason concludes. Each player will be graded on their performance in the 2026 season, including the playoffs. That performance will be weighted based on how much time the player spent with the team (availability is the best ability).

The Padres signed Matsui to a five-year, $28 million deal heading into the 2024 season after the left-hander became an international free agent. He was the first free agent they signed that offseason to bolster their pitching staff after dealing away superstar Juan Soto to the New York Yankees. Matsui gave them modest results in 2024 and '25, posting sub-4.00 ERAs over 60-plus innings in both years.

He took that to new heights in 2026. Matsui posted a blistering 1.83 ERA and .171 opponent batting average across 64 innings of work. Since coming over from Japan, Matsui has struggled with command (1.22 career WHIP). In spite of that, he managed to work out of jams in 2026, regularly recording outs in key situations. Matsui was especially dominant down the stretch with a 1.42 ERA in his final 30 appearances and a 0.68 ERA in his last 15 outings.

Some of that may have been due to luck. Matsui's expected ERA (3.03) was more than a full run above his actual mark (1.83). However, his expected opponent batting average (.170) was almost the exact same as his actual, so it's hard to make heads or tells of it. Fielding Independent Pitching (FIP) is a metric used to determine how dominant a pitcher is on his own (using homers, walks, hit-by-pitches and strikeouts). Essentially, it measures how good a pitcher is without his defense. Matsui's 4.18 FIP isn't exactly sterling, so the defense may have had something to do with it.

All that said, his season with the Friars was incredible. Matsui surprised just about everyone by being one of the most dominant left-handed relievers in the game. He was even better than his teammate Adrian Morejon (though he certainly doesn't have the same track record as Morejon). Matsui decided earlier this week to remain with the Padres for the 2027 season, choosing not to exercise his opt-out. He'll fill an important role in the Padres' bullpen with the potential departures of Morejon and Jason Adam on the rise.

#2918. 49ers defense receives great news as Nick Bosa is suddenly ‘ready to go’

Reinforcements continue to come on the way for the San Francisco 49ers.

A day after wide receiver Deebo Samuel was seen working out at the team's practice facility in Santa Clara, the 49ers saw Nick Bosa back in action as a limited participant during Wednesday's practice.

Nick Bosa returns to practice on Wednesday as a limited participant. Getty Images

Bosa has been out since September 24. after he pulled his calf muscle during a team practice.

According to ESPN NFL reporter Nick Wagoner, Bosa isn't concerned about reaggravating his injury, as he said he feels "great" and is "ready to go."

Bosa, 29, has appeared in only two games for San Francisco and has seven tackles etched to his name.

The 49ers are coming off a bye week after suffering their first loss of the season in Week 7 to the Atlanta Falcons, where the San Francisco defense gave up 29 points, while the offense put up 20.

Bosa told David Lombardi of the SF Standard that "It's been tough," not being with his teammates the past six weeks.

"The team obviously had my back and held it down. We're getting some guys back this week so it's a long year and I kind of reminded myself that I need to be ready for the long haul and I think I'll be just that," Bosa said.

49ers' Gracen Halton celebrating with teammate Khalid Kareem during a Monday Night Football game against the Commanders. AP Photo/Kelley L. Cox

The former Defensive Player of the Year missed most of last season due to a torn ACL he suffered during their third game of the season.

Bosa coming back to the football field will come at the same time the 49ers welcome a former Bay Area rival, the Las Vegas Raiders, who are off to a surprising 4-4 start to the season and are coming off a 38-33 road victory against the New York Jets.

Aside from Bosa coming back, Wagoneer reported that Shanahan gave some good news about Mike Evans who's exited Week 6 early with an Achilles injury.

"I think we've made a lot of progress. I think he's going in the right direction and it's something we definitely got to be smart with, but we're not going to overly do it," Shanahan said. "We just want to wait till he's healthy and he's still working his way to get there."

Wagoner also reported that Christian McCaffrey was surprised when he injured his hamstring during the Atlanta game, but noted he could also return to the field this week.

"It wasn't too bad," McCaffrey said. "I think having the bye week was awesome, but I feel great."
