#2867. Qualcomm stock drops 7% on weak earnings forecast as memory shortage weighs on smartphone sales

Qualcomm reported quarterly results on Wednesday that topped Wall Street estimates, but the chipmaker's earnings forecast came in lighter than expected. The stock slid 7% in extended trading.

Here's how the chipmaker did versus LSEG consensus estimates:

For the current quarter, Qualcomm said it sees earnings per share of between $2.15 and $2.35 on revenue of $10.4 billion to $11.2 billion. Analysts expected earnings per share of $2.27 on revenue of $10.48 billion.

Most of Qualcomm's chip sales come from system-on-chips and modems for smartphones. It's a market that's currently in turmoil as rising memory costs force handset makers to raise prices, hurting demand. Adjusted company revenue fell 7% during from a year earlier.

Qualcomm raised prices in September. Still, handset chip sales during the quarter fell 26% to $5.14 billion, trailing a StreetAccount estimate of $5.21 billion. Qualcomm said it's starting to see sequential growth in the category, and that Android shipments in China have bottomed, even though the company predicts the handset market will be flat in 2027.

Under CEO Cristiano Amon, Qualcomm has tried to turn investor attention to other kinds of chips. The company is looking to enter the fast-growing market for AI data center infrastructure. It had $650 million in data center revenue during the quarter, mostly from customizing and connectivity work, Amon said.

Amon said Qualcomm expects $5 billion in data center revenue in fiscal 2027. He said the company recently signed a customer for optical connectivity and had engaged a global hyperscaler.

"We don't have a demand issue; it's a supply issue," Amon said. "If I had more supply, I would probably be doing better."

Qualcomm also sells chips for cars and other automotive uses. Revenue in that division soared 89% on an annual basis to $1.99 billion, beating StreetAccount expectations of $1.66 billion.

Internet of things revenue includes low-power chips, smart glasses SoCs, laptop chips, as well as the company's bid to provide cellular infrastructure. Earlier on Wednesday, Qualcomm announced a deal to provide technology to SpaceX's cellular buidlout. IoT revenue increased 4% from a year earlier to $1.88 billion, slightly higher than estimates.

Qualcomm's QTL division includes revenue from licensing its intellectual property for 5G and other technologies to other companies. Qualcomm said during the quarter that it had renewed licensing agreements with Apple, Huawei, and Transsion.

"QTL will maintain its scale going forward," Amon said.

QTL revenue sank 7% to $1.3 billion, in line with a StreetAccount estimate.

Anthropic on Monday unveiled its Form S-1 filing, taking the company one step closer to completing its long-awaited move to the public markets, which is now seen as almost guaranteed to come this year.

Speculators on prediction market platform Kalshi place 95% odds that the artificial intelligence giant officially announces an initial public offering by the end of 2026.

#2862. Prediction markets cement their status after forecasting Democratic sweep of Congress

Prediction market platforms cemented their role in election forecasting on Tuesday, when markets accurately predicted a host of races before the polls closed, and projected outcomes before news outlets.

Democrats were seen as favored to flip both the U.S. House of Representatives and Senate on prediction markets before the first polls closed at 6:00 p.m. ET Tuesday, and were seen as narrow favorites to take at least 53 Senate seats, while a 54th was forecast as a toss-up.

So far, Democrats have won 54 Senate seats — including wins in Iowa, which prediction markets placed at 50-50 odds, and South Carolina, where Republicans narrowly favored. Outstanding results remain in Mississippi and Alaska, where prediction markets give Democrat Scott Colom and Mary Peltola 90% odds of winning.

Leading prediction market platforms were actively projecting races on X Tuesday night. Those projections, at times, came before many media outlets made their own projections, or before even a substantial number of votes had been counted.

Kalshi was confident making those calls, as long as odds for a candidate were above 99% and trading activity for the opposite candidate "dried out,"  said Jack Such, a spokesperson for the platform.

Polymarket celebrated on X how it projected races, saying that in 10 key races, the platform called races ahead of outlets including CNN, Fox News and the Associated Press by a median 31 minutes.

"It's now routinely hours between when prediction markets call it versus the TV networks," said Polymarket CEO Shayne Coplan in an X post. "No knock to them, but now there's Polymarket, and many people are realizing they want to know what's happening in real time."

Even in races where markets initially favored the candidate who eventually lost, they quickly adjusted once initial vote counts came in. In South Carolina, Democratic Senate candidate Annie Andrews was seen as the favorite on Kalshi to win shortly after 7:30 p.m., only 30 minutes after polls closed. In Texas, where Democratic state Rep. Gina Hinojosa ousted Republican Gov. Greg Abbott, Hinojosa's odds on Polymarket jumped from 39% to 73% within five minutes of initial poll closings.

"You now have financially motivated number crunchers doing this, around the world, that give you that signal just that much faster," said Flip Pidot, a member of the board of directors at the Coalition for Political Forecasting.

Still, a successful night for prediction markets shouldn't stop efforts aimed at stronger regulation of the platforms, said Michelle Kendler-Kretsch, a researcher at the Anti-Corruption Data Collective, a nonprofit. She cited a recent Reuters/Ipsos survey that found most Americans are against using prediction markets to trade on elections.

"Risks and vulnerabilities didn't change overnight just because the markets were accurate this time around," she said.

Right before the midterms, prediction market forecasts had missed major global elections, including Brazil's presidential race, where Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva only had about 20% odds to win on Kalshi before polls closed.

Prediction markets also whiffed in some high-profile Democratic primaries over the summer, forecasting extremely high odds for progressives in Wisconsin and Michigan, for example. The Michigan Democrat, Abdul El-Sayed, narrowly won his primary before going on to win Thursday's general election.

Academics who've researched prediction markets for decades say the platforms aren't wrong when candidates with low odds on markets end up winning races.

"If you toss two coins, there's a 25% chance that you'll throw two heads in a row, and nobody would be terribly surprised if you did," said Rajiv Sethi, an economics professor at Barnard College.

Despite their early success on Tuesday, Dartmouth College economic professor Eric Zitzewitz said it's still not clear if casual observers understand how market probabilities work.

"Some people who are coming to [prediction markets] for the first time will see 55 by 45 odds and think that, 'Oh the candidate with 55 is way ahead,'" he said.

As prediction markets gain traction, however, the public will improve at understanding how to interpret their odds, Zitzewitz said.

With another election under their belts, prediction markets will remain a force through the next presidential election, experts said.

"Last time around [in 2024], prediction markets were in the discussion, but they were in the discussion in a way that was a little bit unsure," said Harry Crane, a professor of statistics at Rutgers University. "This [was] the first true prediction markets election."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2861. Every stock that moved because of the election

The blue wave across the country has rippled through the stock market too, driving healthcare and clean energy names higher and data center plays and defense companies tumbling.

The broader market soared after Democrats won control of both the House of Representatives and the Senate, and key gubernatorial races in Texas and Georgia. In Washington, the Democratic Congress will now serve as a counterweight to President Donald Trump's agenda, which has included tax cuts and policies that sent inflation, like tariffs and the Iran war.

Local and state elections will also have an impact on data center stocks, with construction limits passed in some states and a Democratic governor-elect in Texas who promised a moratorium.

"Democratic control could slow the push for deregulation in financials, energy and digital assets," Wells Fargo Investment Institute said in a note Wednesday. "We still believe both parties have an incentive to compromise on data center location and AI generally."

Here are some of the biggest movers as a result of the election outcome.

Nextpower jumped more than 11.2 % and Canadian Solar rallied 6.7%, while Plug Power gained 3.6%, First Solar climbed 4.6% and Sunrun ticked 1.3% higher. Nextera Energy added 4.9%. Investors are hoping that Democrats will push back against the Trump administration's rejection of clean energy policies.

Healthcare companies rallied on investor optimism over a slowdown or reversal in healthcare spending cuts, and the potential that enhanced subsidies will extended in Affordable Care Act plans.

Molina Healthcare and Centene gained 8.9% and 12.6%, respectively. HCA Healthcare soared 10.3% and Universal Health Services jumped 10.9%, while Tenet Healthcare rallied 9.6% and Elevance Health added 4.9%.

Elon Musk's SpaceX and Tesla shed 3.8% and 3.3%, respectively, after Democrats won the gubernatorial, Senate, and attorney general races in Texas, as well as wresting control of the Texas House of Representatives . Musk, CEO of both companies, spent at least $474 million backing Republican candidates in the midterms, including $224 million in the Lone Star State, where both Tesla and SpaceX are headquartered.

Defense companies could face headwinds if Democrats challenge military spending. Shares of RTX slipped 2.4%, Lockheed Martin tumbled 6.9%, Northrop Grumman sank 4.5% and Howmet Aerospace fell 4.3%. Palantir Technologies shed 2.1%.

The Trump administration has requested $1.5 trillion for national defense in fiscal 2027. It is also separately asking for an $87.6 billion supplemental appropriation from Congress for fiscal 2026, largely due to the Iran conflict.

Drone stocks also fell. Kratos Defense & Security Solutions dropped 12.3%, while AeroVironment lost 8.3% and Red Cat fell 10.4%. Ondas shed 7% and Unusual Machines tumbled 9%.

Names connected with data centers moved lower amid concern about the potential for restrictions or moratoriums being placed on AI infrastructure buildouts.

Neocloud provider CoreWeave, the poster child for fears over massive AI-related debt, slumped 9.9%. Peers Nebius and Iren dropped 6.7% and 11.2%, respectively.

AI infrastructure stocks also tumbled, with Comfort Systems losing 15.1% and Vistra sliding 13.8%. Micron Technology fell 7%.

That said, five of the Magnificent Seven rallied. Apple rose 5.3% and Microsoft jumped 6.7%, with Apple touching an all-time high. Alphabet gained 3.2% and Meta added 1.4%.

A consumer relief trade also took hold, sending housing, travel and consumer staples stocks higher. Affordability was a big theme in the election, with Americans feeling the pressure of high prices.

Housing stocks like Pulte, D.R. Horton and Lennar gained roughly 7.2%, 8.1% and 7.1%, respectively. Airlines also moved higher, with United Airlines rising 8% and American Airlines climbing 6.3%. Southwest Airlines jumped 6.8% and Delta Air Lines climbed 7.2%. Cruise operator Carnival increased 7.3%, while Royal Caribbean and Norwegian Cruise Line added 6.4% and 6.8%, respectively. Booking Holdings rallied 9.8%.

Consumer staple names also caught a bid. Dollar Tree soared 9.6%, Walmart gained 3.5% and Constellation Brands climbed 5.3%. Dollar General advanced 8.5%, while Conagra Brands increased 7%.

The GOP's wide losses also extended dragged down President Donald Trump's social media and tech company, which plunged 24.5% and hit a 52-week low.

— CNBC's Luke Fountain, Dominic Chu and Nicholas Wells contributed reporting.

#2860. As AI becomes more fractured, one name stands out: AMD

As Anthropic rushes to takes its shares public, the AI trade is showing cracks, and they are not confined to fringe players.

We recently wrote about CoreWeave, which fell 15.5% on Oct. 14 as yields on its 2032 notes touched 15%, and its default-swap spreads nearly doubled in October. IREN is down more than 20% this year. Last Thursday, the contagion reached chipmakers, which makes sense since much of CRWV and IREN's spending is going there. AMD dropped 8.1% as Meta's financing plans and a supply warning from Lam Research exacerbated data-center fears about the pace of AI spending.

Because when you screen for factors that demonstrably generate outperformance over time, the list narrows quickly. My screen: price above the 200-day moving average; revenue growing at least as fast as the S&P 500 (5%+ per annum); EPS growing at least as fast (7%+); rating upgrades; a PEG below 3; and ROE and ROIC of at least 12%. Of the names reporting in the next two weeks, AMD is effectively the only one that makes the list. Yes, you can (and arguably should, as our staples article described) position your portfolio with slightly less tech exposure than the broad indices provide, but we can't ignore the market's biggest and most transformative sector entirely.

Especially because the growth in this case doesn't just meet my criteria; it blows past them. Consensus for Q3 calls for EPS of $1.94 on revenue of $13.06 billion, versus $1.20 and $9.25 billion a year ago. That's YoY growth of nearly 62% and 41%, respectively. The recent drawdown came from a sector selloff, not anything AMD reported, but it shows that if some names in the space get hit, even the larger players are likely to feel it. On Oct. 6, Lisa Su said AMD would substantially increase supply in 2027 because AI orders keep outrunning capacity.

So how do you balance factors we know work for a fast-growing company against a backdrop of cracks in the AI trade? Options.

Owning the stock outright means absorbing a roughly 9% to 11% implied earnings-related move (day of). Earnings are about a week out, and over the past four quarters the smallest move over a similar window was more than 9%, and the largest move, after the company reported its Q1 26 results, was 38%! Of course, that kind of volatility means options aren't cheap, and that rich premium is also a reason not to simply buy calls.

A call spread risk reversal does both jobs: it reduces risk if the cracks widen, and the short options offset the elevated volatility we would otherwise pay for. The bottom chart shows that, by sacrificing about $1,700 of upside for $5,000 in outperformance during a severe downside move, and given how jittery this trade has been lately, this is a way to stay in it with considerably less risk. $5,000 less, specifically. Of course, selling that out will tie up some cash, but less so than simply buying the stock. And selling both the out and the call reduces long call purchase by 66%

#2859. UC Bearcats face another dangerous QB in Houston's Conner Weigman

The tour of dangerous Big 12 quarterbacks continues for the Cincinnati Bearcats as they visit No. 17 Houston on Saturday afternoon, Nov. 7, at TDECU Stadium.

The Bearcats have faced the scrambling abilities of Kansas State's Avery Johnson, the passing threat of Arizona's Noah Fifita, the unpredictability of West Virginia's Michael Hawkins Jr., Texas Tech's Will Hammond and Utah's dual-threat Devon Dampier. The Big 12 wins came against Kansas State with Johnson and Texas Tech with Hammond, while the escapability of Fifita, Hawkins and Dampier led to three Big 12 losses.

Next up is Houston's Conner Weigman, who was at Texas A&M two seasons ago. The 6-foot-3 native Texan is No. 14 in the nation in passing efficiency, ahead of Hawkins and Dampier, who gave UC fits. He is No. 8 in passing touchdowns with 20, more than Fifita and Dampier and seven more than UC's JC French IV. He's No. 20 in passing yards with 2,053, more than Dampier, who lit the Bearcats up for three passing touchdowns.

"Weigman does everything really, really good," UC Bearcats coach Scott Satterfield said. "He can make all the throws, he's sneaky fast, he can make safeties miss when he runs the football. They run the offense through him. He's the catalyst for their offense. They don't ever get themselves out of position."

Houston leads the nation in third-down efficiency at 60%, compared to UC at No. 33 at 44.6%.

It's all even, 15-15, but the Bearcats have won five straight. UC shut the Cougars out 34-0 in 2024, the first year for current coach Willie Fritz. The game before that in 2023 was in Houston, where the Bearcats recorded their first Big 12 win, 24-14 against the Dana Holgorsen-coached Cougars.

Houston's last win came at Nippert Stadium in 2016 as the Cougars rolled Tommy Tuberville's last UC team, 40-16.

Stream Cincinnati Bearcats vs.Houston Cougars

Why Cincinnati Bearcats have an advantage: It's another weekend in 2026 college football where one week you're the hammer, the next you're the nail. Coaches can go from the hot seat to the short list of another job based on a win, or vice versa based on a loss.

"There's a lot of parity in college football," Satterfield said. "Most of these teams have a new roster. What's the chemistry like? And there's a lot of up and downs throughout the season. Who's injured? Who's playing? Who's not playing? Where are you playing? Everybody's playing nine conference games now. It makes it exciting for the fans. This parity is what everybody wants. This year, in all these games, everybody's got a chance to win."

Should the Bearcats get center Taran Tyo and nose tackle Josh Hough back from injury, it would be a shot in the arm. Cole Tabb has run effectively, with three straight 100-plus-yard games; if Gi'Bran Payne can add to that and get UC over the 200-yard mark, it may help slow the Houston offense. Utah has been the only team to surpass 200 yards rushing on the Cougars, gaining 226 on Oct. 24. Outside of that, Texas Tech had 168 on the ground and those are Houston's only two losses.

Why Houston has an advantage: After losing at Utah 21-13 on Oct. 24, the Cougars had a bye week to heal up and scout the Bearcats. Plus, they're home. TDECU Stadium is not as intimidating a venue as others, but everyone loves a winner and Willie Fritz's men have only lost to Texas Tech and Utah, both ranked teams.

Like Cincinnati, Houston has NFL and MLB teams competing with it for fans. They also have the NBA. Arizona State and Colorado are in similar predicaments. Most of the Big 12 schools are primarily in college towns.

Houston averages 39,199 fans this season, about 7,000 more than last season and about 12,000 more than 2024. In UC's last visit in 2023, the Bearcats prevailed in front of 34,312.

It's also November and the Bearcats in the Big 12 are 1-11 in the 11th month of the year. However, the lone November win did come in UC's inaugural Big 12 season in Houston.

"Every year's different; we've got a completely different team," Satterfield said. "Not looking at exterior things and focusing on Houston is what we have to do. Then, you have to go out and play. Last year we played really good teams in November."

Based on this week's rankings, UC still has two ranked teams to play: at No. 17 Houston and at No. 7 BYU on Nov. 28. Iowa State won't be a picnic on the road Nov. 14, and there's a home date Nov. 21 vs. Colorado.

The Bearcats played without nose tackle Josh Hough and center Taran Tyo vs. Utah. Early indications are that both should be available for the Houston game.

Houston quarterback Conner Weigman left Rice-Eccles Stadium in Utah in a walking boot after their Oct. 24 loss. The Cougars were idle and based on Willie Fritz's press remarks Nov. 3, he's back up to speed.

The Big 12 announced Nov. 2 that the UC game at Ames vs. Iowa State is in a six-day window for TV. Kickoff should be known by Sunday, Nov. 8.

#2858. Jordan Love and wife dress as blind refs for Halloween

Jordan Love and Ronika Stone had jokes this spooky season.

The Packers quarterback and his wife dressed up as blind referees for Halloween, donning the classic black-and-white striped tops with sunglasses and canes. In the video posted to Stone's TikTok account, the pair bumped into each other and threw a penalty flag.

Stone captioned the video, "I'm jokin, immmmmm jokin."

Jordan Love of the Green Bay Packers reacts after a touchdown during a game against the Carolina Panthers in Green Bay, Wis., Thursday, Oct. 29, 2026. Getty Images

Fans seemed to enjoy the couple's trolling, while others were concerned that Love would be fined for his choice of costume.

Love's wife joked in the comments, "We're Footlocker employees."

Jordan Love and his wife dress as blind referees for Halloween. Credit: TikTok

The costumes are seemingly a callback to the Packers' troubles with NFL referees thus far this season, especially in a highly controversial 20-17 loss to the Lions in Week 7.

Love, who has been the most hit quarterback in the NFL through Week 8, has not gotten a single roughing the passer call in his favor this season.

It hasn't stopped Love from being one of the most productive quarterbacks in the NFL, though.

Love leads the league with 2,246 passing yards and is fourth with 16 touchdown passes.

Jordan Love and his wife wear blind referee costumes for Halloween. Credit: TikTok

His teammate, safety Xavier McKinney, has called out the refs directly.

"I don't know how the NFL's going to treat it, but I'm not a big fan of the refs right now," McKinney admitted. "I don't think it's great officiating, if I'm being completely honest. I think it's bulls—t a lot of the times."

Jordan Love of the Green Bay Packers stands with Ronika Stone before a game against the Philadelphia Eagles in Green Bay, Wis., Monday, Nov. 10, 2025. Getty Images

Love and Stone got married in June of 2025 after dating for five years. They welcomed their first child, a daughter, this past May. Stone's father, Ron, was an NFL guard for nine seasons.

Love and the Packers head to New England to face the Patriots on Sunday.

#2857. Lakers forward ‘trending in right direction’ after injury setback

SACRAMENTO — The Lakers took a step toward becoming a fully healthy team on Wednesday before their road game against the Kings at Golden 1 Center.

The team announced that Adou Thiero was assigned to the franchise's G League affiliate, the Coachella Valley Lakers.

Adou Thiero's size and athleticism can be game-changers for the Lakers. Getty Images

The second-year forward presumably practiced with the G League team, with Lakers coach JJ Redick saying earlier in the week that the hope is he'll be available to make his season debut later this week.

"Practice was fine [on Wednesday], went well," Redick said. "That was the plan just to get him a live practice today and then he'll be back with us."

The Lakers host the Trail Blazers on Friday before kicking off a road trip against the Pistons on Sunday. The matchup in Detroit is the first night of a back-to-back set, with the Lakers playing the Hawks in Atlanta on Monday.

Adou Thiero appears to be close to making his season debut. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

"He's had some live exposures," Redick said of Thiero, "and I think he's trending in the right direction."

The 6-foot-8 forward had been sidelined since the preseason because of left knee swelling, with the team shutting him down from on-court activities for a couple of days before ramping him back up.

He played in the team's first three preseason exhibitions.

The swelling was happening in the same knee that he had surgery on during the spring of 2025. He was also sidelined during training camp, preseason and the first 13 games of the regular season while dealing with left knee swelling.

But it appears that Thiero is inching closer to being available for the first time in 2026-27.

He was the only player listed on the injury report ahead of the Lakers' matchup against the Kings.

Adou Thiero has been sidelined because of his left knee multiple times since entering the NBA last year. NBAE via Getty Images

#2854. What's next for Rams after season-ending injury to Jaylen Watson?

Just when it looked like the Los Angeles Rams were returning to full strength, the injury bug bites again.

Cornerback Jaylen Watson needs shoulder surgery and will miss the rest of the season, head coach Sean McVay told reporters on Wednesday. Watson exited Sunday's Week 8 win over the Los Angeles Chargers early after re-aggravating the same shoulder originally dislocated in Week 3. He missed the following week but has played every game since, and McVay said on Monday they'd be evaluating the fifth-year DB on a day-to-day basis.

McVay said Wednesday that Watson had hoped to continue to play through the injury with a brace, but the team ultimately decided to shut him down after they were told by doctors "the likelihood of it coming out and doing further damage to it is a very high probability."

"He's been awesome," McVay said. "He's been playing through some different stuff, but he's been really productive. And it's unfortunate that that happened, but I know he wants to be able to play, but it's the right thing to do for him. And that's the tough part about this."

Watson signed a three-year, $51 million contract with the Rams in March shortly after the team traded his former Kansas City Chiefs teammate Trent McDuffie in a blockbuster trade and signing him to a four-year, $124 million extension. The secondary tandem won two Super Bowls as members of the Chiefs and their continued pairing in LA, along with the later acquisition of Myles Garrett, immediately pushed the Rams into offseason favorites for Super Bowl 61, which will be played at SoFi Stadium.

All three have dealt with injuries that have limited their time together on the field. Garrett underwent arthroscopic surgery on his left knee after Week 1 and has been on injured reserve, though he returned to practice this week as a limited participant. McDuffie returned in Week 8 after missing the previous two weeks with a groin injury.

Watson has been a bright spot on the Rams defense, even with playing through pain. The Washington State product has an interception, nine passes defended and 24 tackles in seven games played this season. He has the seventh-highest grade among NFL cornerbacks (80.7), according to Pro Football Focus.

"He's been everything and that much more that we had hoped," McVay said.

Ricky Pearsall, SF (knee) - Out for seasonRicky Pearsall of the San Francisco 49ers works out during training camp practice at SAP Performance Facility on July 27, 2026, in Santa Clara, California.

Chris Brazzell, CAR (knee) - Out for seasonChris Brazzell II of the Carolina Panthers warms up during the Carolina Panthers Training Camp at Carolina Panthers Practice Fields on July 23, 2026, in Charlotte, North Carolina.

Jayden Higgins, HOU (knee) - Out for seasonJayden Higgins of the Houston Texans catches a pass for a touchdown during the second quarter of the game against the Indianapolis Colts at NRG Stadium on January 04, 2026, in Houston, Texas.

Josh Wallace has played well in the absences of Watson and McDuffie, and his number will continue to be called upon by defensive coordinator Chris Shula through the rest of 2026. In his third season out of UMass and Michigan, Wallace has an interception, five passes defended, 29 tackles and a tackle for loss. His 77.3 PFF rating ranks ninth among active cornerbacks.

Emmanuel Forbes Jr. also will be expected to get more reps.

#2853. Ripple strikes deal with Robinhood to expand its RLUSD digital dollar

Ripple has struck a deal with Robinhood to expand the reach of its digital dollar, RLUSD, strengthening trading and financing links between their institutional businesses and improving Robinhood's internal money management.

The partnership involves using RLUSD to settle institutional trades, automate Robinhood's treasury operations and expand access to digital asset trading and financing through Ripple Prime and Bitstamp, the global crypto exchange acquired by Robinhood last year.

It also helps meet Robinhood's ambition of becoming a global financial superapp, centralizing its customers' financial lives while expanding the underlying infrastructure that can serve both retail and institutional clients.

"Utility is what moves stablecoins forward. Working with Ripple, Bitstamp and Robinhood will have access to RLUSD for institutional settlement, Ripple Treasury for our operations and Ripple Prime for financing," Robinhood Crypto General Manager Johann Kerbrat said in a statement shared with CNBC. "That is what a connected, global financial system looks like."

Stablecoins have long been a workhorse of the crypto world, giving traders a way to move money among exchanges, buy and sell crypto assets, and park funds between trades without having to convert back into traditional currency. But as the crypto industry evolves beyond trading and markets move toward 24/7 liquidity, stablecoins are becoming essential financial infrastructure because of their ability to enable near-instant settlement and move dollar-denominated value at any time, regardless of traditional banking hours.

As firms look to do more with stablecoins beyond simply listing them on exchanges, stablecoin issuers are betting they'll favor a one-stop-shop approach that bundles the technology and services needed to move money, settle trades and manage cash.

"Stablecoins have evolved from a crypto trading tool into a critical piece of financial infrastructure," Ripple President Monica Long told CNBC. "The clearest evidence of this is where they're now being embedded — from corporate payments to capital markets. Our partnership with Robinhood reflects that evolution."

"This is about much more than trading," she added. "It's about fundamentally changing how money moves and is managed — giving institutions the ability to operate 24/7/365, unlocking liquidity and putting capital to work more efficiently."

Circle Internet Group, a Ripple competitor, is positioning itself for a similar future, where winning platforms, in addition to issuing stablecoins, also provide the infrastructure to put them to work. Earlier this year, for example, it launched the Circle Payments Network, connecting financial institutions and payment providers using its flagship USDC stablecoin and other digital assets for cross-border payments.

The network is Circle's answer to the 8-year-old Ripple Payments business, a service that helps businesses move money across borders, using any of several assets moving through that system, including USDC, Ripple-created XRP and, as of its debut in December 2024, RLUSD.

RLUSD's market cap has grown to more than $2.5 billion. Tether's USDT and Circle's USDC still dominate the total stablecoin market, which now exceeds $300 billion.

RLUSD will also be listed on Robinhood's trading platform, and is currently available to traders on Bitstamp as well as Robinhood Chain, the company's home-grown blockchain for tokenized stocks and other assets.

Robinhood, which reports third-quarter results for the three months ended in September after the market closes, rose about 2.5% in early trading Tuesday, snapping a three-day decline.

Correction: A previous headline misspelled RLUSD.

#2852. Wizards vs. Hawks Discussion

The Washington Wizards can match their best start in five seasons with a win over the Atlanta Hawks on Wednesday at Capital One Arena.

Washington has won two straight games behind stout defense, especially in the second half, and impressive offensive showings from Anthony Davis, Trae Young and Kyshawn George.

A bulk of that scoring has come at the rim behind a revamped frontcourt and slashing wings. Look for Washington to attack the rim against Atlanta's 27th-ranked interior defense that allows 53.8 paint points per game.

#2851. Crude oil prices fall below $90 a barrel as Vance blames GOP election defeat on Iran war

Crude oil prices tumbled Wednesday after Vice President JD Vance blamed the war against Iran for Republican midterm election reversals.

U.S. West Texas Intermediate lost 8.3% to close at $88.12 a barrel. Brent crude futures, the international benchmark, shed 8.7% to settle at $94.62.

"The reason we lost is the war," Vance said in a social media post Wednesday. "The president himself said so last night."

Democrats swept the House and Senate in Tuesday's elections, giving the party leverage to pressure President Donald Trump to end the unpopular war that has spiked gasoline and diesel prices.

"The elections are leading the market to conclude that Trump might now be inclined to make a deal," said Andy Lipow, president of Lipow Oil Associates.

The Democrats may try to restrict funding for the war, Lipow said. Trump's announcement earlier this week that the U.S. will cut some funding to Israel could force Jerusalem to accept Washington's decisions, he said.

Trump told the Financial Times on Tuesday that he has no immediate plans to strike Iran. The oil market had priced in the risk of a major escalation after the elections, gaining more than 10% in October.

Oil futures have fallen more than 14% this week as senior U.S. and Iranian officials indicated they're open to a diplomatic settlement of the conflict, which has disrupted global energy supplies for eight months.

The sell-off "could be getting a bit overdone," said Rebecca Babin, senior energy trader at CIBC. The market is in a "shoot first, ask questions" later mode as emotions run high after the unexpected election results, Babin said.

Many observers expected a Democrat tsunami could push Trump to escalate with Iran, Babin said. "Vance's commentary and the public lack of backing from key allies have challenged that assumption," she said.

But the odds still favor military escalation in the region, warned Bob McNally, president of Rapidan Energy and a former advisor to President George W. Bush.

"Iran is hardened in its uncompromising position," McNally said. "Trump, having now paid a huge price for his determination to prevent Iran from obtaining a nuclear weapon, is unlikely to let go of this legacy goal and walk away."

#2850. Notre Dame commit Roman Igwebuike honored as a 2027 Navy All-American

Linebacker Roman Igwebuike (Chicago, Illinois/ Mount Carmel High School) was officially honored as a 2027 Navy All-American during a virtual jersey presentation as part of the Road to the Dome tour.

Igwebuike will play in the annual East vs. West matchup inside the Alamodome in San Antonio, Texas at 1 p.m. ET on Saturday, January 9, 2027, live on NBC and Peacock, which marks the twenty-seventh anniversary of the event.

The nationwide Road to the Dome tour will honor 100 Navy All-Americans with live and virtual jersey presentations. The series will be released weekly through social platforms across NBC Sports.

Igwebuike was selected by the Navy All-American Bowl Selection Committee, comprised of the All-American Bowl, Andrew Ivins (247Sports), Brandon Huffman (Rivals), and NXGN. Navy All-Americans are eligible for the Navy All-American Bowl Player of the Year Award, Anthony Muñoz Lineman of the Year Award, Navy All-American Bowl Defensive Player of the Year Award, Navy All-American Bowl Man of the Year, and Navy All-American Bowl Game MVP Award.

As an NBC Sports-owned property, the All-American Bowl is part of a marquee lineup of elite events that includes the Olympics and Paralympics, the NBA, WNBA, Major League Baseball, Notre Dame Football, the Premier League, and primetime's #1 show for an unprecedented 15 consecutive years: Sunday Night Football. The All-American Bowl is annually the most-watched, most-talked about, and most-prestigious high school all-star event with more than four million unique television viewers and more than 25,000 fans in attendance. The history and tradition of the All-American Bowl is unparalleled, as it features: 665 draft picks; 110 Super Bowl champions; 300 Pro Bowl selections; and 18 Heisman finalists. #2849. Democrats map out an agenda to address affordability and corruption after midterm sweep

The newly elected Democratic majorities in the Senate and the House are planning to pass a flurry of bills as soon as they take the reins next January, CNBC has learned, and are preparing to tussle with an antagonistic President Donald Trump.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

The legislative push aims to show a united Democratic caucus taking quick action on the issues they were elected to solve, especially oppressively high costs on everything from gas to groceries.

In their first 100 hours after being sworn in, House Democrats plan to bring to the floor bills reinstating the enhanced health insurance subsidies that expired under the GOP-held Congress, and reversing the Medicaid cuts Trump signed into law last year.

Democrats — likely led by Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries (D-N.Y.), who is poised to become the first Black Speaker of the House in U.S. history — also plan to push forward a bill that would undo Trump's sweeping tariffs and reassert Congress' role in setting import taxes. And they will again vote on legislation to end the Iran war or force Trump to seek congressional authorization to keep it going. Some details of the Democrats' plans had been previously reported by the New York Times.

But Democrats will need to strike a balance between these legislative priorities and a robust oversight and investigatory agenda that the party is mapping out to scrutinize Trump, his family, cabinet and supporters in the business world.

"We haven't evaluated who are going to be the targets of the investigation right out of the gate. We'll have those conversations over the next couple of weeks," Jeffries told CNBC on Wednesday.

"But clearly, things aren't going right for the American people. Capitalism is broken in the way in which it is being experienced by so many working-class Americans and middle-class Americans. We've got to make sure we build an economy, a free market economy, that works for everyday Americans," Jeffries said.

Democrats on the campaign trail for months hammered home a message of affordability. And as corruption emerged as a top issue for voters in the waning months of the election, they tried to connect the Trump administration's perceived bad behavior to Americans' pocketbooks.

Already, prominent Democrats are taking a similar tack as they prepare to assume majorities in both chambers.

Sen. Ron Wyden of Oregon, the top Democrat on the Senate Finance Committee, said in a statement on Wednesday that the panel's top priorities "will be affordability for us, accountability for them."

Wyden said the committee would double down on its efforts to make sure billionaires pay their fair share in taxes, address the "corporate chokehold on our nation's health care system" and reassert congressional authority over trade. He also vowed to target the "special interests" that had cozied up to Trump in the past two years.

"From the banks that helped cover up Epstein's crimes, to insurance companies that are delaying and denying health care to churn a profit, to the pharmaceutical company CEOs that price gouge while enjoying Trump's hand-delivered windfalls, when I am chair, they will be forced to answer to the Senate Finance Committee for their role in hurting the American people and enabling Trump's corruption," Wyden said.

Rep. Robert Garcia of California, the top-ranking Democrat on the House Oversight Committee, has vowed to take on Trump's family, corporate corruption, Department of Homeland Security and Immigration and Customs Enforcement overreach and the handling of files related to disgraced sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

Garcia issued a pointed, two-word statement as results tipped toward Democrats on Tuesday night: "Lawyer up."

And Rep. Pramila Jayapal, D-Wash., a former chair of the Congressional Progressive Caucus and member of the House Judiciary and Foreign Affairs committees, said in a recent interview with CNBC that she believed Democrats should target figures like Elon Musk, as well as cabinet secretaries like Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, Secretary of Health and Human Services Robert F. Kennedy Jr., and Attorney General Todd Blanche.

But she acknowledged the challenges that could come with pursuing a large number of potential targets all at once, especially with a potentially unwieldy and ideologically diverse Democratic caucus.

"I think what we should do is pick one or two cases that really exemplify the worst of the corruption, and that we feel we have the most evidence of wrongdoing," Jayapal said. "There are so many things — I think it's important to focus on a couple."

Meanwhile, other Democrats are angling for some kind of ethics reform package that could include prohibitions on stock trading and use of prediction markets for government officials, a federal ban on gerrymandering and limiting super PAC spending.

"My expectation is that when Democrats are the majority, we will pass a strong bill to ban stock trading in Congress, in the executive branch, and beyond, and that this will be part of a larger package of ethics reforms that we pursue," said Rep. Seth Magaziner, D-R.I., a longtime advocate for such legislation.

#2848. Luka Doncic’s underrated superpower making giant difference for Lakers

Luka Dončić's scoring is dominating headlines.

But a huge reason the Lakers are soaring right now is his passing.

He had 13 assists in Monday's 138-117 win over the Heat as the team finished with 39 assists, the most they've had in any game since Lakers coach JJ Redick took over the helm in June 2024.

When the ball is moving like that, the Lakers are very difficult to defend.

Doncic's passing is an underrated part of his game, overshadowed by his glitzier scoring deluges that drop jaws. But his passing is just as important. It's what makes his teams dangerous.

He reads defenses in a fraction of a second as though he were doing advanced calculus through throngs of hands trying to obscure his vision.

Los Angeles Lakers guard Luka Doncic, right, is defended by Miami Heat forward Andrew Wiggins during an NBA game Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, in Los Angeles. AP Photo/Jayne Kamin-Oncea

In many ways, that's his real super power.

"He can make plays, every play in the book that you need to make as a point-of-attack guy," Heat coach Erik Spoelstra said after the Lakers' 138-117 win on Monday.

"You have to pick him up 30 feet. He can make every pass in the book. He can read your defense. He understands what we're trying to do, positions guys in the right spots and he makes the right play over and over and over. That's what the greats do."

With Doncic regularly drawing double- and triple-teams, Redick has made decisiveness a major point of emphasis for the Lakers this season.

"It's the thing we talked about before the game: just shoot, drive, kick it," Redick said. "Just a lot of 0.5-[second] decision making."

The Lakers executed against the Heat, making a defense laden with superstars such as Giannis Antetokounmpo and Bam Adebayo look discombobulated and hapless as they scrambled to slow down Doncic while also stopping leaks.

Sure, Doncic scored an eye-popping 42 points, his fourth 40-point performance in his last five games.

But what really made the Lakers unstoppable?

As a team, they shot 57.3% from the field and 44.2% from beyond the arc. From top to bottom, everyone was starring in their roles.

The Heat's blitzes on Doncic led to him dishing the ball out to Sandro Mamukelashvili and Quentin Grimes for wide open 3s.

Walker Kessler also capitalized on those moments, finishing with his third straight double-double in a 15-point and 15-rebound performance against the Heat.

Antetokounmpo called Doncic "one of the best scorers to ever play this game," but added what Doncic is doing behind the scenes is truly what's making the Lakers elite.

"You can see the message, after every double team he was telling them, like, 'Hey, be aggressive, get downhill,'" Antetokounmpo said. "That fills players up with confidence. You see Sando was aggressive, Kessler was aggressive, Thybulle was aggressive, Grimes — all of them."

Luka Doncic (77) of the Los Angeles Lakers dribbles during a game against the Miami Heat on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

Doncic is leading the league in scoring with 38.3 points per game, but he's also third in assists (8.1).

When Doncic is taking advantage of the attention he draws and his teammates are starring in their roles, the Lakers are hard to beat. They currently have the best offensive rating in the league (121.9).

For a Lakers team with nine new players, including Kessler, it has been a learning process. Let's not forget the Lakers blowing a 25-point lead against the Warriors on Oct. 30 when Golden State dialed up its defensive pressure.

Playing with a guy like Doncic requires unique skills.

Luka Doncic #77 of the Los Angeles Lakers plays defense on Giannis Antetokounmpo #7 of the Miami Heat on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, at Crypto.Com Arena in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

His teammates must always be ready. They have to react instantaneously. They have to be fearless.

And if his teammates make the right reactions, defenses are stunted.

"I think at the beginning of the season, we didn't execute well on double teams," Doncic said. "But I just thought [Monday], I think we improved a lot. Everybody was in the right spots. So it's not just me, I think it's everybody, just being in the right spot, being aggressive and shooting with confidence."

Eight games into the season, it appears guys are adjusting well to Luka Magic.

That makes the Lakers one of the most dangerous teams in the league.

#2847. Seahawks sign former UFL MVP quarterback following Sam Darnold injury

The Seattle Seahawks have a banged up quarterback room, and unlike the last time Sam Darnold was injured, they are adding another player to the roster.

Prior to the start of Wednesday's practice, the Seahawks signed former Nebraska and Kansas State quarterback Adrian Martinez, who was most recently with the San Francisco 49ers. This comes after Darnold was ruled out of Week 9 against the Arizona Cardinals due to a quad injury, in addition to a cracked rib suffered in Monday's loss to the Chicago Bears. Drew Lock is slated to start, but he's dealing with a right elbow injury that caused him to miss the last two games, and therefore Martinez joins the practice squad as some insurance.

Martinez's career highlight at the professional level was not in the NFL, but the UFL. As starting quarterback of the Birmingham Stallions, Martinez compiled a 6-1 record as a starter, throwing 15 touchdown passes and only 3 interceptions, while also rushing for over 500 yards and 3 more TDs. His performances earned him league MVP honors. In the 2024 UFL Championship against the San Antonio Brahmas, Martinez was named game MVP after throwing a touchdown to former Seahawks draft pick Gary Jennings Jr, while adding a couple of scores on the ground in a 25-0 victory.

If Lock is still limited due to his elbow injury, it's more likely than not that Martinez will be a game day elevation against the Cardinals this Sunday.

Seattle also brought Jamie Sheriff back to the practice squad after his brief stint with the Los Angeles Rams. Wide receiver Malick Meiga and linebacker Jimmy Ciarlo were released to make room for Sheriff and Martinez.

#2846. Pleasant volleyball dominates again in OHSAA regional with Centerburg

ONTARIO — The Spartans continue to stick to the script.

For the 26th time in 26 matches in 2026, Pleasant's volleyball team won a 3-0 match, this time beating Centerburg 25-12, 25-10, 25-16 during a Division V regional semifinal Nov. 4 in the O-rena at Ontario High School.

Pleasant, ranked No. 7 in the state coaches poll for D-V, will face top-ranked Coldwater at 2 p.m. Saturday, Nov. 7 back in the O-rena. The Cavaliers (22-4) also posted a 3-0 win, topping No. 3 Huron (22-4) by scores of 25-16, 29-27, 25-13.

"They just keep gaining the momentum and the confidence, and I think they want it so badly that they aren't even willing to let a point go. I love it," Pleasant coach Kory Ute said of her team.

In a season of dominance, it might have been the most dialed-in performance of the season by the Spartans (26-0).

"This is definitely the best we've played skill-wise. We had so much energy. We were very honed in in what we were doing," Pleasant senior defensive specialist Siri Veladanda said. "We all know this is the time of season where you can't afford to make small mistakes. This is when you need to hone those skills. We knew that coming into this game."

Back at the O-rena, site of their closest set of the season when the Warriors scored 22 points in a 25-22 third-set loss, Pleasant didn't leave anything to chance. The longest run they've allowed this season was five points at Ontario, but against Centerburg (23-3), they only allowed the Trojans to peel off a 3-point run.

And with the exception of 1-0 to start the second set, the Spartans never trailed Wednesday night.

"It was the first thing we said when we were watching film. We've got to make sure we establish what we can do and do it quickly so they know," Ute said. "I don't want another team having any hope. I don't want them thinking they are going to be part of the game."

State cross country preview: Marion County sending four runners to OHSAA state cross country

More on Pleasant volleyball: Pleasant volleyball's Kam Cocherl newest member of the Fahey Four club

In the first set, Pleasant went out to an 8-2 lead, and the Trojans called a timeout while trailing 12-5. They couldn't stop the momentum as the Spartans pushed the lead to 19-8 and 23-11 before winning 25-12.

The second set was more of the same as Pleasant built leads of 6-1, 11-4 and 17-8 before closing with an 8-2 run.

For the third set, Pleasant again rushed out to leads of 5-1, 10-4 and 12-5, but three times Centerburg — champions of the Knox Morrow Athletic Conference — cut it to three, the last being at 16-13. However, the Spartans finished it with a 9-2 run and advanced to their third straight regional championship match.

Putting together quick starts for each set was a point of emphasis for Ute.

"They have taken that seriously the last three matches. We're trying to hop on them quick, so we can make sure we can stick a fork in them," she said.

Now they will face Coldwater, the team that beat them for the Division VI state championship in 2024. Pleasant is trying to return to a third straight Final Four in volleyball.

"It's going to be not a lot of sleep and a lot of studying," Ute said

#2845. Democrats take Congress in a blue wave. Here's what it means for your money

With Democrats flipping control of Congress in the midterm election, the shift in power on Capitol Hill could reshape household budgets — from student loan bills and health insurance premiums to housing costs and taxes.

Experts say results show that rising costs were top of mind for voters.

"I see this election as a repudiation of the anti-affordability agenda from the Trump administration," said Daniel Bunn, president and CEO of the Tax Foundation, a nonpartisan, nonprofit research organization.

"Whether that's the conflict in the Middle East or the tariffs, just top to bottom, it's clear to me that was a real concern of the electorate," he said.

White House spokesman Matthew Middleton said in an emailed statement that the Trump administration has delivered "tangible wins to make life more affordable for American families." Officials "will continue to put hardworking American's first with or without support from Democrats," he wrote.

Democrats won control of both the House of Representatives and Senate, which could make it easier for the party to advance legislation. They can also use hearings and investigations to press federal agencies and companies to answer for policies and practices that impact consumers, and exert leverage over confirmation of President Donald Trump's nominees.

But turning campaign promises into changes people feel in their finances won't be easy. Trump can veto bills a Democrat-controlled Congress passes, limiting how much of their agenda could become law. Based on projections, Democrats will lack the numbers needed to override a presidential veto.

Any changes will also take time: Newly elected members of Congress won't take office until January.

Here are eight ways Democrats’ new congressional majority could affect your money.

"Not surprisingly, the Dems are going to be keyed in on affordability with respect to healthcare," said Zach Gaumer, vice president of health policy at the Bipartisan Policy Center, a nonpartisan think tank.

OBBBA made Trump's 2017 tax cuts permanent and added temporary tax breaks. These included new deductions for tip income, overtime earnings, seniors and auto loan interest, which will expire after 2028 without an extension.

Democrats may support extending these provisions. But as the Democrat-controlled Congress maps out its agenda, lawmakers will have to address the federal budget deficit. For fiscal year 2026, the budget deficit ballooned to nearly $2 trillion, the Treasury announced in October.

Newly elected Democratic senators James Talarico of Texas, Troy Jackson of Maine and Annie Andrews of South Carolina all campaigned on expanding the child tax credit. Earlier this year, Senate Democrats also floated ideas to expand the standard deduction and create income tax exemptions for lower-earning Americans — which are costly proposals, according to Bunn from the Tax Foundation.

"I've called these termites in the tax code," he said. "You cannot consistently narrow your base, especially for the income tax, and expect your spending priorities to be well-funded."

With Democrats in control of Congress, they are likely to hold hearings and subpoena Trump officials over the administration's student loan policies, said higher education expert Mark Kantrowitz.

One priority may be pressing the Education Department to fix the popular Public Service Loan Forgiveness program, which cancels the remaining federal student debt of eligible government and nonprofit workers after a decade. Over the summer, Trump officials reassessed repayment timelines, delaying some borrowers' forgiveness date by years.

Broad student loan forgiveness is unlikely: Trump could veto such a bill, and the Supreme Court blocked the Biden administration's plan. More attainable changes could include restoring tax-free treatment of forgiven student debt, raising the new borrowing caps and expanding repayment options — measures that may attract bipartisan support, Kantrowitz said.

Trump has also said he may support legislation capping federal student loan interest rates, a proposal that could give Democrats common ground in their push to ease borrowers’ costs.

Housing affordability was a key election issue, and Democrats will likely explore ways to deliver relief, such as introducing measures to help first-time homebuyers, according to an Oct. 30 analysis by Desjardins, a Canadian financial services company.

However, lawmakers generally have little direct impact on home prices, which are based on market conditions. Mortgage rates, too, are influenced by forces generally beyond congressional control. The yield on the 10-year Treasury, which remains higher than it was before the Iran war — due at least partly to concerns about inflation eroding the value of those bonds — influences mortgage rates.

"Jumpstarting the housing market relies on a calmer geopolitical environment and bond market in the short run," plus more housing supply and a "more dynamic labor market in the long run," said Jake Krimmel, senior economist at Realtor.com.

The bipartisan 21st Century ROAD to Housing Act, which automatically became law July 11 without the president's signature due to a constitutional provision, is "a model for what can pass in a divided Washington," Krimmel said. It includes measures to encourage housing construction, reduce regulatory barriers, expand financing options and limit purchases of single-family homes by large institutional investors.

The incoming class of senators will have to address Social Security insolvency.

The program's trustees project that the trust fund Social Security relies on to help pay retirement benefits, formally known as the Old-Age and Survivors Insurance Trust Fund, may be depleted in the fourth quarter of 2032. Without action from Congress before then, benefits could face an across-the-board cut of 22%.

A number of Democratic lawmakers and some Republicans have called for increased payroll taxes on high earners to help shore up Social Security.

A potential SECURE 3.0 bill — a follow-up to bipartisan retirement-savings laws passed in 2019 and 2022 — could help expand workers' access to workplace retirement plans like 401(k)s, for example, among a roster of other reforms, experts said.

"Trump promised to cap credit card rates at 10%," said Aaron Klein, a senior fellow in economic studies at the Brookings Institution, a think tank. "A Democratic Congress might be willing to do that."

Any such cap is likely to be closer to 36% than to the 10% cap Trump has sought, Jaret Seiberg, a financial services policy analyst at TD Cowen, an investment bank, wrote in a research note Wednesday. Lawmakers in the current Congress have proposed legislation with these thresholds.

A cap would likely be welcome news for low- and middle-income Americans, Klein said. They are more likely than wealthy households to use debt to finance their lives and to carry a credit card balance — and therefore owe interest on their charges, he said.

Cardholders might see other impacts of such a policy, such as lower credit limits and a clawback of certain card rewards, he said.

Democrats' takeover of Congress could stall efforts to put crypto-friendly regulations into federal law, but everyday investors shouldn't expect much to change immediately.

"Platforms will keep offering the products they offer today, and they still have friendly regulators at the SEC and CFTC who are approving an ever-wider range of crypto products," said Lee Reiners, a cryptocurrency regulation expert and lecturing fellow of economics at Duke University.

The election results could threaten the Clarity Act, which would give the Commodity Futures Trading Commission greater authority over cryptocurrency trading while reducing the Securities and Exchange Commission's role. The bill passed the House in July 2025 by a 294-134 vote, but failed to advance in the Senate in September on a 49-50 procedural vote. "CLARITY is dead," said Reiners, adding that he sees little chance of the bill passing during the lame-duck session before Democrats take control in January.

Any future legislation aimed at creating a regulatory framework for digital assets would likely need to address Democrats' concerns about investor protections and conflicts of interest involving Trump's crypto businesses, said Martin Mulyadi, an accounting professor at Shenandoah University.

The SEC and CFTC can also continue developing crypto-friendly rules without Congress. But those rules are easier for a future administration to reverse than legislation, said Marcel Miu, a certified financial planner and founder of Simplify Wealth Planning based in Austin, Texas. "I'd tell investors to size their crypto investments as if the rules could change repeatedly over the coming years," Miu said.

The Iran war has led oil prices — and those of gasoline, jet fuel, heating oil, diesel and other fuels refined from oil — to surge. That has trickled into higher prices across the U.S. economy, experts said.

Oil prices fell on Wednesday on hopes that the election outcome will push Trump to make a deal to end the war.

"Ending the war in Iran would be the biggest policy lever to reducing inflation," the Brookings Institution's Klein said.

A Democrat-led Congress could try to limit Trump's ability to continue the war, perhaps by cutting funding.

"Congress' biggest power is the power of the purse," Klein said. "Wars are expensive."

— CNBC's Lorie Konish and Stephanie Dhue contributed reporting.

#2843. AI debt fears ease ahead of Oracle, Alphabet and Amazon updates. Here's what to watch.

Fears over artificial intelligence debt have eased after a bout of volatility. But more tests for AI debt are coming.

Oracle holds its Investor Day on Wednesday. And hyperscalers Alphabet and Amazon.com are scheduled to report quarterly results after the market close on Wednesday and Thursday, respectively.

Credit markets are on edge over a flood of AI debt, though fears have eased up a little lately. Meta Platforms' plans for a $95 billion stock offering, announced on Monday, helped bring down its credit-default swap spreads. Those spreads had risen last week when the company reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter. Other CDS spreads had moved higher too, though they have since backed off.

Investors had expected Meta to bring a large bond deal to market shortly after its earnings announcement, said Richard Cheng, head of Nuveen's investment-grade corporate sector team. The fact that it wasn't announced came as a relief.

"Just the absence of the bond deal itself is a direct catalyst for AI-related spread tightening," he said.

A credit spread is the difference in the yield between the corporate bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. When spreads tighten, it means investors are becoming more confident about the bond issuer.

The market action over the last few days shows that investors are recognizing that "credit fears may have been overblown," said CNBC contributor Paul Meeks, head of technology research at Freedom Capital Markets.

"I expect that these better feelings, maybe not good but better, may continue as companies announce their quarters over the next few weeks."

Still, spreads on CDS remain wider over the past year due to a surge of debt issuance and free cash flows turning negative across the AI space. Meta's CDS spread, for instance, is still up 177% year to date, while Amazon's spread has gained 129%.

"It's not necessarily fundamentals," said CreditSights analyst Jordan Chalfin. "It's more, I'd say, 80% driven by technicals and sentiment."

Alphabet and Amazon pose the next big hurdle for tech bonds as they update their capex plans and free cash flow forecasts. Microsoft's earnings are also due Wednesday, but the company has yet to issue any bonds to pay for its AI spending.

Both Amazon and Alphabet reported negative free cash flow in the second quarter and analysts expect that trend to continue.

Analysts expect Alphabet to report free cash flow of -$2.3 billion while spending $55.2 billion on capital expenditures in the third quarter, according to FactSet consensus estimates. Amazon is expected to report -$9.9 billion in free cash flow against capex of $58.7 billion.

S&P Global Ratings estimated in an August report that the hyperscalers will have negative free operating cash flow in 2026 and 2027.

More critical will be their outlooks, Cheng says. "The singular focus on the investors' minds is really what the capex guidance trajectory looks like across the hyperscaler space."

Meeks believes elevated capex is a sign of strong AI demand. The hyperscalers have "decided that with all the evidence they have — and they have more than you and me — that this is worthwhile spending, and not just worthwhile; it's required," he said.

CreditSights' Chalfin doesn't expect the hyperscalers to announce major new bond issuance around their earnings reports. Oracle's updates may be more critical for credit markets. The company has issued $117 billion in long-term debt and is clinging to an investment-grade credit rating of BBB-, the lowest level of investment grade above junk, according to S&P.

"It's the most important IG tech credit, just given how it's a bit more stressed relative to the A-rated names," he said. "It's trading more like a high-yield credit, even though it's IG, and so there's just a lot of investor concern on that one."

One wrinkle to the AI debt story: balance sheets don't reveal the whole picture. The companies are using complicated off-balance sheet arrangements for data centers and chip financing, and they aren't fully transparent, S&P Global said in a September report.

"There are commitments they are entering into, for example, into data center leases or purchase commitments on chips that aren't necessarily treated as debt," Mark Habib, digital infrastructure sector lead at S&P Global Ratings, said on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Monday.

S&P is trying to provide the market with more visibility on those commitments "to give a fuller picture of what kinds of obligations they're actually entering into," he added.

Oracle aside, hyperscaler credit ratings are all comfortably above A levels — reflecting the fact that they have broadly diversified revenue streams, ample coverage for interest payments, and capacity to add leverage if they choose.

Nuveen's Cheng isn't concerned, pointing out that while there has been some credit repricing, there is no credit crisis.

"They continue to have exceptional credit profiles, strong cash generation liquidity," he said of the largest hyperscalers.

"The market is really not concerned about credit quality at this point," Cheng added. "It's really just the trajectory of the capex needs and the supply of debt to fund that. Both of those are pointing in one direction — and that's up."

Correction: This story has been revised to reflect that Oracle's investment-grade credit rating is BBB-, according to S&P. A previous version mischaracterized the company's credit rating.

#2842. Myles Garrett returns to Rams practice; return to play unclear

Rams star edge rusher Myles Garrett, who has been sidelined for seven games following knee surgery, returned to practice Wednesday.

Garrett, the reigning NFL defensive player of the year, has been on injured reserve since undergoing left knee surgery after the Rams' season-opening loss to the San Francisco 49ers in Australia.

Coach Sean McVay said the Rams have attempted to be "as diligent, as thorough" as possible before allowing Garrett to practice and opening the 21-day window for him to return to the roster.

McVay said Garrett would take half the amount of full reps on Wednesday and would be evaluated each day before determining when he would be activated to the roster for a game. Garrett went through individual drills with edge rushers during the portion of practice open to reporters.

The Rams play the Washington Commanders on Sunday in Landover, Md.

"A lot of it is going to be predicated on his communication and feedback to us," McVay said of when Garrett will play in a game for the first time, "and then ultimately being able to see that, hopefully the expectation is he's ready to rock this week."

Read more: Rams cornerback Jaylen Watson to miss rest of season after shoulder surgery

When Garrett returns to game action, it will mark the first time that he and defensive lineman Aaron Donald will play together.

The two stars have five NFL defensive-player-of-the-year awards and 236½ sacks between them.

Donald, sparked by the Rams' trade for Garrett in June, came out of a two-year retirement to rejoin a team that opened the season as the Super Bowl favorite. But Donald, a three-time defensive player of the year, did so on the eve of the opener and did not travel for the first game.

Garrett did not make a tackle against the 49ers. In six games, Donald has 12 tackles, three for losses, and two quarterback hits but no sacks.

Last season, Garrett amassed an NFL season-record 23 sacks for the Cleveland Browns.

#2841. NextDC shares surge 43% after Goodman, Digital Realty stake purchases

Shares of Australian data center operator NextDC surged more than 42% Thursday after Goodman Group and U.S.-listed data center operator Digital Realty moved to acquire stakes of up to 9.9% in the company.

In a regulatory filing on the same day, NextDC said the two companies conducted an aftermarket bookbuild Wednesday, seeking shares at A$9.50 apiece.

NextDC's board called the move "opportunistic," noting that it came two weeks after the company disclosed a power supply delay at its M3 Melbourne data center and revised its fiscal 2027 guidance.

The company said it was not aware of the outcome of the share purchase. Goodman and Digital Realty told investors they had no current intention of making a takeover bid, but reserved the right to do so.

NextDC's rally came against a backdrop of weakness in U.S. data center stocks overnight. Shares of CoreWeave fell about 9%, while Iren dropped roughly 11.2% on Wednesday.

The sell-off followed sweeping Democratic victories in the U.S. midterm elections, which raised expectations of tighter scrutiny and potential restrictions on data center development that could slow the AI infrastructure buildout.

#2840. Leave No Doubt: Pat Terrell, Lou Holtz, and the Play That Helped Change Notre Dame History

As Notre Dame prepares to face Miami once again, a look back at the 1988 classic through the eyes of the man who made one of its most unforgettable plays.

There are certain moments in Notre Dame football history that never seem to get old, no matter how many times we watch them.

Rocket Ismail's electrifying returns. Tony Rice weaving his way through opposing defenses. The final seconds ticking off the clock as Notre Dame celebrated another unforgettable victory.

If you were a Notre Dame fan in 1988, you probably remember exactly where you were when the Fighting Irish defeated the top-ranked Miami Hurricanes, 31–30, in what would forever be remembered as the Catholics vs. Convicts game.

It was October 15, 1988. Notre Dame Stadium was electric, the rivalry was intense, and the Irish were determined to prove they belonged among the nation's elite.

Thirty-eight years later, with Notre Dame preparing to host Miami on Saturday, November 7, I thought it would be fun to revisit that unforgettable afternoon through the eyes of one of the players who helped make it happen.

And thanks to a conversation I had with former Notre Dame defensive back Pat Terrell for my book Echoes From the End Zone – The Men We Became, I have a few stories to share.

Including one about why he didn't catch the football.

One of the things I've always loved about interviewing former Notre Dame players is discovering the stories behind the moments we remember.

We see the touchdowns, the interceptions, the celebrations, and the championships. But we don't always see the struggles, the disappointments, or the unexpected turns that helped shape those players.

Pat's journey was certainly no exception.

Coming to Notre Dame from Florida was an adjustment. He missed home, and the South Bend weather wasn't exactly what he was accustomed to.

But the bigger challenge came on the football field.

Pat arrived at Notre Dame with dreams of becoming a star wide receiver. In fact, he had some pretty lofty expectations for himself.

As he told me, "I just knew as soon as Tim Brown graduated that I would come in and win MY Heisman Trophy."

But football, much like life, doesn't always follow the plan we've carefully mapped out for ourselves.

Instead of becoming Notre Dame's next great wide receiver, Pat was moved to defensive back during his junior year.

For a young man who had envisioned catching touchdown passes and perhaps winning the Heisman Trophy, it was a difficult adjustment.

He suddenly had to learn a new position, compete for playing time, and prove himself all over again.

And he wasn't simply trying to earn a starting job at Notre Dame. He also hoped to play professional football someday.

As Pat explained, he didn't become a starting defensive back until the third game of his junior season.

One of the most memorable defensive plays in Notre Dame football history would come from a young man who had only recently become a starter at his position.

Sometimes the path you never expected turns out to be the one that takes you exactly where you're supposed to go.

To understand the significance of that afternoon, you have to remember what the rivalry between Notre Dame and Miami had become.

The Hurricanes were the defending national champions. They were talented, confident, and accustomed to winning.

Notre Dame, meanwhile, was looking to restore its place among college football's elite under head coach Lou Holtz.

The Irish had endured some painful losses to Miami in previous seasons, including the infamous 58–7 defeat in 1985.

Notre Dame had confidence. It had leadership. And it had a group of players who believed they could compete with anyone in the country.

Pat remembered the tension surrounding the game, including a pregame confrontation between players from the two teams.

He also remembered Coach Holtz's ability to prepare his players, both physically and emotionally, for the biggest moments.

And then there was defensive coordinator Barry Alvarez.

Pat credited Alvarez with helping him become the player he needed to be, particularly given how recently he had moved to the defensive side of the football.

The coaches knew Miami's tendencies. They studied the Hurricanes' offense. And they practiced the situations they expected to encounter.

That preparation would become especially important in the final moments of the game.

Notre Dame led Miami 31–24 late in the fourth quarter when the Hurricanes mounted one final drive.

Miami quarterback Steve Walsh connected with Andre Brown for a touchdown, cutting Notre Dame's lead to 31–30.

Rather than attempt an extra point and settle for a tie, Miami head coach Jimmy Johnson decided to go for two.

It was the kind of decision that can define a rivalry.

And suddenly, Pat Terrell found himself in the middle of one of the most important plays of Notre Dame's season.

Pat told me that the Irish had practiced defending that particular play several times.

He was lined up against Leonard Conley, a Miami player he knew from his high school days in Florida.

Two young men who had competed against one another back home were now facing each other on one of college football's biggest stages.

Pat was prepared for the play, but he was also quick to point out that he didn't make it alone.

George Williams applied pressure to Walsh, affecting the timing of the throw. Pat was in position, read the play, and knocked the pass away.

It was the defensive stop that preserved one of the most celebrated victories in Fighting Irish history.

But when I interviewed Pat, I had a question for him.

Actually, it wasn't entirely my question.

Tony Rice had suggested that I ask Pat why he merely knocked the ball down instead of intercepting it.

After all, wouldn't catching the ball have been even better?

"Tell Tony because I wanted to make it dramatic. ANYONE can catch the ball."

Of course, no discussion of Notre Dame's 1988 national championship season would be complete without talking about Lou Holtz.

Coach Holtz had a remarkable ability to motivate his players, challenge them to believe in themselves, and convince them that they were capable of accomplishing things they might never have imagined.

Pat remembered the famous pregame speech before the Miami game, including a particularly colorful remark directed at Jimmy Johnson.

Let's just say Coach Holtz made it very clear that his players were to take care of business on the football field, and he'd handle the opposing coach.

He described Holtz's speeches as consistently moving and motivational, but this particular one left him especially fired up and ready to play.

Holtz wasn't simply asking his players to compete with Miami.

That belief became part of the identity of the 1988 Fighting Irish.

It also speaks to something I've always admired about Coach Holtz's approach to football and life.

He understood that success wasn't just about talent.

It was about preparation, discipline, confidence, and believing in the people around you.

And perhaps most importantly, it was about leaving no doubt that you had given everything you had.

You might assume that Pat's favorite memory from Notre Dame would be knocking down that two-point conversion against Miami.

After all, it's the play that helped preserve one of the most famous victories in program history.

But when I asked him about his most memorable moment, his answer surprised me.

It was the night after Notre Dame won the national championship.

Pat remembered the joy of celebrating with his teammates and, especially, seeing his parents share in that moment.

He talked about realizing that he and his teammates had accomplished something extraordinary together, something that would connect them for the rest of their lives.

Because when all is said and done, the most meaningful moments in sports aren't always the ones that make the highlight reels.

Sometimes they're the moments spent with the people who helped you get there.

The parents who supported you. The coaches who believed in you. The teammates who pushed you. The friends who celebrated alongside you.

As we prepare for another Notre Dame–Miami matchup this Saturday, I've been thinking about the lessons that players like Pat Terrell carried with them from their time in South Bend.

Pat arrived with one dream, found himself on a completely different path, and ultimately became part of a national championship team.

He learned to adapt, to work hard, to trust his coaches, and to believe that he could make a difference.

That's one of the things I love most about Notre Dame football history.

The stories aren't just about wins and losses. They're about perseverance, leadership, friendship, and the people who help us become the best versions of ourselves.

Those values are also why I wanted to honor Coach Holtz through my Leave No Doubt T-shirts and hoodies.

The design is a tribute to Coach Holtz and the lasting impact he has made on generations of Notre Dame players, students, alumni, and fans.

I'm also proud that a portion of the proceeds from every Leave No Doubt shirt and hoodie sold benefits the Holtz's Heroes Foundation, which carries forward his commitment to making a positive difference in the lives of others.

You can see the shirts, hoodies, and the rest of my Notre Dame-inspired collection here.

As Coach Holtz often reminded us, the goal is to make a positive difference in the lives of others.

And I can't think of a better legacy than that.

So here's to Pat Terrell, the 1988 national champions, Coach Lou Holtz, and all the unforgettable moments that make Notre Dame football history so special.

And to the 2026 Fighting Irish as they prepare to face Miami once again:

Pat Terrell's recollections are drawn from my interview with him for Echoes From the End Zone. The memories and quotations are shared with gratitude for his willingness to tell his story.
