#2839. Five Plays: Stanford First Quarter

Louisville started the game with an unbelievable script, attacking the edges of Stanford's defense. They ran some option plays and a nice swing pass to Isaac Brown early on, looking to get their fast players into space against a Stanford defense that is not very athletic.

I really like that they also looked to take advantage of Stanford's aggressive man coverage on the outside. It was obvious that the plan was to get Tre Richardson the ball in this game, and this go route down the sideline was a great way to start. Lincoln Kienholz throws a nice ball here, but I would have loved to see him put this ball on more of a rope to lead Richardson down the field.

I'm fairly certain that this play is designed to go to Brody Foley across the middle of the field on a delayed crossing route. It looks to me that Lincoln Kienholz just doesn't make the throw even though it's open. They only need 3 yards on this play, so Kienholz should put this on Foley to pick up the short gain.

I think Kienholz sees the linebacker as he works to the left, but doesn't realize he is far enough away from Foley that he won't be able to make a play on the ball. I've pointed it out a lot this season, but he has to let his playmakers go to work for him. Foley is 6-6/260. I like his chances to get a few yards here.

Louisville continues to struggle in zone coverage as we see on this play. Louisville's linebackers were not asked to get downhill in run support in this game. It's a frustrating aspect of the defensive game plan, but it is how the defense is called. With them not being asked to support the run, it is more frustrating that they bite on this very basic play action.

The linebackers end up frozen in the middle of the field while Stanford's receivers run right past them into a giant void in the defense. This play is designed for this outcome, which should be acknowledged, but my issue is that the linebackers on this play are not in a position to make a play on any play that could be called. This is just too easy for the offense to get a first down.

This play worked out for the defense to get a third down stop, but Stanford converted on the next play. I was actually surprised Davis Warren didn't wait for a receiver further down the field to get open so he could get the first down. He had time to wait because he easily identified that Louisville would not be blitzing on this play, even though they had the linebackers showing blitz.

This play was easy to read for Warren because the Cards put themselves into an obvious numbers disadvantage if they actually blitzed on this play. There are two defenders to cover three receivers to the bottom of the screen, and when the Stanford receiver goes in motion, no one on the defense moves. It's a little thing, but this is the type of nuanced stuff that the defense should have nailed down by this point in the season.

I watched this play more times than I can remember, and I could not tell you what the defensive call was on this play. My only guess is that Kaleb Beasley is supposed to count the running back as a passing option here, which would mean that he should not blitz on this play.

So, the call would be that if the running back was to the left of the quarterback, Beasley should blitz, as there is only one "threat" to the top of the screen. However, if this were a slot receiver, he would drop into a zone because there are two receivers to that side. The back doesn't stay in to block, so there are two threats for the linebacker to account for. Warren completes an easy pass to the guy who is left uncovered. The zone coverage continues to be an issue.

#2837. This 'SNL' alum is a huge Bengals fan and thinks you're doing it wrong

Bengals mascot Who Dey dances before the preseason game between the Cincinnati Bengals and the Indianapolis Colts at Paycor Stadium on Aug. 22, 2024.

Cincinnati Bengals mascot Who Dey signs autographs during a Christmas shopping event in 2024.

Greater Cincinnati is filled with passionate football fans (even if they don't root for the Bengals), but one Cincinnati native takes football season very seriously.

True to her hometown, born-and-bred Cincinnatian Jasmine Pierce is a self-proclaimed "sports gal" and ride-or-die for the Bengals, especially the team's three core players: Joe Burrow, Tee Higgins and Ja'Marr Chase.

Pierce, the Los Angeles and (sometimes) New York-based writer and producer, who's worked on shows including "Saturday Night Live," "Big Mouth" and "The Tonight Show starring Jimmy Fallon," attributes much of her success on the school level to Cincinnati's School for Creative and Performing Arts, where she attended high school.

After that, she wanted to branch out and ended up at New York University. But no matter where she goes, her love for the Bengals and the Queen City remains. So much so that the last time she was in the Big Apple, she took the moment to talk about the Bengals on the highly popular internet show SubwayTakes.

Pierce joined SubwayTakes, the New York-based internet talk show hosted by comedian Kareem Rahma, on June 26, decked out in a custom Tee Higgins jersey-turned-corset (which she made).

During her appearance, she expressed her love for the Bengals and dished out some NFL hot takes, like how playing fantasy football makes you not truly loyal to your team.

"If even part of you is rooting for your team to win, but not quite too much, or for your team to do well, but for one player to not do well, or if you're rooting for your team to win but you care about the spread, then that's not loyalty, and it's disgusting to me."

When asked if she had a favorite between Chase and Higgins, Pierce stood her ground that she loves both of them but wants to push them to be better. (She also noted that Chad Johnson, aka Ochocinco, was one of her biggest crushes in high school.)

"Listen, I love Ja'Marr, so there's nothing about thinking one is necessarily better. I think they're equally good ... and I think that they are good motivating forces for each other."

And when it comes to her favorite player, it should come as no surprise that it's Joe Burrow, and it's not because of his looks (even if Pierce does think "he's hot").

"I love his mentality. I love the way he plays. I love the calmness. I love his attention to detail. I love his purposefulness. I love his focus and his drive. Like, there are so many things about him that I'm just like, if I could have built a perfect quarterback, I mean, I would have probably made him Black. But aside from that, everything else about him I think is just so ideal," she said.

While the internet show looks like it happens on the fly in New York's subway system. Pierce, who often returns to the Big Apple, decided to do the show while in town for the 2026 Roots Picnic.

Naturally, Pierce seized the moment to don her Bengals gear and make the segment mostly about her hometown team (and if you follow her on social media, you know she's passionate about them).

"I definitely packed a few options. I always probably have at least one Bengals thing with me just because it's so much of my wardrobe," she said, adding that she wasn't sure what outfit she'd wear but sporting Bengals "felt like the right thing to do."

Pierce, whose love for sports grew from being raised in the same home as her mother and all her grandparents, said she's spent the last few years learning how to sew specifically so she could upcycle her old Bengals jerseys.

She has about 30 Bengals jerseys now. "I have all these jerseys like Rudy Johnson and T.J. Houshmandzadeh and all these other players who I love, and I still want to celebrate," she said.

Women cementing their place in the NFL world, especially with fashion, is something Pierce thinks the league is "intentionally doing," since there's a bigger market for women in football and sports in general. This has been especially true thanks to Taylor Swift's emergence in the NFL world. According to research published by Quantumrun, the female audience has grown to 47% of the total U.S. fanbase.

"There are a lot more groups popping up, social media accounts popping up about women who are specifically obsessed with football. And that's been such a relief to me as a woman who often goes to sports bars and hears like, 'Oh, wow, you really know your stuff.' Like, yeah, I do!"

She also attributed her "abnormal obsession with football" to growing up in Cincinnati.

"Certainly other cities have football obsessions, but Cincinnati fans' loyalty, specifically, is unmatched. That's pretty much what my SubwayTake was about. I feel very strongly that we should be loyal and passionate toward our teams. But it was weird coming out and realizing that my fandom was so much more insane than other people's."

Pierce doesn't make the rules, but if it were up to her, there would be no negative talk when it came to the Cincinnati Bengals, especially from Queen City natives. Now, this doesn't mean the team can't be criticized or critiqued, but talking down on the team and players is not something a true fan would do, according to her.

"Suffer with them. Be there. We're there to support our team," she said. "Support involves being there when things are hard or things aren't going well. And then, like, have criticism, certainly. But it can be thoughtful, useful criticism."

Pierce attributes her crazy fanaticism to her grandfather, or "Papa," who was "super into the Reds" growing up. They rooted for the Bengals, but they couldn't always afford football tickets, so attending Reds games was the next best thing.

"He was the one that made me so strict and loyal. He's very adamant that the game's not over till it's over. We were never allowed to leave. And I think that is a pretty strong sentiment in Cincinnati in general," she said. "There are certainly some people, but you don't see the mass abandonment at the end of games in the same way that I see at other stadiums."

She might not reside in Cincinnati and hasn't in quite some time, but the city and its teams are always on her mind. It's a testament not only to the Bengals' fan base, but the city itself.

"I don't know any Bengals fans that stopped being Bengals fans," she said. "We might be mad. We might watch fewer games, but I don't know any Bengals fans that switch to other teams. I don't know any that just completely dissolved their loyalty.

"We're just mad and pouting in the corner until something gets better, or we're always there. And I think that's my favorite thing about Cincinnati and why I'm so loyal, because that loyalty is given back."

Now that Pierce lives in Los Angeles, she often frequents the city's Bengals bar, but it's not the same as attending a game in person. The 36-year-old has made it her life goal to see the Bengals play every NFL team in their own home stadium.

This article originally appeared on Cincinnati Enquirer: This 'SNL' alum is a huge Bengals fan and thinks you're doing it wrong

#2836. A 'demand decade' ahead for energy? BP, Baker Hughes CEOs weigh the investment outlook

Rising energy demand is creating new investment opportunities even as geopolitical risks and uncertain oil prices challenge the industry.

Baker Hughes CEO Lorenzo Simonelli is optimistic about the longer-term outlook, pointing to AI, population growth and developing nations as factors supporting continued energy demand growth.

"I think we have a 'demand decade' ahead of us," Simonelli told CNBC's Dan Murphy on "Access Middle East" at the ADIPEC energy conference in Abu Dhabi. "We're looking at very much seeing capital investments improve and increase over time," as growing consumption will require additional infrastructure to support demand.

However, turning that demand into profitability may be a challenge.

Markus Krebber, CEO of German energy company RWE, said AI and electrification were driving electricity demand, but geopolitical tensions, supply chain pressures and higher interest rates were complicating investment decisions.

"You have huge investment opportunity, but on the other hand, a very challenging investment environment," Krebber said during a separate ADIPEC discussion. On planned expansions in computing capacity and the power needed to support them, he said "it's going to come, but it probably comes slow."

The wide range of global energy demand forecasts is an important consideration in investment decisions, given that new oil and gas projects can take years to develop and remain in production for decades, BP CEO Meg O'Neill said at ADIPEC.

She also highlighted the uncertainty surrounding oil prices, saying that "we need to plan our business for a lower-price environment."

Industry activity is expected to rise by a low-single-digit percentage in 2027, according to Simonelli. The Middle East conflict has highlighted the need for more resilient energy infrastructure, including additional storage and pipelines, he said.

"If you look at the energy system, though, what it really means is it's going to have to be more resilient," Simonelli said. "That means more storage. It means more pipelines. It means eliminating choke points that exist today."

Schneider Electric CEO Olivier Blum noted that some projects in the Persian Gulf have slowed because of the Middle East conflict. However, "in the mid-term, I would say the opportunity for more infrastructure has even increased," he told CNBC.

The investment equation also depends on the supply outlook, of course.

Oil markets could face oversupply in 2027 and 2028, Richard Bronze, co-founder and head of geopolitics at energy consultancy Energy Aspects, told CNBC in a separate interview.

Supply-demand forecasts, including those from Energy Aspects, "project an oversupplied market 2027, 2028 — if we get Middle Eastern flows back to normal," Bronze said.

#2835. Buccaneers linebacker Alex Anzalone named NFC Defensive Player of the Week

Tampa Bay Buccaneers linebacker Alex Anzalone has been named the NFC Defensive Player of the Week following his standout performance in the team's 16-0 shutout victory over the Atlanta Falcons in Week 8.

The veteran linebacker played a key role in Tampa Bay's defensive dominance on Sunday, intercepting Falcons quarterback Michael Penix Jr. twice in the first quarter. Anzalone also finished the game with six tackles, including one for loss, helping the Buccaneers secure their second consecutive victory.

Both interceptions came during Atlanta's second and third offensive possessions, setting the tone for a Buccaneers defense that held the Falcons to just 224 total yards and forced four turnovers. Atlanta also failed to convert any of its 12 third-down opportunities.

Anzalone became just the second NFL linebacker since 2000 to record two interceptions in the first quarter of a game, joining former Seattle Seahawks linebacker Lofa Tatupu, who accomplished the feat in 2007.

The recognition marks the first NFC Defensive Player of the Week award of Anzalone's 10-year NFL career and the first weekly award earned by a Buccaneers player this season. He is also the first Tampa Bay defender to receive the honor since outside linebacker Anthony Nelson in Week 8 of the 2025 season.

Signed by Tampa Bay in March after five seasons with the Detroit Lions, Anzalone has recorded 42 tackles, one sack, three interceptions and three passes defensed through eight games this season. His three interceptions lead all NFL linebackers.

The Buccaneers will look to build on their defensive momentum when they travel to Chicago to face the Bears on Sunday Night Football in Week 9.

#2834. Klay Thompson heating up just in time to bury Warriors in Golden State return

SAN FRANCISCO — The same Klay Thompson will be back in the building where he helped build the Warriors' dynasty Thursday night, but he won't look quite the same.

The second Splash Brother is on his second team since going solo.

Yes, it's still an odd experience for Steve Kerr seeing Thompson rise and fire in a No. 11 uniform that isn't blue and gold. But after four previous trips back to the Chase Center and seven matchups total since their breakup, it doesn't quite shock the eyes as much as it once did.

"It'll always be a little strange seeing [him] in a different uniform. But it's getting a little less strange," Kerr said. "Those first couple times were really bizarre. And then you get used to it.

"It's a reminder that most of us in the NBA end up on multiple teams."

Miami Heat forward Klay Thompson (11) celebrates after scoring against the Indiana Pacers in an NBA Cup basketball game in Miami, Friday, Oct. 30, 2026. AP Photo/Lynne Sladky

Thompson spent 13 years in the Bay Area, won four championships, rehabbed from two catastrophic leg injuries and drained more than 2,500 3s. Born and raised in Los Angeles, Thompson fell in love with the region — and its fans embraced him just as tightly.

As Kerr alluded to, few things last forever in the NBA. Thompson's run with Golden State reached its end when he wanted more money and a bigger role than the Warriors were willing to offer, coming off his age 33 season and on the wrong side of the actuarial curve.

Before the season, Stephen Curry said, "I miss that dude everyday."

Golden State Warriors head coach Steve Kerr, left, talks with Stephen Curry during a game against the Phoenix Suns in Phoenix, Sunday, Oct. 25, 2026. AP Photo/Ross D. Franklin

But that doesn't mean Thompson is any less inclined to take it to his former team Thursday night. Seeing Kerr and Curry and Draymond Green only makes him want it more.

"I want to beat them bad," Thompson told reporters in Miami. "We're all still very competitive in our mid-30s and I love to go compete against them because they're some of my best friends."

It might be a challenge for a Miami team that was handed its fifth loss in seven games to begin this season Monday night in Los Angeles, a more traditional homecoming for Thompson, who grew up watching his dad, Mychal, wear a Lakers uniform for the final five years of his playing career.

Klay Thompson of the Miami Heat arrives before a game against the Los Angeles Lakers in Los Angeles on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Thompson poured in 19 points in the loss and seems to be heating up just at the right time to put on a show against his former team. After knocking down only 12 of his first 37 3-point tries to start the season (32.4%), he's sunk 11 on 19 attempts over his past two games.

After being reduced to a bench role for most of his second season with the Mavericks, Thompson has started all of Miami's first seven games, averaging 11.9 points in 21.9 minutes.

His name won't be the only one to receive loud cheer during introductions, and no, Giannis Antetokounmpo, isn't expecting a warm welcome after spurning the Warriors' advances.

Klay Thompson of the Miami Heat celebrates during a game against the Indiana Pacers at Kaseya Center in Miami on Friday, Oct. 30, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

Miami Heat guard Klay Thompson (11) shoots a 3-point basket against Indiana Pacers guard Quenton Jackson (29) on Friday, Oct. 30, 2026, in Miami. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

Two-fifths of the Heat's starting five is composed of players who started for the Warriors when they beat the Celtics in Game 6 of the 2022 NBA Finals to clinch their fourth title.

By signing with Miami, Thompson teamed up again with Andrew Wiggins, who was traded there in the deal that brought Jimmy Butler to Golden State. Unlike Thompson, Wiggins has only made one previous trip back to Chase Center since the trade.

It's taken some time for Wiggins to fit in around Antetokounmpo, averaging a career-worst 10.3 points per game while shooting 29.4% from 3, almost seven points below his career rate.

He hasn't scored more than 18 points in any of his previous three rematches with the Warriors. Thompson went off for 22 and 29 in his first two games back against Golden State, but in five matchups since, he's been held to single digits twice, averaging 11.2 per game.

Andrew Wiggins (22) of the Miami Heat drives to the basket against the Indiana Pacers on Friday, Oct. 30, 2026, at Kaseya Center in Miami. NBAE via Getty Images

"This is a big part of the business. It's a small circle, a small community. You get to know each other. You end up competing against friends – sometimes even family," Kerr said. "It'll be great to see both guys — Klay and Wiggs. Two just incredible contributors to the Warriors championships, to the culture, just the vibe of the team. Both Klay and Wiggs are wonderful human beings and great teammates. It'll be good to see them."

The Heat have two free days after Thursday's game before they head up the I-80 corridor to Sacramento to face the Kings on Sunday. They plan to stay in San Francisco, giving the former Warriors teammates plenty of time to wax nostalgic if they so choose.

Maybe Thompson will even take up the opportunity to reprise his daily commute, navigating the water on the San Francisco Bay from his Tiburon home to the Warriors' waterfront facility.

"I just can't wait to go back and see familiar faces," he said. "Not just the players, but the training staff, the equipment managers, the people who work the stands and the concessions, the custodians. I have friends for life in the Bay and LA, so it's always special."

#2833. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2832. MLB Network’s Intentional Talk Reversal Proves Connection Is Media’s Most Valuable Currency

In media, we talk a lot about the currency of connection. It's the ability of a brand or talent to find ways to attach itself to the everyday consumer and become an important part of that person's daily life. It's difficult to earn and even harder to maintain in an increasingly crowded content space that continues to grow. When Intentional Talk announced last month that the show would be ending its 16-year run on MLB Network, baseball fans and players alike demonstrated just how strong their connection to the program had become.

Social media was buzzing. Commentators across traditional and digital media were aghast. How could MLB Network decide to cut its flagship program when so much uncertainty lies ahead this winter for the sport itself? The reaction was loud, and MLB heard it. On Wednesday, MLB reversed course and chose connection over cost.

It goes without saying that Major League Baseball has a lot on its plate at the moment. The glory and spotlight of the World Series are now in the rearview mirror. In addition, the hangovers from the Milwaukee Brewers' first World Series parade have begun to fade. Ahead lies the expiration of the collective bargaining agreement and the potential for a long, drawn-out negotiation.

Yet, amid all the noise and the storm ahead, MLB wanted to ensure that one element of the baseball experience wouldn't disappear. The one hour each weekday that belonged to Kevin Millar, Ryan Dempster and Siera Santos.

Yesterday was originally scheduled to be Intentional Talk's finale. Moreover, the MLB Network website's television schedule even listed the program's timeslot as "Final Show."

Instead, the episode became a celebration that was never planned. The show wrapped up its 16th season on MLB Network with a "see you next season." Instead of the depressing "see you down the road." While the reasons behind the decision to end Intentional Talk were never revealed, the reasons to keep it were evident.

Connection is currency. In media, it's among the most valuable assets any brand or talent can own. Intentional Talk was never a ratings juggernaut, crushing viewership records year after year. The program rarely broke major news or leaned into the kind of hot takes that drive engagement online.

Instead, Intentional Talk earned its connection through honest, revealing and entertaining conversations that celebrated the game and the personalities surrounding it. For years, I heard people talk about how baseball doesn't do enough to market its biggest personalities. Of course, superstars from large-market teams are often the ones featured in national advertising campaigns.

However, the underlying roster of talent and the personalities behind those players represent a largely untapped source of interest in the sport. That's where Intentional Talk swung for the fence.

Intentional Talk was the vehicle that connected fans to that layer of baseball through personality. It took a game some consider boring and made it entertaining by showcasing its lighter side. More importantly, it gave players an opportunity to reveal their personalities. They could crack jokes, answer trivia questions and connect with audiences in ways that no home run or postgame press conference could replicate.

Intentional Talk has become, and continues to be, your favorite baseball player's favorite baseball show.

It shouldn't be surprising that the outpouring of support for the program ultimately tipped the scales and prompted MLB to reconsider its decision. I wish more companies would put the viewer's perspective first when making programming decisions rather than allowing other considerations to take precedence.

Yes, shows and talent come and go. That's the nature of the industry. However, some programs are outliers that become incredibly difficult to replace. We have this discussion all the time about Pardon the Interruption, Inside the NBA and several other programs. They are built around identities that connect with viewers and make them feel invested in the entertainment value of sports.

Intentional Talk should serve as a reminder to every show, network and talent in sports media that the most valuable connection you can build doesn't necessarily come through the biggest interview, the loudest opinion or the most viral moment. It comes from finding a way to make sports more personal for the people watching.

Millar, Dempster and Santos, along with former hosts Chris Rose and Stephen Nelson, found that pathway through baseball. They created a space where fans could connect with the personalities behind the game rather than simply the results on the field.

Every show and every talent should be looking for its own version of that connection. What makes your audience feel invested? What makes people want to spend an hour with you when they have countless other options? And what can you offer that makes your relationship with them difficult to replace?

In an industry where programming decisions are often dictated by ratings, viewership and the bottom line. Intentional Talk demonstrated that audience connection still carries tremendous value over all that. Moreover, you can't always control the decisions made above you, the direction of the industry or how long you have a platform. But you can control the relationships you build with your audience.

If there's one lesson to take from Intentional Talk's 16-year run and reverse course by MLB Network, it's that finding your own pathway to connection through sports might be the best investment you can make in securing your future in sports media.

Barrett Media produces daily content on the music, news, and sports media industries. Sign up for our newsletters to stay updated and get the latest information right in your inbox.

The post MLB Network's Intentional Talk Reversal Proves Connection Is Media's Most Valuable Currency appeared first on Barrett Media.

#2831. White House panel delays hearing on Fed's Lisa Cook to Dec. 14

A White House committee of inquiry postponed from Thursday to Dec. 14 a hearing with Federal Reserve Governor Lisa Cook for an investigation of whether allegations of false statements in mortgage applications warrant her firing by President Donald Trump, a White House official said Wednesday evening.

But it was unclear if Cook, whom Trump unsuccessfully tried to fire in August 2025, would agree to attend.

The official said the postponement came at the request of Cook.

Her lawyer Abbe Lowell last month said questions about the committee's fairness and objectivity would have to be addressed before the hearing could occur.

Lowell also said the hearing could not take place until December, at the earliest, because she "will be executing her official duties as Governor at financial and monetary policy meetings in Europe."

The New York Times first reported the hearing's rescheduling.

Trump in early October established the committee to investigate allegations that Cook made false statements related to "one or more mortgage instruments."

"The Committee shall investigate the allegations against Governor Cook and report to me whether there is 'cause' for Governor Cook's removal within the meaning of" a federal law that was cited in a Supreme Court ruling in June that temporarily blocked her firing.

The committee is comprised of Kevin Hassett, assistant to the president for economic policy, Andrea Lucas, the chair of the Equal Employment Opportunity Commission, and Office of Government Ethics Director Keith Sonderling.

In a lawsuit challenging her firing, she argued that Trump's claim about the mortgage statements is pretextual, and that his real reason for trying to oust her is her refusal to agree to his demands that she vote for lower interest rates.

Trump created the panel more than three months after the Supreme Court ruled that the president, for now, could not fire Cook from the central bank's board.

The ruling kept in place a lower court's order preventing Cook from being terminated while the suit challenging her dismissal proceeds.

The decision did not determine whether Trump ultimately has the power to fire a Fed board member. But it said she could remain on the board as her suit was pending because she was not given due process to challenge her firing.

The committee of inquiry Trump set up may be designed to show the Supreme Court that Cook has now received due process.

#2830. Justin Herbert 'was throwing deli meat' in 2-INT loss to Rams, S Quentin Lake says; Jim Harbaugh disagrees

Back in September, after Los Angeles Chargers quarterback Justin Herbert was picked off on the doorstep of the end zone in the fourth quarter of a Week 3 road loss to the Buffalo Bills, Tom Brady described the intercepted pass as a "meatball sub."

"Herbert trying to throw down the middle of the field," Brady said at the time on the Fox Sports broadcast. "But there's a safety in the middle of the field. … Poor awareness. Poor decision."

More than a month later, Herbert still hasn't snapped out his head-scratching funk, and the sandwich metaphor has resurfaced.

This time, it was used by Los Angeles Rams safety Quentin Lake, who nabbed one of Herbert's two picks last week in a 24-9 win over the Chargers at SoFi Stadium.

"I mean, he was throwing deli meat out there," Lake said on the "St. Brown Podcast," which is co-hosted by brothers Amon-Ra St. Brown and Equanimeous St. Brown.

Amon-Ra St. Brown, a four-time Pro Bowl wide receiver for the Detroit Lions, asked, "Was he?"

"Bro," Lake said prior to shaking his head and starting to smile.

Lake then began cracking up before reeling his laughter back in.

"Bro, I don't know what's going on," he continued, "but, like, we knew we were going to get a couple [interceptions]. At least one."

Herbert, the 2020 NFL Offensive Rookie of the Year and a two-time Pro Bowler since, shockingly leads the NFL with 10 picks this season.

Some of those turnovers have been straight-up flummoxing. Lake's interception came on an out route that he read like a book midway through the first quarter Sunday.

Later in the game, with the Chargers trailing the Rams 17-9 late in third, Herbert tossed the ball into heavy traffic. Cornerback Trent McDuffie easily caught it and ripped off a 26-yard return that set the stage for the Rams' final touchdown.

Coming into the 2026 campaign, Herbert had been revered throughout his young NFL career. Right from the get-go, the No. 6 overall pick thrived in the league, translating his success from Oregon to the next level.

To see him struggle the way he has this season has been jarring. Hearing Lake say that Herbert's "seeing ghosts" is comparably sobering.

When asked about Lake's comments Wednesday, Chargers head coach Jim Harbaugh disagreed with the defensive back's assessment.

"As far as anybody's opinion, I mean, I don't see that," Harbaugh said, per The Athletic's Noah Furtado. "Everybody's entitled to their opinion. That's not what I see. ... Pass game, I thought [Herbert] did his job exceptionally well. You know, two throws he would've liked to have back."

Herbert finished the game 24 of 32 with 257 yards in the air, no touchdowns and those two picks. He hasn't thrown a TD pass since an Oct. 11 matchup versus the Denver Broncos. Granted, the Chargers have had a bye week since, but that Broncos game is also the last time they've scored any kind of touchdown.

Harbaugh has frequently backed Herbert during their overlap in L.A. But it's becoming increasingly difficult to defend some of his giveaways.

After posting a completion rate north of 65% in each of his first six NFL seasons, the 28-year-old Herbert's percentage in that department has dropped to 61.8%. His sudden inefficiency and uncharacteristically high interception total have raised serious questions about his fit with first-year Chargers offensive coordinator Mike McDaniel.

The 0-7 Chargers haven't moved off McDaniel yet, however, and the ETA on Herbert's long-awaited bounce-back performance is up in the air.

#2828. Jim Cramer warns investors may be reading too much into Wall Street's post-election rally

CNBC's Jim Cramer warned Wednesday that Wall Street may be getting ahead of itself following the midterm elections.

Stocks rallied after Democrats scored a decisive victory in the midterms, but the gains masked a divide within technology. Hyperscalers surged as investors anticipated that greater opposition to data center development could slow the AI buildout and ease spending pressures. Meanwhile, semiconductor stocks and data center developers came under pressure. Cramer cautioned that investors may be drawing premature conclusions about the election's economic implications.

"The midterm results controlled today's trading, but by Friday we will be talking the Labor Department's nonfarm payrolls report, and we could see a repeal of so much of this move that I hesitate to guess where we go next," the "Mad Money" host said.

"But I do like the word guess," he added. "Because that sums up what I saw throughout today's session and it's not the stuff I want to base investment decisions on, even as it's terrific for a one-day trade for the ages."

Apple shares hit an all-time high Wednesday as investors bet that a slowdown in data center construction could ease memory chip prices, which have been pressuring the company's margins. Microsoft and Amazon also rallied on expectations that a slower AI buildout could reduce data center costs and rein in their massive capital spending.

Conversely, investors sold shares of companies whose growth depends on continued AI infrastructure investment, including chipmakers Nvidia and AMD and data center developers CoreWeave, Nebius and Iren.

But Cramer questioned whether a single election could fundamentally alter the forces driving the market. "I want you to understand that, as important as a midterm election can be, and this one is important, the elections did not upend the business cycle," Cramer said.

Cramer noted that he's aligned with JPMorgan strategist Michael Cembalest, who publishes the closely followed Eye on the Market commentary.

In his latest piece Wednesday, Cembalest wrote, "The business cycle drives markets, and the important components are things that midterms do not affect: 10 yr interest rates, commodity prices, Fed policy, corporate profits, budget deficits, defense spending, the pace of AI investment over the next 12 months, the ferocious borrowing needs of AI companies and the impact on credit spreads, etc."

Cramer said it's important that investors understand that short-term market moves may not always align with the actual fundamentals. "Never confuse what happens in the stock market with what happens in reality," he said.

Cramer's Charitable Trust, the portfolio run by CNBC's Investing Club, owns shares of AAPL, AMZN, MSFT, and NVDA.

#2826. What Republicans' huge loss means for Trump

Democrats just put President Donald Trump in a bind.

The party is projected to win commanding majorities in the House and Senate after thrashing Republicans in key races up and down the ballot across the country. The blue tsunami will knock Washington on its head and crush the Republican congressional bulwark that both supported and shielded Trump through the first two years of his term.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

The midterm result snuffs out any chance Trump had of passing partisan legislation in the second half of his presidency. And many of the interests he has been able to pursue without Congress' input — including tariffs, the Iran war and an array of Washington-area construction projects — are sure to face sudden pushback from newly empowered Democratic majorities.

The paradigm shift hardly leaves Trump helpless, however. As president at a time of executive power expansion, Trump wields singular authorities that could stymie Democrats' efforts to pursue their own agendas.

But with his grip on the Capitol shriveling and his approval ratings scraping record lows in numerous polls, it's unclear how much support he'll be able to muster to fight the coming political battles.

Even before Election Day, analysts and pundits had contemplated a Democratic rout so decisive that it would force a reckoning for the GOP, and potentially even pose an existential threat to Trump's "Make America Great Again" movement.

Marc Short, chairman of Advancing American Freedom and onetime chief of staff to former Vice President Mike Pence, told CNBC on Tuesday afternoon that the GOP has moved away from its conservative foundations.

"We need a refresh right now," Short said in an interview. "We've lost our way."

Trump has been vague about what he hopes to achieve with the rest of his term. But he has shared a vision of how he might coexist with a Democratic majority, and it involves heavy use of the veto pen.

"It's one of the stronger powers that a president has," Trump said in a Fox News interview overnight. "They'll send over things that are insane, and I'll veto them."

"And after a couple of months of vetoes, they'll call and they'll say, 'How do we get this stuff done?' And we'll work together," he said, adding, "I have a lot of friends that are Democrats."

Trump has previously stated his willingness to veto reams of legislation that Democrats send across his desk. In a New York Post podcast interview released Monday, Trump said that if Republicans don't win, "I veto everything."

Pressed to detail his plans for the next two years, Trump touted his record and said, "The most important thing is to keep it going. It's going to get better and better and better, unless people come in that hate our country."

If that happens, "I guess I'll become Mr. Veto," Trump said.

He may soon get his chance: House Democrats, poised to be led by current Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries of New York, are planning to pass a flurry of bills as soon as they take the reins next January, CNBC has learned.

The legislative push, first reported by The New York Times, aims to show a united Democratic caucus taking quick action on the issues they were elected to solve, especially oppressively high costs on everything from gas to groceries.

In their first 100 hours after being sworn in, House Democrats plan to introduce bills reinstating the enhanced health insurance subsidies that expired under the GOP-held Congress, and reversing the Medicaid cuts Trump signed into law earlier this year.

Democrats also plan to push forward a bill that would undo Trump's sweeping tariffs and reassert Congress' role in setting import taxes. And they will again vote on legislation to end the war with Iran, or seek congressional authorization to keep it going.

But without Trump's approval, those bills may end up as little more than symbolic gestures, as Democrats will lack the two-thirds majorities in both chambers that are required to override a presidential veto without GOP help.

And there's no guarantee the bills will even make it out of Congress: Despite their landslide victories, Senate Democrats will still end up shy of the 60 votes required to bypass the chamber's filibuster rules for most legislation, giving Republicans key leverage in the minority.

Democrats also could feasibly benefit from Trump's vetoes in the long term. Forcing the president to shoot down numerous popular bills could give them fodder in the 2028 election.

It's far from clear whether Trump believes he can find compromise with Democrats on any of their initial legislative priorities.

But any potential for a working relationship with the opposition could be poisoned early on, as Democrats in both chambers are aggressively signaling their plans to launch politically charged investigations against Trump, his political and corporate allies and even his family members.

Winning control of House committees gives Democrats the ability to issue subpoenas under the current rules.

Rep. Robert Garcia, D-Calif., who is likely to chair the House Oversight Committee in the next Congress, said his panel will target the Trump family's business dealings, corporate corruption and alleged abuses by federal immigration agencies. He also plans to return focus to the Trump administration's handling of files related to notorious sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

As results rolled in Tuesday night, Garcia summed up his plans in a two-word statement: "Lawyer up."

It's not just Garcia. Other Democrats have already demanded records from the Trump administration on a range of issues. Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., for instance, last week accused Trump of using tariff exemptions to cut "sweetheart deals" for industries whose leaders have plied the president with gifts.

Senate Democrats have sent similar signals.

"They are going to need to plant new forests for all of the subpoenas we'll be printing," Sen. Ed Markey, D-Mass., said in an X post late Tuesday in response to a post, appearing to refer to tech executives.

The investigations are widely expected to consume much of the rest of Trump's term.

Trump has ample experience being targeted by Democrats, who impeached him twice in the House during his first presidential term.

But with his base of support appearing to erode inside and outside of Washington, Trump may have to face his political foes with a smaller arsenal than he's used to.

"I'm not expecting Republicans to revolt" against Trump following the midterm losses, AAF's Short told CNBC. "But I'm expecting some of them to quietly distance themselves."

#2825. Coinbase net income surges 66% in third quarter, benefiting from bitcoin investment gains

Coinbase Global earned third-quarter adjusted net income of $719 million, up 66% from $433 million a year ago, largely due to a recovery in the cryptocurrency market.

Coinbase said it earned $2.51 a share in the latest quarter, although it wasn't immediately clear if that compared with the 6-cent-per-share profit that Wall Street analysts had estimated. Revenue of $1.20 billion trailed the $1.233 billion expected by analysts polled by LSEG.

Net income at the San Francisco-based platform surged thanks to the value of its volatile cryptocurrency investment portfolio, which can swing wildly even when the composition of the underlying holdings are little changed.

The third quarter value of Coinbase's crypto investment portfolio climbed due to a resurgence in the digital asset market. The price of bitcoin — often used as a gauge for all crypto assets — rose 39% in the three-month period ended Sept. 30 and the flagship cryptocurrency recently changed hands at roughly $80,809.

In the third quarter, Coinbase deepened its push into non-crypto products and services, including artificial intelligence integrations for traders, tokenized real-world assets and prediction markets.

In July, VP of product Max Branzburg told CNBC that Coinbase would debut an "everything exchange" spotlighting tokenized stocks and other real-world assets, derivatives trading, prediction markets and early-stage token sales. The trading platform also introduced Coinbase for Agents in early October, supporting users that want to use ChatGPT, Muse or other AI agents to manage their portfolios or research and trade various assets.

Coinbase also expanded its prediction market offerings, making a wider range of event contracts available to users.

In the third quarter, prediction markets revenue grew to more than $200 million on an annual basis, while the number of traders on those markets increased 60% compared with the second quarter.

#2821. Analysis: The Democrats have one chance to extract concessions from Trump. Markets will hate it

The electoral sweep by Democrats on Tuesday night injects a new, unstable dynamic into the single biggest political risk to financial markets in the coming year: the debt limit. Unless a defeated Republican Party makes a swift and unlikely show of unity to take the issue off the table in its last few weeks in power, the protracted fight that will unfold is set to be nastier than ever, adding volatility to an already shaky bond market.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm election results

Past episodes of brinkmanship around the need to raise the government's borrowing limit have involved Republicans in Congress demanding budget cuts from a Democratic president as the price of their vote. This time it will almost certainly be the left seeking concessions from the right.

The debt limit is the definition of a must-pass bill, even more than issues like funding the government and the defense budget. That makes it potentially the one real chance the new Democratic majority in Congress has to extract concessions from a president who is already pledging to veto their agenda. Markets will hate the entire process, even if — as many expect — it ends in compromise.

Because it is Democrats who hold the leverage, unlike past episodes, the ultimate agreement is more likely to increase government spending than lower it. With high bond yields weighing on the economy, and Americans expecting action from a newly empowered Democratic opposition, the fight could be particularly damaging.

Many in the markets are stuck on the outdated idea that Democrats are less assertive than Republicans, said Aniket Shah, head of the Washington policy team at the investment bank Jefferies.

"They have this sort of mental image of Biden-Harris 2024," said Shah. "I don't think they realize how aggressively Democrats plan to take 2027."

The debt ceiling is a quirk of the congressional budgeting process. It stops the government from borrowing funds that Congress has already ordered federal agencies to spend. And because Congress has run deficits for decades — this year at $1.99 trillion — the pile of debt periodically bumps into the debt ceiling.

Congress last set the limit in 2025, when it gave the Treasury Department the authority to borrow $41.1 trillion. The Treasury is likely to hit its maximum borrowing capacity by midsummer, according to the Bipartisan Policy Center, a nonprofit that tracks the issue.

The Treasury can use budgeting techniques to extend the so-called X-date, when the U.S. would no longer be able to meet all its obligations, to sometime in the fourth quarter of 2027, according to Wall Street estimates.

A few technical hiccups aside, the U.S. has never meaningfully breached the debt limit. The results of the U.S. missing payments would be chaotic as damage ripples out across the financial system into the global economy. U.S. default would immediately call into question trillions in Treasury securities that are the lifeblood of the global financial system. Damaging them could send the U.S. and global economies into an economic tailspin with no obvious means of recovery.

Because the stakes are so high, Shah and many others on Wall Street have come to believe that the episode will produce volatility but will ultimately be resolved in compromise.

Fights under former Presidents Barack Obama and Joe Biden saw Republican congressional majorities use the issue to ram through spending reductions. (The 2025 raise was less contentious, because Republicans held Congress and the presidency. It was passed along party lines.)

Still, the risk-taking in those episodes contributed to ratings firms' decisions to downgrade U.S. debt — and could again.

President Donald Trump has called for Republicans to resolve the issue in the next few weeks, before the new Democratic-majority Congress is seated.

That isn't likely. The 2025 debt limit increase only passed because it was tied to a package of tax cuts and other policy changes. Republican debt hawks complained but held their noses.

Republicans may attempt to raise the limit in the coming lame-duck session under a budgeting procedure called reconciliation, which would allow them to move ahead without Democrats' votes. But success would require unity among a fractured, fleeting Republican majority that also faces several other more pressing priorities.

The most likely path toward raising the debt limit is the incoming Democratic majority negotiating another temporary fix with Trump. But they aren't going to make it easy. The president has occasionally made noises about making deals, but the political incentives ahead of the 2028 presidential election are for both sides to dig in.

"[Democrats] are going to want to embarrass the White House, and they're going to want to get leverage," Shah said. Democrats will use that to push to reverse cuts to Medicaid spending passed in 2025 and to try to restore healthcare spending, he said.

Democrats could also seek to tie the debt limit to restraining signature Trump policies, such as limiting the war with Iran or rolling back tariffs, analysts at Goldman Sachs said in a research note Wednesday.

Another potential compromise being considered in some corners of Washington is for Democrats to tie an increase in the corporate tax rate to a debt limit raise, said George Pollack, senior U.S. analyst with Signum Global Advisors. Trump has shown he can be surprisingly flexible on similar issues, Pollack noted.

That is what will make this debt limit fight different. In the past, Republicans used it to try to win spending reductions they couldn't get through regular budget negotiations. Now Democrats will broaden it out into an all-purpose hostage. That effort is far more likely to raise overall government spending than cut it. "There's a very weird irony here," said Robert Sockin, chief U.S. economist at PGIM, an asset manager.

The path to compromise will likely be rocky. Sockin believes it may turn out like the 2025 government shutdown. That was the longest government-wide shutdown in history, even though Democrats weren't in control of either chamber of Congress. This time they will have even more power.

If the fight extends beyond the X-date, missing debt payments could do serious damage to the financial system.

"It's not our base case, but we think the risk is higher than ever that you could get a technical default from the government," Sockin said. That would see the U.S. miss a payment, even if the government eventually makes it up.

That would still hurt. "You'll get more downgrades to the U.S. sovereign, and those might bite more this time," Sockin said. Yields on longer-term Treasurys would rise to account for added uncertainty.

Crossing the red line could prompt a "very negative reaction in equity markets," he said.

The coming months will show just how close Democrats are willing to get to that line.

#2820. Inside India newsletter: India weighs a tax cut to win over foreign investors. Will it work?

Hello, this is Priyanka Salve, writing to you from Singapore.

Welcome to the latest edition of "Inside India" — your one-stop destination for stories and developments from the world's fastest-growing large economy.

Once a favorite emerging market for foreign investors, Indian equities have been losing their charm. Foreign holdings of Indian stocks are currently at an all-time low of 14%. Now a tax cut, which puts India on par with several Asian markets, is being proposed to win back these investors.

This week, I spoke with experts to find out whether a tax cut alone can restore the Indian stock market's appeal to foreign investors.

Any thoughts on today's newsletter? Share them with the team.

The clamor for removing tax on long-term capital gains, or LTCG, on foreign portfolio investors is rising in India as the country's equity markets witness record capital outflows.

On Monday, Reuters reported that India's capital market regulator has urged the government to scrap the LTCG tax for foreign investors.

One of India's oldest and most prominent industry bodies, PHD Chamber of Commerce and Industry, last week asked the government appealing to abolish the tax.

It argued that the capital gains tax was increased in 2024 at a time when the stock market was "near record highs" and as the market conditions have now changed, the government needs to review it.

The country's Finance Minister Nirmala Sitharaman on Monday acknowledged the exit of foreign capital outflows and added she would consider the calls for reviewing LTCG tax, as per local media reports.

These demands come at a time when foreign investors' appetite for Indian equities has sharply declined. As per a Nomura report on Monday, foreign holdings in Indian stocks are at an all-time low of 14%.

Experts agree that the removal of the tax cut would improve foreign investors' sentiment by putting India's tax regime on par with other emerging markets. But the tax cut alone may not be enough to bring back foreign investors in droves, they said.

"A tax cut helps at the margin, but allocators tend to buy expected returns, not tax breaks," said Amit Bhartia, portfolio manager at Singapore-based Delorean Partners. He added that foreign investors have exited India as earnings growth became less predictable, while risks shot up.

Bhartia highlighted that foreign investors put in about 570 billion rupees ($6 billion) into Indian equities in September of 2024, and a month later pulled out 940 billion rupees to chase cheaper Chinese stocks. "The tax rate didn't change between those two months. The perceived relative return did," Bhartia explained.

Until the record exit in March this year, the outflows in October 2024 were the highest in a month.

Multiple risks are weighing down Indian markets right now, including strained ties with the U.S., higher energy prices, a depreciating currency and uncertainty over the impact of artificial intelligence.

A possible LTCG cut for foreign investors, a drop in global bond yields and expectations of the rupee holding up on central bank intervention would lend support to the Indian markets, Nomura said in its report.

However, it added that from a longer-term perspective, concerns around geopolitical and technological uncertainty persist, "weighing on long-term growth expectations."

India was the world's third worst-performing market after Indonesia and Turkey between January and October this year, the report showed. India's benchmark Nifty 50 as lost nearly 9% so far this year.

Foreign investors, who just three years ago celebrated India as the best-performing emerging market, have soured over the last two years, cashing out net $18.9 billion in 2025 and more than $30 billion so far this year.

India has "to fight for capital in a competition it could once afford to take for granted," global brokerage Bernstein said in a report on Monday. It argued that multiple "catalysts," including a tax cut and easing of tensions in the Middle East, are needed to reverse capital outflows from India.

"India's valuations remain expensive, while earnings growth has been disappointing," the report said.

As per the data shared in the report, India is trading at a rolling 12-month forward price-to-earnings of 18.6 times, higher than Taiwan's 16.8 times and Korea's 3.9 times. Both these markets have been outperformers in the emerging countries, propped up by strong artificial intelligence names.

On the earnings growth front, while emerging markets on average are expected to deliver a 45% improvement, India's forecast is much slower at 11%, as per Bernstein.

The tax cut alone will not "trigger a major reallocation into Indian equities as the fundamental trade-off facing foreign investors remains unchanged," Hanna Luchnikava-Schorsch, head of Asia-Pacific economics at S&P Global Market Intelligence, told CNBC.

As cracks emerge in the global AI rally, data from LSEG shows Indian benchmark indexes have outperformed global peers over the past month, as some investors see the Indian market as a hedge against AI. Foreign capital flows at the start of this month have been positive. But will they sustain?

India has a strong structural growth story, but investors still need earnings growth to justify the valuation premium, Luchnikava-Schorsch said.

Canada-India $5.5 billion bank takeover attracts opposition from workers, politiciansCanadian firm Fairfax Financial Holdings' proposed $5.5 billion takeover of IDBI Bank is facing pushback from employees of the Indian state-owned lender and political parties in the country.

Indian AI infrastructure firm reportedly seeks to raise $3 billion of debt to buy Nvidia's next-generation chipsAn artificial intelligence infrastructure firm in India is reportedly seeking to raise debt to fund the purchase of Nvidia's Rubin graphics processing units. AM Intelligence, a Hyderabad-based company, is planning to raise up to $3 billion through bond issues and bank loans to purchase Nvidia Corp's next-generation chips, Bloomberg reported on Friday.

#2818. $6.3 billion Binance hack is largest crypto theft on record

Binance said Friday that a security incident impacted about $6.3 billion in client assets, with around $3 billion frozen on-chain, marking the largest crypto hack on record.

The company later added it began recovering assets impacted by the breach after pledging to cover losses using its Secure Asset Fund for Users, which the company created in 2018 as a protection for clients in the event of a hack.

It wasn't clear how the attack took place. The world's largest crypto exchange initially said the hack may involve "a compromised DevOps engineer's laptop, which allowed an attacker to gain access to a service container and submit unauthorized withdrawal transactions." However, Co-CEO Richard Teng later said the attack involved a more complex process rather than the misuse of a single engineer's permissions.

The FBI said Friday it was aware of the incident and was in touch with "relevant public and private partners to understand the scope of the activity, evaluate ​potential impacts, ​and ⁠coordinate appropriate investigative steps."

It wasn't clear either who was responsible for the breach. But TRM Labs, a blockchain intelligence and AI investigations company, pointed to North Korean hackers as the potential culprits. "Several features of this attack match earlier North Korea-linked heists, including the Bybit hack and the KelpDAO hack in April 2026: small Tornado Cash withdrawals funding an operator wallet, staging that began well before the theft, and signatures obtained in advance then executed in one burst," the company said in a post.

Blockchain data platform Chainalysis also said this appears "be the work of North Korean cybercrime group TraderTraitor. This group has repeatedly targeted the cryptocurrency industry, exchanges, DeFi protocols, and investors, to raise funds for the regime. We base our intelligence assessment on on-chain activity observed in the hours following the hack."

Binance temporarily suspended withdrawals while it overhauls its withdrawal security systems and has revoked the affected address authorizations, Teng said. The exchange is also working with law enforcement and blockchain intelligence firms to trace and recover the stolen funds, while deposits remain unaffected.

Binance co-founder Yi He said deposits remained unaffected and continued to operate normally, while the exchange was working on enhancements to its withdrawal service and related address controls. It expects to begin resuming withdrawal services within the next 48 hours.

"We have found no evidence of any wallet private key leak. We will share a detailed technical post-mortem once the investigation is complete and the facts are fully verified," Binance said.

The announcement followed moves by several crypto firms, including Circle Internet Group, Tether and Ripple, to freeze funds tied to the hack.

Exploits are not uncommon in the crypto space. In 2025, Bybit suffered roughly $1.5 billion in losses due to a hack. More recently, Bitget lost more than $350 million worth of digital assets in a cyber theft incident. Over the past several years, bad actors have also drained billions of dollars in crypto from decentralized exchanges.

This week's breach "proves our exact point in proposing regulations for crypto markets earlier this month," Michael Selig, chairman of the Commodity Futures Trading Commission, said Friday in a CNBC "Squawk on the Street" interview. The U.S. derivatives regulator on Oct. 5 proposed a framework that would effectively put crypto exchanges under federal government oversight — after Congress failed to approve legislation to regulate the asset.

"The real issue here is that these are unregulated offshore exchanges, and they don't have the same sorts of safeguards that we have in our financial markets here in the United States. We want to offer a federal option to these exchanges to come and register with the CFTC, and they would be subject to the same sorts of cyber security safeguards that we have in our markets today," he said.

Bitcoin fell more than 4% to $77,187.60 on Friday as the hack weighed on crypto markets.

Circle CEO Jeremy Allaire said Thursday evening in a social media post that the company had frozen more than 800 million in USD Coin associated with the incident. USDT stablecoin issuer Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino also said in on X that his firm had frozen more than 1.5 billion of its stablecoin that may have been related to a potential breach.

Jack McDonald, Ripple's senior vice president of stablecoins, said it was actively freezing affected funds held in RLUSD, its U.S. dollar-backed stablecoin.

#2817. Yankees’ Aaron Judge unfairly called out because of Jazz Chisholm Jr’s reported attitude

Yankees' Aaron Judge unfairly called out because of Jazz Chisholm Jr's reported attitude originally appeared on The Sporting News. Add The Sporting News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

There seems to be an expectation around Major League Baseball that the New York Yankees aren't going to bring Jazz Chisholm Jr. back.

Not extending him a qualifying offer seems all but guaranteed right now, though anything can happen in free agency.

If Chisholm doesn't find the market he's looking for and the Yankees can get him at a relatively low price, bringing him back on a short-term deal wouldn't be the worst idea.

As the Yankees get prepared for some other moves they have to make over the next couple of months, it is at least something they'll have to consider. However, the problem isn't with his play, as many have reported multiple times now that his attitude hasn't made many in the Yankees' front office or clubhouse too happy.

The latest suggestion, however, is a bit unfair to slugger Aaron Judge. Judge is undoubtedly one of the best players in Major League Baseball and the Yankees' captain.

Unfortunately, he dealt with some injuries this season, but he was still the guy the Yankees relied on to be the voice in the locker room. According to one NJ.com writer, however, he blames Judge because of Chisholm's attitude and antics.

"I'll start with this: All the stuff that Jazz Chisholm got away with the last few years and that clubhouse culture stuff you're hearing from CC Sabathia, Joe Girardi, Alex Rodriguez, Jorge Posada and other Yankees alums who won World Series is partially an indictment on Aaron Judge. He is the captain. I know Judge is a great leader because I've heard it from almost every player who's been in the Yankees clubhouse going back to 2017, Judge's rookie year.

"You can't find a better teammate. He calls team meetings to give pep talks and point out mistakes. But I think the Yankees had problems this year that weren't stopped — Jazz's antics, players getting picked off base, fundamental mistakes. I think that needs to change and I'd like to see Judge be a little tougher. I think if he was tougher, some of this stuff would stop because Judge is so respected," Randy Miller of NJ.com wrote.

As a captain, Judge has a job to ensure guys are doing and saying the right things. However, it's also fair to mention that Chisholm is a grown man who makes his own decisions.

Judge can't control him going out to second base with a lollipop in his mouth, making comments to the media, and showing some other frustrating parts.

It's easy to point fingers, but the reality is that the Yankees' captain can only do so much when it comes to problems like this.

#2816. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2814. LIV Golf faces liquidation after BC Partners scraps rescue plan

LIV Golf could be headed for liquidation after BC Partners Credit reportedly dropped plans to finance a relaunch of the breakaway league.

The investment firm had pledged up to $300 million for a revamped version of LIV, known as LIV 2.0, but withdrew the plan after LIV Golf failed to secure enough player commitments, according to the Associated Press.

LIV is scheduled to appear in U.S. bankruptcy court Thursday, with the hearing now expected to focus on liquidation of its assets. The league's ultimate fate remains unclear.

LIV Golf and BC Partners did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

LIV filed for Chapter 11 bankruptcy protection in September after Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund said it would stop funding the league after the 2026 season.

PIF had invested more than $5 billion since LIV launched in 2022, according to its Chapter 11 filing.

BC Partners' proposed relaunch would have made players majority owners of the reorganized league and featured a scaled-back schedule.

But LIV has lost several prominent players, including Jon Rahm and Sergio Garcia, as it sought commitments for the new venture.

Rahm said Tuesday that uncertainty over the league's future and proposed changes to its format influenced his decision to leave.

"There were too many unknowns at the time, and there's still quite a few," he told reporters ahead of the Abu Dhabi Championship. Rahm added that the revamped competition appeared to be moving away from the team format that initially attracted him.

Garcia, who secured a release from his LIV contract in October, has outlined plans to return to the DP World Tour and explore a possible return to the PGA Tour.

Only four players had publicly committed to LIV 2.0: Richard Bland, Cameron Tringale, Brendan Steele and Ben Campbell, according to Golf Digest.

LIV launched in 2022 as a challenger to the PGA Tour, recruiting prominent players including Phil Mickelson, Dustin Johnson and Bryson DeChambeau with lucrative contracts.

Its arrival split men's professional golf, with the PGA Tour suspending members who competed in LIV events. The disputes later spilled into the courts, with players challenging suspensions and the rival organizations pursuing competing legal claims.

The PGA Tour denied the allegations, while LIV dismissed the counterclaims.

A June 2023 framework agreement between the PGA Tour, DP World Tour and PIF ended their litigation and proposed combining their commercial businesses, although the deal was never completed.
