#2811. Inside the chip talent war: Trade secrets and rising Chinese rivals

A former Samsung Electronics employee who later joined Chinese memory chipmaker ChangXin Memory Technologies, or CXMT, was reportedly sentenced by the Seoul High Court on Oct. 22 to five years in prison for leaking Samsung's technology.

The sentence was reduced from seven years at his first trial in April. The former employee had joined CXMT in 2016.

The case highlights an intensifying battle over semiconductor know-how as Chinese chipmakers narrow the gap with established rivals, putting the race for top engineering talent increasingly at odds with efforts to protect trade secrets.

In 2023, a South Korean court granted Samsung's request to block a former DRAM researcher from joining U.S. rival Micron. It ruled that the two-year non-compete was not excessive and was necessary to protect Samsung's technology and business information, JoongAng Ilbo reported.

A decade ago, Chinese memory chipmakers were not Samsung's biggest concern when it came to losing experienced engineers.

Instead, the South Korean company was more focused on employees leaving for established rivals such as SK Hynix and Micron, according to a former employee who asked not to be identified because the information was private.

Samsung has been monitoring where certain departing employees went — to signal to remaining staff that moving to competitors would be difficult — according to a former employee.

The company also used non-compete agreements restricting employees from joining rivals for two years, as part of their employment contracts, with senior employees holding valuable technical expertise likely facing closer scrutiny.

But Samsung's concern extended beyond individual departures.

"It wasn't just about one employee leaving. The company wanted to send a message to those who stayed that moving to a competitor wouldn't be easy," the former employee said.

Meanwhile, Chinese semiconductor companies have sought to close the technology gap by recruiting experienced overseas engineers with high salaries, stock options, housing and family support, according to Jeongku Choi, a senior analyst at Counterpoint Research.

Their expertise has helped Chinese chipmakers overcome technical bottlenecks, particularly as U.S. export restrictions have limited China's access to advanced chipmaking equipment, Choi said.

Once viewed as unlikely to catch up with established players, Chinese manufacturers are now seen as "meaningful competitors," she added.

Counterpoint's Memory Tracker for the second quarter of 2026 projected CXMT's share of global DRAM revenue would reach 10%.

Established chipmakers are strengthening legal protections and internal security measures while adjusting compensation packages to retain key engineers, according to Choi.

South Korea has also designated certain semiconductor manufacturing technologies as "National Core Technologies," raising the penalties for unauthorized leaks of those technologies, Choi said.

The legal battle could intensify as Chinese memory companies expand beyond their home market.

"I believe that this management of trade secrets will expand its scope even further to IP and legal areas once Chinese memory companies move beyond the Chinese market and begin to expand their exports," Choi said.

The growing competition is putting greater focus on how semiconductor companies protect the knowledge accumulated by engineers working on complex manufacturing processes.

In Taiwan, home to the world's largest contract chipmaker TSMC, protections for trade secrets have evolved alongside the semiconductor industry’s growing strategic importance.

Jamie J. Yang, a partner specializing in antitrust and intellectual property at Innovatus Law, said Taiwan limits post-employment non-compete agreements to two years and requires employers to compensate former employees at least half their average monthly salary during the restricted period.

Yang added that the complexity of semiconductor manufacturing makes it difficult for any single engineer to possess enough knowledge to replicate an entire production line elsewhere.

"There is just very little chance that one person ... will know enough to replicate that line in any of the other companies," Yang said.

Moves by engineers between rival semiconductor companies could increase modestly as competition for top talent intensifies, while trade secret litigation is also likely to rise as firms seek to protect their technological edge, she added.

In Singapore, Zechariah Chan, a partner at law firm Lee & Lee, said protecting trade secrets can be particularly difficult in the semiconductor industry, where proprietary knowledge often involves intangible manufacturing techniques and methods.

Proving that a competitor has used proprietary manufacturing techniques can also be difficult, as outsiders generally see only the final product, according to Chan.

"There is no bright line between an engineer's expertise and proprietary information," he said. General skills and knowledge stay with the engineer, while trade secrets remain with the company.

In Singapore, non-compete clauses are enforceable only if they protect legitimate business interests and are reasonable in scope, geographical area and duration.

In Hong Kong, courts generally take a restrictive approach to non-compete clauses, enforcing them only when they protect legitimate business interests and go no further than necessary, according to Jezamine Fewins, head of litigation at Lewis Silkin in Hong Kong.

Courts are more willing to protect trade secrets through court injunctions, Fewins said.

"The difficulty is that often the trade secret is carried in the employee's head," she added. Information is more likely to qualify as a trade secret if its disclosure to a competitor could cause significant harm to the former employer and the company has taken steps to restrict access.

For chipmakers, Fewins said the key is to clearly define what constitutes a trade secret, make employees aware of that information and put safeguards in place to limit access.

Samsung and CXMT did not respond to CNBC's request for comment.

#2809. Democrats flip dozens of GOP seats. Republicans haven't flipped a single one

Seven Senate seats. At least 24 House districts. Five governorships. Twelve state legislative chambers. By Wednesday afternoon, that's how far Democrats' historic midterm rout had reached.

Republicans, meanwhile, had yet to flip a single Democratic-held congressional seat, governorship or state legislative chamber, according to Associated Press data. Their remaining opportunities for congressional pickups are largely confined to two newly gerrymandered House districts that weren't even supposed to be competitive in the first place.

The lopsided results underscored the breadth of the GOP's shellacking, with Democrats breaking decades of Republican dominance in reliably red states and reshaping the balance of power from Washington to state capitols.

As of late Wednesday, nine House seats remained uncalled by the AP along with two Senate seats and two governors races. The one seat Republicans may have a chance of flipping that hasn't been redistricted ahead of this election is Maine's 2nd District, FiftyPlusOne head of research Mary Radcliffe said by email.

In Kansas, voters elected their first Democratic U.S. senator since 1932, ending a 94-year losing streak for the party. In Texas, Democrats toppled three-term Republican Gov. Greg Abbott and captured a Senate seat that had eluded the party since 1988. Republican-held Senate seats also fell in Iowa, South Carolina and Ohio, along with seats in Maine and North Carolina.

The same wave swept through governors' mansions. Democrats scored upsets in Georgia, Iowa, Ohio and Nevada — all states President Donald Trump won in 2024 — giving the party a majority of the nation's governorships for the first time since 2010.

Upheaval even reached state capitols, where Republican legislative majorities that had endured for decades fell like dominoes.

Democrats seized control of the Texas House for the first time in more than two decades, flipped both chambers of the Wisconsin Legislature and wrested the North Carolina House from Republicans after 16 years in the minority.

In Pennsylvania, Democrats captured the state Senate for the first time since before 1994, giving the party complete control of state government. They also reclaimed lower chambers in Michigan and Minnesota, restoring full Democratic control in both states. In Arizona, unofficial results pointed toward Democrats taking both legislative chambers for the first time in roughly 60 years.

Even where Republicans held on to legislative majorities, Democrats stripped away veto-proof supermajorities in Florida, Indiana, Kansas and Missouri. Not since the aftermath of Watergate in 1974 had the GOP faced a statehouse reckoning of this magnitude, according to the Democratic Legislative Campaign Committee.

The scale of the losses is all the more remarkable given how aggressively Republicans had tried to protect their power. At Trump's urging, GOP lawmakers spent months pursuing an extraordinary mid-decade redistricting campaign, redrawing congressional boundaries in Texas, Florida and North Carolina to tilt the electoral map in their favor.

Yet the gerrymandering push did little to blunt the Democratic wave. And the damage might not be over. Republicans were still fighting to hold Senate seats in Alaska and Mississippi, with several House contests also yet to be called Wednesday.

— Kevin Breuninger contributed to this report.

#2808. Sean McVay sounds alarm as Rams’ injury crisis reaches historic levels

The Rams' injury crisis has turned into a nightmare, and they've seen this movie before. Only this time, coach Sean McVay believes the sequel is much worse than the original.

Three season-ending injuries in two weeks. A revolving door to the training room. A roster that began the season with Super Bowl aspirations now looks like an ER's waiting room.

Los Angeles Rams cornerback Jaylen Watson leaves the field on a cart during the first half of an NFL football game against the Denver Broncos, Monday, Sept. 28, 2026, in Denver. AP Photo/David Zalubowski

And for McVay, the carnage has surpassed even the disastrous 2022 season, when the defending Super Bowl champions were decimated by injuries and finished 5-12, the worst record by a reigning Super Bowl champion in NFL history.

"I've been the coach for 10 years, and I know 2022 was a season that we had a lot of injuries, particularly at the offensive line, but this has been more than any year, including '22," McVay told reporters this week.

The latest casualty is cornerback Jaylen Watson, who will undergo season-ending shoulder surgery after emerging as one of the Rams' brightest offseason acquisitions.

"He's been such a stud for us," McVay said. "He wanted to be able to play through it."

Los Angeles Rams defensive tackle Poona Ford stands on the field before an NFL game against the Arizona Cardinals in Inglewood, Calif., Sunday, Jan. 4, 2026. Icon Sportswire via Getty Images

Watson joins defensive tackle Poona Ford (torn triceps) and kicker Blake Grupe (torn ACL) as the third Rams player lost for the season since they played the Cardinals on Oct. 18.

But even though those injuries have more of a long-lasting impact, it's just the tip of the iceberg for the Rams this season.

Myles Garrett underwent knee surgery. Davante Adams suffered a ruptured hamstring. Kobie Turner tore his groin. Running back Dean Connors broke his hand. Terrance Ferguson injured his ankle, Trent McDuffie battled a groin strain, Colby Parkinson has dealt with shoulder and rib problems, and Puka Nacua has fought lingering core and calf issues.

The injuries have struck every position group, leaving McVay scrambling to keep his roster competitive.

Los Angeles Rams head coach Sean McVay speaks at a news conference after an NFL football game against the Arizona Cardinals in Inglewood, Calif., Sunday, Oct. 18, 2026. AP Photo/Gregory Bull

"Guys are out at different spots that are key and critical to your identities, really in all three — on offense, on defense and on special teams," McVay said.

Yet unlike 2022, when the season collapsed beyond repair, these Rams are still fighting.

"Let's just take it a day at a time," McVay said.

Speaking of one day at a time, if there was a silver lining on Wednesday, it was the news that reigning Defensive Player of the Year Garrett returned to practice and could potentially play as soon as Sunday against the Commanders.

"It's great to be able to get him back out there. I think what we've said, just trying to stay in the moment, one day at a time," McVay said of the plan for Garrett. "As far as what that snap count looks like if he is able to play, it's really truly like, 'Let's just see how today goes, and then let's see how tomorrow goes and [then] the following day.'"

The injuries are so significant and consistent that even 49ers fans are joking on social media that the Rams might have a substation problem near their practice facility instead of the Niners.

For a team that's already exhausted its supply of healthy bodies, surviving November might be just as impressive as finishing with a winning record.

#2807. What Wall Street billionaires are saying about Democrats' midterm sweep

America's billionaires woke up Wednesday to a political reckoning — and a Wall Street rally.

As Democrats celebrated a sweeping midterm victory, some of the country's wealthiest donors traded barbs over record election spending. Others warned the results could threaten the artificial intelligence boom.

Wall Street, however, cheered the outcome. The Dow Jones Industrial Average soared 1,569 points, or 3%, while the S&P 500 gained 1.6% and the Nasdaq Composite rose 1.4%, as investors welcomed political gridlock, reduced uncertainty and President Donald Trump's willingness to accept the results.

Virgin Group founder Richard Branson took aim at the billions spent influencing the election.

"I have felt embarrassed by the billions that tech entrepreneurs and hedge fund managers have spent," Branson wrote Wednesday on LinkedIn, calling for campaign finance reform. Branson did not give donations that met the threshold reporting in the 2026 election cycle.

His comments came as Elon Musk, the election's largest individual donor, watched his preferred Republicans lose the Texas Senate and governor races despite pouring $224 million into the state — nearly half his record $474 million in political giving nationwide, according to a CNBC analysis of campaign finance records.

Early Wednesday, the Tesla and SpaceX CEO suggested on X that data centers in space were looking increasingly appealing before joking that his "election-interfering space lasers" had failed.

LinkedIn co-founder Reid Hoffman, another major political donor, took a sharper tone.

"We should get money out of politics," Hoffman wrote late Tuesday on X, accusing Musk of spending "outrageous amounts to keep authoritarian clowns in power" while defending his own contributions to Democrats.

Other Democratic megadonors celebrated. "The world changed yesterday," Alex Soros wrote Wednesday on X, hailing the results as a generational victory after his family contributed roughly $103 million to Democratic political efforts.

But beyond the political sparring, some of the country's richest investors were focused on a potentially bigger consequence of the Democratic sweep: the future of the AI boom.

Even before polls closed Tuesday, Pershing Square founder Bill Ackman warned on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that slowing AI development could jeopardize America's technological edge. "Practically speaking, we can't slow down," he said.

As results rolled in Tuesday night, billionaire investor Chamath Palihapitiya sounded an even starker warning.

Palihapitiya initially questioned whether the early results would hold, but warned that if they did, the implications for the AI trade could be severe.

"And the only way to sink the AI trade is to sink the data centers," Palihapitiya said during an election-night livestream of the "All-In" podcast.

Texas offered an early test of those fears. Democratic Gov.-elect Gina Hinojosa, who has proposed a moratorium on new data-center construction until stronger safeguards are established, defeated three-term Republican incumbent Greg Abbott.

During the same livestream, Altimeter Capital CEO Brad Gerstner warned the Democratic wave could mean at least 10 to 15 gigawatts of expected AI data-center capacity would not come online in 2027. Wall Street analyst Dan Ives estimated Wednesday that 10% of planned U.S. data centers would no longer go ahead.

AMD CEO Lisa Su acknowledged the growing resistance to AI's expansion, urging technology companies to demonstrate the benefits of data centers to local communities.

"At the end of the day, what we need to do is ensure that everybody wins from AI," Su told CNBC Wednesday.

Yet Wall Street may have found opportunity in the same slowdown that worried AI's biggest backers.

Fewer new data centers could increase the value of existing facilities while easing the enormous spending burden on technology giants. Big Tech stocks surged Wednesday, adding more than $750 billion in market value at one point, even as smaller, debt-dependent data-center operators tumbled.

#2806. USC commit Alifeleti Tuihalamaka honored as a 2027 Navy All-American

Defensive lineman Alifeleti Tuihalamaka (Westlake Village, California/ Oaks Christian School) was officially honored as a 2027 Navy All-American during a virtual jersey presentation as part of the Road to the Dome tour.

Tuihalamaka will play in the annual East vs. West matchup inside the Alamodome in San Antonio, Texas at 1 p.m. ET on Saturday, January 9, 2027, live on NBC and Peacock, which marks the twenty-seventh anniversary of the event.

The nationwide Road to the Dome tour will honor 100 Navy All-Americans with live and virtual jersey presentations. The series will be released weekly through social platforms across NBC Sports.

Tuihalamaka was selected by the Navy All-American Bowl Selection Committee, comprised of the All-American Bowl, Andrew Ivins (247Sports), Brandon Huffman (Rivals), and NXGN. Navy All-Americans are eligible for the Navy All-American Bowl Player of the Year Award, Anthony Muñoz Lineman of the Year Award, Navy All-American Bowl Defensive Player of the Year Award, Navy All-American Bowl Man of the Year, and Navy All-American Bowl Game MVP Award.

The All-American Bowl is annually the most-watched, most-talked about, and most-prestigious high school all-star event with more than four million unique television viewers and more than 25,000 fans in attendance. The history and tradition of the All-American Bowl is unparalleled, as it features: 665 draft picks; 110 Super Bowl champions; 300 Pro Bowl selections; and 18 Heisman finalists. #2805. Russia, China may be more aggressive if they feel Trump is on "his way out": Brookings

Russia and China could become more aggressive over the next two years if they conclude that President Donald Trump is politically weakened and on "his way out" after Democrats won control of both chambers of Congress, Brookings Institution senior fellow Robert Kagan told CNBC.

Speaking on "Squawk Box Asia," Kagan said the war with Iran had also exposed how Washington has "proved itself rather incapable militarily" in terms of capacity, which would be critical in Asia.

"Everybody can see how much our stocks are depleted in terms of interceptors and missiles, which are of vital interest in the Asian theater," he said.

Congressional Budget Office reports have estimated that the conflict depleted as much as two-thirds of U.S. missile-defense interceptors since June 2025, largely because of efforts to defend Israel against Iranian attacks. This could leave the U.S. with constrained inventories for several years.

Kagan said the deployment of three U.S. aircraft carriers to the Middle East had reduced the U.S. naval presence available in Asia, potentially giving China more room to act assertively in the region.

"I think Trump's only interest right now is in cutting economic deals with China. I don't think he has any real interest in confronting China, but that is going to lead China to be even more aggressive," he said.

In December, Trump approved an $11 billion arms package for Taiwan, but later held back on a second package worth about $14 billion, calling it a "bargaining chip" in talks with China.

China claims self-governing Taiwan as its territory. While Washington does not have a mutual defense treaty with the island, U.S. law requires it to provide Taiwan with the means to defend itself.

Former CIA Director William Burns said in 2023 that Chinese President Xi Jinping had instructed the military to be ready to invade Taiwan by 2027, while stressing that this did not mean Xi had decided to launch an invasion that year or at all.

Kagan's comments come as U.S., China and Russia discuss a possible trilateral summit between Trump, Xi and President Vladimir Putin at the APEC Leaders' meeting in Shenzhen later this month.

Chinese Foreign Ministry spokesperson Mao Ning said Wednesday that Beijing was "in communication with relevant parties regarding a meeting between the three heads of state," although no agenda has been announced.

Putin had reportedly floated the idea of a three-way summit with Trump and Xi in October to discuss ending the war in Ukraine, without Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy at the table.

"If it's clear that Donald Trump is on his way out, and the United States may be returning to something like normal, I don't want to overstate that, but they may feel both of them, both Russia and China, may feel that the time to move is in the next two years," Kagan said.

#2803. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2802. What was really behind Wednesday's monster rally? Trump quickly making peace

The stock market skyrocketed on Wednesday on the heels of a decisive sweep for Democrats in the 2026 midterm elections. But that was not necessarily the only driver of the climb.

"President Trump's acknowledgement of the Democrats' victory in the midterms and his expressed openness to collaborating with the opposition have helped markets to rally on hopes that contestation of the results and more adverse polarization can be avoided," said Thomas Hempell, Generali Investments senior economist.

Trump told The New York Times and The Wall Street Journal overnight Tuesday that he accepted the results and would work with Democrats. Those headlines coincided with a rebound in stock market futures.

In the lead-up to this year's midterms, some on Wall Street grew increasingly concerned about the prospect of Trump contesting the election results. The Department of Justice's Alaska election lawsuit led to fears over delays with the outcome that would exacerbate the uneasiness in the market that's typically removed once the elections conclude.

CNBC's Fed Survey showed that among 30 fund managers, strategists and economists, 63% believe that the extended uncertainty around the balance of power created by legal challenges to the election results posed "some" market risk. That was a slight increase from 61% in September. Additionally, 14% believed it represented "significant" market risk, which was up from 7%.

Now, investors are breathing a sigh of relief over Trump's acceptance of the results, which should "act as a break on extreme policy outcomes and bring down risk premia," so long as the president's decision remains, said George Saravelos, head of FX Research at Deutsche Bank.

Trump has a history of rallying markets, as well as driving them lower, with his announcements. Jay Woods of Freedom Capital Markets, who noted that the rebound in stocks overnight was partially due to the president's "initial tone and reaction to the results," said the focus will shift to whether Trump's latest move will actually have staying power.

"I think Trump is going to try to do and say the right thing, but at two in the morning, he may change his mind," the chief market strategist said. "He is an X factor."

Woods added that he's "encouraged" by Wednesday's massive rally, but he noted that there are still headwinds that could plague the market over the coming weeks — such as concerns about inflationary pressures as a result of higher energy prices from the conflict in the Middle East.

"How Trump reacts to it will help us navigate those headwinds," he said.

Osaic's Philip Blancato said he believes that if Trump "continues to play nice" with Democrats, the market is going to have a "very strong" end to the year.

Stocks historically tend to rally after the midterms anyway, but this time around, some were worried the solid run-up in the S&P 500 this year was going to potentially hinder those gains. After Trump's reaction, Blancato said he doesn't think the S&P 500 hitting 8,000 by year-end is totally out of the question and that there's "a lot predicated on him, but not completely."

The broad market index gained more than 1% on Wednesday, with its closing price implying a roughly 4% gain to that level.

However, investors should be prepared for any future volatility if Trump's rhetoric does not remain the same, the chief market strategist said.

"The president's always going to be a wildcard," he said. "Let's not let one day make a year. This is an instant reaction to this thing. There's still plenty of things to be hashed out."

#2801. Bills should be considering value of trading for player set to be a free agent

Of course I've been thinking plenty about what the Buffalo Bills might do before the NFL trade deadline.

And I've decided to transfer my stream of consciousness on this topic to my keyboard for this piece.

I'm a firm believer that in the NFL, in-season trade value is set, or at least calibrated against, the compensatory pick a player most likely would be able to fetch his original team in the future.

Of course, those compensatory picks don't arrive until the year after a player signs a new deal in following March, so the waiting game must be considered.

But for example, I'd essentially make no sense for a team to accept a fifth-round pick in a deadline deal for a veteran if said veteran could net the club a third-rounder a year down the road.

Let's use Josh Downs for example, the most recent feature in the Bills trade candidate series here at Rumblings.

Spotrac's current market value for Downs is $16.9 million per year. It projects him to sign a four-year, $67M contract in free agency next March, if he makes it there.

With Downs on an expiring contract, the team he's on the end of 2026 NFL season would likely receive a fourth-round compensatory pick (and a third-rounder isn't out of the question) in 2028 for his departure, if, of course, that club signs fewer unrestricted free agents than it loses in the 2027 free-agent cycle.

(Given the Bills are set to be $37.8M OVER the 2027 cap and currently have eight players set to be qualifying unrestricted free agents, it's a reasonably safe bet they will lose more UFAs than they sign. In 2026's free-agent cycle, Buffalo lost three qualifying UFAs and signed two.)

For the sake of the argument here, let's assume a deal for Downs in the $16M – $17M APY range would net that fourth-round compensatory selection in the 2028 NFL Draft.

That likely sets the floor regarding what the Colts would like to get in return for Downs in a trade before the deadline on November 10.

Assuming there are a few suitors for Downs, the Colts could be in search of a third-round pick for the veteran receiver.

The Bills are expected to have two fourths in the 2027 NFL Draft — their own pick and the projected compensatory pick they got for David Edwards signing a sizable deal with the Saints.

In continuing this hypothetical, the Bills would, of course, prefer to send a fourth-round pick for Downs and initially reluctant to trade away a 2027 third-round selection for him.

(again, I do not know anything in the previous sentence to be true… we are living in hypothetical land right now)

Here's why I think it'd make perfect sense for the Bills to ultimately decide to use that third-round selection for Downs (or any other trade candidate on an expiring deal):

If they did make that trade, these are the possible outcomes, within reason. And let's start by assuming Downs almost assuredly makes the Bills a better team for the second half of the 2026 regular season, and presumably, the playoffs:

After major productivity, he prices himself out of what the Bills want to pay him on a second contract, and he signs a lucrative, multi-year deal elsewhere in free agency. In this scenario, Buffalo nets a 2028 third-round (or fourth-round) compensatory pick for their "rental." The draft-pick cost to acquire him would, at worst, would be Buffalo moving down from one round to the next, and waiting a year. At best, the compensatory pick would negate the draft-pick cost to trade for him.

After major productivity, the Bills decide he's a core piece of the receiving corps who will be 26 years old before the start of the 2027 campaign and sign him to a multi-year extension. Said extension features a reasonable cap hit in the first year, a slightly higher one in the next, then finally a cap hit above his contract's APY in 2029, when he'll be 28 and D.J. Moore will be 32.

After solid-but-unspectacular productivity, the Bills let Downs walk in free agency, and net a fifth or sixth-round compensatory pick in the process. Of course, this is the least desirable outcome for the Bills and Downs. A two or three-round "drop" in the draft for the Downs rental (going from third-round pick to acquire him to, potentially, a sixth-round pick from the compensatory system).

After solid-but-unspectacular productivity, the Bills and Downs agree to a more team-friendly deal in the $10M – $14M APY range. For context, the contract Khalil Shakir signed before the 2025 campaign features $13.6M in APY. Buffalo gets a solid-but-unspectacular possession wideout entering his prime at somewhat of a bargain price.

Of course, there are other possible outcomes, including Downs playing horribly and/or getting hurt. It's impossible to predict whether or not the latter would occur, so it's not worth mentioning here.

As far as the former, given his steadiness in the NFL, fine start to 2026, and the offense he'd be entering, it's reasonably safe to project Downs making a solid impact at the very least in Buffalo.

Therefore, the worst possible outcome (within reason) for the Bills would be sending a third-round pick to the Colts and only getting a sixth-round pick in 2028 for his March 2027 departure from Buffalo.

But wouldn't amplifying the offense this season be worth, in the worst-case scenario, a possible three-round drop in the draft?

#2800. How Shannon Terry Built On3 Into a Powerhouse in College Sports Media

Covering college sports is a daunting task. From high school recruiting and NIL to the transfer portal, weekly rankings and injuries, the volume of information continues to grow. As audiences demand more coverage, traditional media outlets are finding it increasingly difficult to keep pace. Shannon Terry saw an opportunity to meet that demand, using his forward-thinking approach to build On3 into a major destination for college sports news and information.

Terry is no stranger to building digital content platforms focused on amateur athletics. He co-founded Rivals in 2000 before selling the popular website to Yahoo Sports seven years later for a reported $98 million. Three years after that sale, Terry launched 247Sports, which CBS Sports acquired in 2015. However, he wasn't finished. Driven by his entrepreneurial spirit and passion for building communities around college sports, Terry launched On3 in 2021.

"I was too young to retire and felt the work I did with Rivals and 247Sports were building blocks to do something bigger and better than before," Terry explained. "On3 was significantly larger in vision and concept. I felt there was more personally and professionally that I wanted to do, and there was an opportunity to go do it. So, we did."

The strategy behind On3 differed from the approach Terry used to build his previous ventures. By combining a community-focused platform, a national media operation dedicated to breaking news and a digital content business, Terry sought to connect every corner of college athletics with audiences nationwide.

"Five years ago, we saw that college sports was going to explode. There was a conflation on everything going on, but this undercurrent of passion and community that fit what we're about," Terry said. "We didn't think a lot of media companies were really good in covering college sports. There was a lot of aggregation and blogs, but there wasn't a company that sought to own college sports media and data. We thought we could do that."

Terry and his team bet on the timing, and that decision has paid off. Five years after its launch, On3 has established itself as a major destination for college sports news, data, editorial content and community. What began with a relatively small aggregation model has evolved into a platform increasingly focused on breaking news and serving a growing audience.

That growth rests on three core principles: building a community through authentic storytelling, establishing trust as a news source and connecting athletes with the broader college sports ecosystem. On3's progress has demonstrated the potential of that approach, giving other media companies a model to consider.

"There's nothing that's going on that our news team isn't working with multiple sources on. If we can identify our audience, we don't want to compromise on our pillars. We're not perfect because it's a beast to manage this. Community, authenticity and trust. That's what we work within, but that's our mission every day," Terry said.

Although Terry doesn't consider himself a fortune teller, his vision for On3 reflects his understanding of how the internet and digital media continue to evolve. While building Rivals and 247Sports, he witnessed how audiences on Facebook could drive advertising revenue. Those experiences helped shape his approach to creating a business capable of adapting as distribution platforms and consumer habits change.

"When we built On3, we knew we couldn't be dependent on any platforms. YouTube, Facebook, Twitter, Google. We're all dependent on them to some degree, but we could build a business solely dependent on that," Terry said. "We also knew we wanted to be more than a media company. We wanted to be a platform and community, because we had 25 years of building communities already. So, we built On3 to connect that ecosystem."

Partnerships with creators and other trusted voices in college sports have become an important part of that strategy. Terry believes people develop stronger connections with other people than with the platforms that distribute their content. That philosophy influences how On3 identifies potential partners and determines whether to pursue a partnership or an exclusive arrangement.

In recent years, several established personalities and outlets have joined the On3 ecosystem. The company has partnered with Josh Pate, David Pollack and Ross Dellenger while strengthening its reporting staff with the additions of Brett McMurphy, Chris Low and Zak Herbstreit, among others.

"There are two things we look for with partners. Sheer talent is first and foremost. Does this person got it? We're not signing Pat McAfee. We don't play in that sandbox. We have to work within a sandbox of finding people to be with us for four to five years," Terry noted. "Also, passion matters. Why are you doing what you do? I've experienced the essence of college sports. Do you have that? If you have it and talent, that's powerful."

The quality of On3's work over its first five years has also attracted the attention of major networks, according to Terry. He believes those organizations will increasingly recognize the value of the grassroots communities that help define college athletics.

"This is from talking to c-suite level people. You're going to see more of an investment from the major [networks], because the fabric of college sports is the passion, pageantry, and the community. It's difficult for big brands like ESPN, CBS, and FOX to see things from the grassroots level. It's arduous to manage and build infrastructure that manages to that," Terry explained. "You will see that going forward with the networks tapping into the communities and the pageantry of college sports."

However, On3's growth has not come without some controversy. After acquiring Rivals from Yahoo Sports in the summer of 2025, Terry described the return of his former platform as "jet fuel" for On3's efforts to build a stronger athlete community. In September 2026, the company introduced Rivals Recruits, a service that allows athletes to pay a fee to claim their profiles on the Rivals website while receiving a scouting report, an evaluation grade and consideration for rankings.

According to the website, the program aims to evaluate more athletes, discover additional talent and create more opportunities. Its stated mission centers on maintaining Rivals' integrity while helping athletes who earn recognition receive the exposure they deserve.

"We wanted to give athletes education, and an opportunity to tell and control their story," Terry said of Rivals Recruits. "It's very early, but we have more than 5,000 athletes that are currently active. We are just starting this, and there are so many robust services that we are offering."

The service also offers community message boards where parents and athletes can seek information from a staff of more than 40 Rivals experts. Looking ahead, Terry hopes to expand the business by adding services for schools, potentially making those offerings available at no cost.

"We want to connect that ecosystem on our platform because we already have their eyes. Every single school lives on On3 and Rivals. Athletes and parents alike. They are all here," Terry explained. "We shouldn't rely on social media or some sass company. We should have a seat at the table at connecting that community. It's as simple and as difficult as that."

As artificial intelligence continues to reshape digital media, Terry believes On3 and Rivals are positioned to adapt. Changes in search driven by AI agents, combined with declining traffic from digital platforms, have reinforced his belief that media companies must build direct relationships with their audiences rather than rely on outside distribution channels.

Terry said On3 anticipated those challenges when developing its business plan. He views the changes as an opportunity to strengthen the company's reporting, produce more relevant content and deepen its connection with readers.

"It was written as part of our business plan. It hurts all media companies. But the positive thing about it is it's forcing us to work harder, smarter, and together. To produce better content, break more news, and be more relevant. If you are a media business built on aggregation and top of funnel audience but don't have a direct endemic audience base, you're probably done," Terry explained.

That philosophy also informs On3's position on artificial intelligence. Terry said the company will not partner with brands, creators or platforms that use AI to produce content.

"On3 is built differently and has been from the inception. We see that as an enormous opportunity moving forward, because we're seeing ad yields higher by brands that want to buy authentic audiences which we provide," Terry said. "Can AI create interesting content that can be cannibalized and hijacked? Yes, in a lot of ways. We don't use it and will not use it. If we find a fan site using it, we're done with you. We want you to read Brett McMurphy, Chris Low, and everyone associated with On3. That's important to us."

Five years after launching On3, Terry sees significant room for the company to grow. Its foundation combines original reporting, editorial content and digital media with a community designed to connect every part of the amateur sports experience. For Terry, that combination positions On3 to remain a central destination for college sports fans and the people who shape the industry.

The next chapter involves more than expanding news coverage, adding talent or developing new services. Terry wants to continue building a platform where fans, athletes, parents and schools can connect through their shared passion for college sports. Having already built and sold two successful digital sports brands, he understands the importance of adapting as the industry changes.

With On3, he believes the opportunity is even greater.

"We have an amazing platform with a lot of reach. Moving into 2027, we want to spend time and money developing talent and shows outside of editorial. That's the next evolution for On3," Terry noted. "Also, with Rivals. Let's help parents, athletes and the whole ecosystem. We're going to hit the business with everything we have, and it's exciting."

As technology changes how audiences consume information and media companies search for new ways to connect with fans, Terry is betting that authenticity, trust and community will remain essential to long-term success. His track record suggests he understands how to build businesses that evolve alongside their audiences. If On3's first five years are any indication, the company may be only beginning to realize its potential.

Barrett Media produces daily content on the music, news, and sports media industries. Sign up for our newsletters to stay updated and get the latest information right in your inbox.

#2799. Lakers rookie in awe of franchise superstar’s scoring ability

SACRAMENTO — Heat superstar Giannis Antetokounmpo made his feelings on Luka Dončić clear when he referred to the Lakers superstar as possibly the greatest scorer in NBA history.

And consider the Lakers' youngest player just as impressed with how his teammate has opened the 2026-27 season.

Lakers rookie Cameron Carr is in awe of what he's observed from Luka Dončić. NBAE via Getty Images

Cameron Carr watched Dončić dominate the NBA during his combined three years in college at Tennessee and Baylor.

Now, as teammates, he's getting a firsthand look at what makes the Slovenian star special, with the 6-foot-5 wing calling the pace Dončić plays at "unique."

Luka Dončić plays at a "unique" pace in Cameron Carr's eyes. AP Photo/Jayne Kamin-Oncea

"I've said it since college, he moves in slow motion, but it just works," Carr said ahead of the Lakers' road game against the Kings on Wednesday. "I don't know how it works, but it works. He plays at his own pace; he doesn't get sped up. It's impossible to take the ball from him — I don't know how it works."

The last part of what the Lakers rookie said is an understatement.

Dončić entered Wednesday averaging a league-high 38.3 points per game to go with 8.1 assists, eight rebounds and 1.9 steals.

His 1.072 points per minute entering the matchup against the Kings would be an all-time league record, besting Wilt Chamberlain's record of 1.038 points per minute in 1961-62, if sustained for the season.

"Just to be able to be in his presence and see how his mind works every day and what he's thinking about … it sharpens his game but also sharpens people around him," Carr said. "I don't think he realizes how much impact he has on dudes like me. I'm here every day, just soaking things up. He's the best scorer in the NBA. That's big-time."

The Lakers' Cameron Carr averaged 18.9 points during his lone season with Baylor. AP Photo/Jayne Kamin-Oncea

Carr debated whether he can refer to himself as a scorer considering he just made his NBA debut during Sunday's home win over the Raptors and has played two minutes in the league.

But when he was in college, that's exactly what he was, with the former Baylor Bear averaging 18.9 points during his lone season with the program.

So he has a deeper appreciation for Dončić's scoring outburst early in games.

Dončić entered Wednesday averaging a league-high first-quarter 15 points and 22.9 first-half points.

"When you look up in the first quarter and he has 20, 22 points, you're like, 'Wow, how did he get there so fast?' That's the only question you can really ask," Carr said. "Like, he's about to make something ridiculous. Oh, he banked in a [3-pointer against the Raptors]. I ask him every game like, 'Did you mean to do that?' He like, 'Don't even ask — you know I did.'"

#2796. Who plays on 'Thursday Night Football' tonight? Time, TV channel, schedule, live stream for NFL Week 9 game

Who plays on 'Thursday Night Football' tonight? Time, TV channel, schedule, live stream for NFL Week 9 game originally appeared on The Sporting News. Add The Sporting News as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

Coming into 2026, the Jaguars and Ravens were anticipated to be a pair of top AFC contenders. But they're trending in different directions entering Week 9.

Jacksonville has been one of the best teams in football, riding high after a blowout over the Colts last week that improved the team to 5-2 this season. Trevor Lawrence is having a career year, and the Jaguars' offense has been dynamic.

Baltimore, however, is dealing with quarterback issues. Both Lamar Jackson and Tyler Huntley went down with injuries last week, and at 4-4, urgency is picking up for the Ravens to build momentum toward the playoffs. But they'll face a tall task in prime time this week.

Here's what to know about watching "Thursday Night Football" in Week 9.

The Ravens will host the Jaguars on "Thursday Night Football" at M&T Bank Stadium in Baltimore.

Kickoff for Jacksonville vs. Baltimore is scheduled for 8:15 p.m. ET on Thursday, Nov. 5.

"Thursday Night Football" will air exclusively on Prime Video with Al Michaels (play-by-play) and Kirk Herbstreit (color commentator) on the call.

Amazon Prime offers a free trial, allowing new users to tune into Thursday's game and everything the service has to offer before commitment.

Radio station:SiriusXM app and channels 193, 192

Listen to live play-by-play coverage of Jaguars vs. Ravens nationwide on the SiriusXM app, or in your vehicle on channels 192 (Ravens broadcast) and 193 (Jaguars broadcast).

The SiriusXM Sports Pass allows fans to listen to live game broadcasts of the NFL, MLB, NBA, NHL, college football and basketball, the PGA Tour, NASCAR and more. Get your first month of the SiriusXM Sports Pass for free, then pay just $5 a month or $49 for a year.
